“You need fresh air,” he said, as they stepped from the veranda; “but, also, I need you to talk to. I can formulate my ideas better if I express them aloud, and you are such a clearheaded and sympathetic listener that it helps a lot.”

Kitty smiled with pleasure at the compliment, then her pretty face became grave again as she remembered what must be the subject of their conversation.

“Before I talk to the lawyers or detectives who will doubtless soon infest the house, I want to straighten out my own ideas.”
===Chinese===
费森登也不等看旁人如何，径自转向基蒂·弗伦奇，邀她一同出去散散步。

“你需要透透新鲜空气，”两人步下游廊时，他说道，“不过，我也需要有个人说说话。把想法说出声来，思路才理得清楚；何况你头脑清楚，又善解人意，肯这样听我说，帮处实在不小。”

这句恭维话让基蒂笑逐颜开；随即想起两人要谈的话题，那张俏脸又重新板了起来。

“过不了多久，这宅子准会被律师和侦探们踏破门槛。趁还没同他们照面，我要先把自己的想法理清楚。”

===Original===
“I don’t see how you can have any,” said Kitty; “I mean, of course, any definite ideas about who committed the murder.”

“I haven’t really definite ones, but I want you to help me get some.”

“Well,” said Kitty, looking provokingly lovely in her serious endeavor to be helpful, “let’s sit down here and talk it over.”

“Here” was a sort of a rustic arbor, which was a delightful place for a tête-à-tête, but not at all conducive to deep thought or profound conversation.
===Chinese===
“我看你能有什么想法，”基蒂说，“当然喽，我是说，关于凶手是谁，你还能有什么拿得准的想法。”

“拿得准的倒真没有，所以才要你帮我找出来呀。”

“好吧，”基蒂说。她一本正经地想帮上忙，那模样偏偏娇俏动人。“咱们就坐在这儿，从头细谈吧。”

所谓“这里”，是一座乡野趣味的凉亭。两个要说体己话的人坐在里面，自然其乐陶陶；可要凝神深思、纵谈正事，那就全不相宜了。

===Original===
“Go on,” said Kitty, pursing her red lips and puckering her white brow in her determination to supply the help that was required of her.

“But I can’t go on, if you look like that! All logic and deduction fly out of my head, and I can think only of poetry and romance. And it won’t do! At least, not now. Can’t you try to give a more successful imitation of a coroner’s jury?”

Kitty tried to look stupid and wise, both at once, and only succeeded in looking bewitching.

“It’s no use,” said Fessenden; “I can’t sit facing you, as I would the real thing in the way of juries. So I’ll sit beside you, and look at the side of that distant barn, while we talk.”
===Chinese===
“说吧。”基蒂说着，噘起红唇，蹙起白净的额头，横下心来要拿出人家要她出的那份力。

“可你摆出这副模样，我就说不下去了！什么逻辑、什么推理，统统从我脑袋里飞了出去，满脑子只剩下诗歌和爱情。这可不行！至少眼下不行。你就不能装得更像个验尸陪审团一点吗？”

基蒂努力想同时摆出又木然又高明两种神情，结果只是显得越发撩人。

“没有用的，”费森登说，“对着货真价实的陪审团，我可以正襟危坐地面对；对着你可不成。我还是坐在你身旁，眼睛望着远处那座谷仓的侧墙，咱们再谈。”

===Original===
So he turned partly round, and, fixing his gaze on the stolid red barn, said abruptly:

“Who wrote that paper?”

“I don’t know,” said Kitty, feeling that she couldn’t help much here.

“Somehow, I can’t seem to believe that Dupuy girl wrote it. She sounded to me like a lady reciting a fabrication.”
===Chinese===
于是他侧过半个身子，目光定在那座呆板的红色谷仓上，突兀地问道：

“那张字条是谁写的？”

“我不知道。”基蒂说，觉得在这一节上自己帮不上什么忙。

“不知怎么，我总觉得难以相信是杜皮伊那姑娘写的。听她的口气，倒像一位太太在背诵编好的谎话。”

===Original===
“I thought that, too,” said Kitty. “I never liked Cicely, because I never trusted her. But Maddy was very fond of her, and she wouldn’t have been, unless she had found Cicely trustworthy.”

“Come to luncheon, you two,” said Tom Willard, as he approached the arbor.

“Oh, Mr. Willard,” said Kitty, “who do *you* think wrote that paper?”

“Why, Miss Dupuy,” said Tom, in surprise. “She owned up to it.”
===Chinese===
“我也这么想过。”基蒂说，“我从来就不喜欢西塞莉，因为我从来信不过她。可是玛迪非常喜欢她——若不是觉得西塞莉靠得住，玛迪是不会喜欢她的。”

“你们两位，回去吃午饭吧。”汤姆·威拉德走近凉亭，招呼道。

“哦，威拉德先生，”基蒂说，“您自己觉得，那张字条是谁写的？”

“怎么，是杜皮伊小姐呀。”汤姆诧异地说，“她自己都承认了。”

===Original===
“Yes, I know; but I’m not sure she told the truth.”

“I don’t know why she shouldn’t,” said Tom, thoughtfully. And then he added gently, “And, after looking at it closely, I felt sure, myself, it wasn’t Maddy’s writing, after all.”

“Then it must be Cicely’s,” said Kitty. “I admit I can’t tell them apart.”

And then the three went back to the house.
===Chinese===
“是，我知道；可她是不是说了实话，我不敢保险。”

“她为什么要撒谎呢，我想不出。”汤姆若有所思地说。随即又轻声补了一句：“况且，仔细端详过那字条之后，我自己也认定，那毕竟不是玛迪的笔迹。”

“那准是西塞莉的笔迹了。”基蒂说，“我承认，她们俩的字我实在分辨不出。”

于是三人一道回宅子里去了。

## IX The Will / 九　遗嘱

===Original===
Immediately after luncheon Lawyer Peabody came. This gentleman had had charge of the Van Norman legal matters for many years, and it was known by most of those present that he was bringing with him such wills or other documents as might have a bearing on the present crisis.
Mr. Peabody was an old man; moreover, he had for many years been intimately associated with the Van Norman household, and had been a close friend of both Richard Van Norman and Madeleine. Shattered and broken by the sad tragedy in the household, he could scarcely repress his emotion when he undertook to address the little audience.
But the main purport of his business there at that time was to announce the contents of the two wills in his possession.
The first one, the will of Richard Van Norman, was no surprise to anyone present, except perhaps those few who did not live in Mapleton. One of these, Robert Fessenden, was extremely interested to learn that because of Madeleine’s death before her marriage, and also before she was twenty-three years of age, the large fortune of Richard Van Norman, which would have been hers on her wedding day, passed at once and unrestrictedly to Tom Willard.

===Chinese===
午餐一过，皮博迪律师（Lawyer Peabody）便到了。这位先生多年来一手执掌范·诺曼家的法律事务，在场多数人都知道，他随身带来的正是那些可能与眼下这场变故有所关涉的遗嘱及其他文书。
皮博迪先生已是风烛残年；何况他多年来与范·诺曼家亲厚异常，同理查德·范·诺曼和马德琳都是至交。家门遭此惨祸，他心碎神伤，对着这一小圈人开口之际，几乎按捺不住胸中情绪。
然而他此来的正题，是宣布他手中那两份遗嘱的内容。
头一份，理查德·范·诺曼的遗嘱，在座除却几位不住梅普尔顿的，谁也不觉意外。罗伯特·费森登恰在那几位之列。他极感兴趣地得知：由于马德琳未及成婚、又未满二十三岁便已身故，理查德·范·诺曼那笔原该于婚礼之日归她所有的巨额财产，当下便毫无限制地尽归汤姆·威拉德。

===Original===
But also by the terms of Richard Van Norman’s will the fine old mansion and grounds and a sum of money, modest in comparison with the whole fortune, but ample to maintain the estate, were Madeleine’s own, and had been from the day of her uncle’s death.
Possessed of this property, therefore, Madeleine had made a will which was dated a few months before her death, and which Mr. Peabody now read.
After appropriate and substantial bequests to several intimate friends, to her housekeeper and secretary, and to all the servants, Madeleine devised that her residuary fortune and the Van Norman house and grounds should become the property of Miss Elizabeth Morton.
This was a complete surprise to all, with the possible exception of Miss Morton herself. It was not easy to judge from her haughty and self-satisfied countenance whether she had known of this before or not.

===Chinese===
然而按照理查德·范·诺曼遗嘱的条款，那座精美古宅连同庭园，另加一笔款项——较之全部家产虽属有限，撑持这份产业却绰绰有余——皆属马德琳自有，且自她叔父谢世之日起便已归她名下。
马德琳名下既有产业，遂立下一纸遗嘱，日期在其殁前数月；此刻便由皮博迪先生当众宣读。
遗嘱先向数位挚友、向女管家与秘书、向全体仆人各遗赠一笔得体丰厚的财物，继而载明：马德琳的剩余财产，连同范·诺曼宅邸与庭园，一并成为伊丽莎白·莫顿（Elizabeth Morton）小姐的产业。
此事满座无不愕然，莫顿小姐本人或许例外——却也难说。她那副倨傲自得的面色，教人实在看不出事先知情与否。

===Original===
Fessenden, who was watching her closely, was inclined to think she had known of it, and again his busy imagination ran riot. The first point, he thought to himself, in discovering a potential murderer, is to inquire who will be benefited by the victim’s death. Apparently the only ones to profit by the passing of Madeleine Van Norman were Tom Willard and Miss Morton. But even the ingenious imagination of the young detective balked at the idea of connecting either of these two with the tragedy. He knew Willard had not been in the house at the time of the murder, and Miss Morton, as he had chanced to discover, had occupied a room on the third floor. Moreover, it was absurd on the face of things to fancy a well-bred, middle-aged lady stealing downstairs at dead of night to kill her charming young hostess!
It was with a sense of satisfaction therefore that Fessenden assured himself that he had formed no suspicions whatever, and could listen with a mind entirely unprejudiced to such evidence as the coroner’s inquiry might bring forth.
He was even glad that he had not discussed the matter further with Kitty French. He still thought she had clear vision and good judgment, but he had begun to realize that in her presence his own clearness of vision was dazzled by her dancing eyes and a certain distracting charm which he had never before observed in any woman.
But he told himself somewhat sternly that feminine charm must not be allowed to interfere with the present business in hand, and he seated himself at a considerable distance from Kitty French, when it was time for the inquest.

===Chinese===
费森登一直在细细察看她，倒倾向于认为她早已知情；他那不肯安分的想象力于是又恣意驰骋起来。要寻出潜在的凶手，他暗自思量，第一步便是查明：谁能从死者的死亡中得利？照眼前情形看，能从马德琳·范·诺曼之死得到好处的，分明只有汤姆·威拉德与莫顿小姐二人。然而要把这两人中任何一个同这场惨剧牵到一处，就连这位年轻侦探的巧思也望而却步。他知道案发之时威拉德根本不在宅内；至于莫顿小姐，他碰巧得知，她住的是三楼的一个房间。更何况，一位教养良好的中年淑女，深更半夜溜下楼来，杀死她那位迷人的年轻女主人——这事摆明了就荒唐！
于是费森登颇觉安然地认定：自己不曾有过半点疑心；验尸官讯问中无论出现何种证词，他都能以全无成见的心境听取。
他甚至庆幸没有同基蒂·弗伦奇把这事深谈下去。他仍认为她眼明心亮、识见不俗，却已渐渐察觉：只要她在跟前，她那双顾盼流光的眼睛，还有她身上某种令人心神迷乱的魅力——他以往从未在任何女子身上见过的魅力——便会晃得他自己也目光不清。
但他板起面孔告诫自己：眼下正事当头，女性的魅力断不容来搅扰。于是待到验尸审讯开始，他特意拣了离基蒂·弗伦奇远远的座位坐下。

===Original===
A slight delay was occasioned by waiting for Coroner Benson’s own stenographer, but when he arrived the inquiry was at once begun.
At the request of Miss Morton, or, it might rather be said, at her command, the whole assembly had moved to the drawing-room, it being a much larger and more airy apartment, and withal less haunted by the picture of the tragedy itself.
And yet to hold a coroner’s inquiry in a room gay with wedding decorations was almost, if not quite, as ghastly.
But Coroner Benson paid no heed to emotional considerations and conducted himself with the same air of justice and legality as if he had been in a courtroom or the town-hall.
As for the jury he had gathered, the half-dozen men, though filled with righteous indignation at the crime committed in their village, wasted no thought on the incongruity of their surroundings.

===Chinese===
为等本森验尸官自己的速记员，审讯略耽搁了片刻；速记员一到，讯问立即开始。
应莫顿小姐之请——或者不如说，是奉她之命——众人已尽数移坐大客厅。那间屋子宽敞得多，敞亮得多，而且不那么萦绕着惨剧本身的影子。
然而在一间缀满婚礼装饰、喜气未褪的屋子里进行验尸讯问，即便不说同样阴森刺目，也相差无几了。
但本森验尸官全不理会这些情绪上的讲究，举止之间一秉公正合法的气度，仿佛置身法庭或镇公所一般。
至于他召集的陪审团——那六名汉子虽对本村发生的罪案义愤填膺，对周遭环境的格格不入却全未措意。

===Original===
Coroner Benson put his first question to Mrs. Markham, as he considered her, in a way at least, the present head of the household. To be sure, the house now legally belonged to Miss Morton, and that lady was quickly assuming an added air of importance which was doubtless the result of her recent inheritance; but Mrs. Markham was still housekeeper, and by virtue of her long association with the place, Mr. Benson chose to treat her with exceeding courtesy and deference.
But Mrs. Markham, though now quite composed and willing to answer questions, could give no evidence of any importance. She testified that she had seen Madeleine last at about ten o’clock the night before. This was after the guests who had been at dinner had gone away, and the house guests had gone to their rooms. Miss Van Norman was alone in the library, and as Mrs. Markham left her she asked her to send Cicely Dupuy to the library. Mrs. Markham had then gone directly to her own room, which was on the second floor, above the drawing-room. It was at the front of the house, and the room behind it, also over the long drawing-room, was the one now devoted to the exhibition of Madeleine’s wedding gifts. Mrs. Markham had retired almost immediately and had heard no unusual sounds. She explained, however, that she was somewhat deaf, and had there been any disturbance downstairs it was by no means probable that she would have heard it.
“What was the first intimation you had that anything had happened?” asked Mr. Benson.

===Chinese===
本森验尸官将第一个问题提给马卡姆太太，因他视她为——至少在某意义上——眼下这家的当家主事之人。诚然，宅子如今在法律上已属莫顿小姐，那位女士也正迅速添出一副愈发端重的气派，无疑是新得遗产使然；但马卡姆太太仍是这里的女管家，念其与此宅渊源日久，本森先生待她礼数周至、敬重有加。
不过马卡姆太太此刻虽已十分镇定、有问必答，却拿不出什么要紧证词。她证称，最后见到马德琳是在前一晚十点钟上下。其时来赴晚宴的宾客已然散去，宿在宅中的客人也各自归房。范·诺曼小姐独自在书房里，马卡姆太太离开时，她嘱她去叫西塞莉·杜皮伊到书房来。马卡姆太太随即径回自己房中。那房间在二楼、大客厅正上方，位于宅子前部；其后另有一间屋子，同样悬在那间长长的大客厅上头，如今用作马德琳婚礼贺礼的陈列室。马卡姆太太几乎倒头即睡，未闻任何异响。不过她亦说明，自己有些耳背，楼下纵有动静，她多半也是听不见的。
“您最初是怎么察觉出了事？”本森先生问。

===Original===
“Kitty French came to my door and called to me. Her excited voice made me think something was wrong, and, dressing hastily, I came downstairs, to find many of the household already assembled.”
“And then you went into the library?”
“Yes; I had no idea Madeleine was dead. I thought she had fainted, and I went toward her at once.”
“Did you touch her?”
“Yes; and I saw at once she was not living, but Miss Morton said perhaps she might be, and then she telephoned for Doctor Hills.”

===Chinese===
“基蒂·弗伦奇跑到我门口来叫我。她那激动的嗓音让我觉得出了乱子，我匆匆穿衣下楼，发现家中众人已聚在那里不少。”
“随后您就进了书房？”
“是的；我压根儿没想到马德琳已经死了。我只当她是晕了过去，立刻就朝她走去。”
“您碰她了吗？”
“碰了；我一眼便看出她已没了生气，可莫顿小姐说或许尚有一线生机，便打电话去请希尔斯医生。”

===Original===
“Can you tell me if the house is carefully locked at night?”
“It is, I am sure; but it is not in my province to attend to it.”
“Whose duty is it?”
“That of Harris, the butler.”
“Will you please call Harris at once?” Mr. Benson’s tone of finality seemed to dismiss Mrs. Markham as a witness, and she rang the bell for the butler.

===Chinese===
“您能告诉我，这宅子夜里都仔细上锁了吗？”
“锁好了的，我确信；不过这些事不归我经管。”
“那是谁的职责？”
“是管家哈里斯的职责。”
“可否请您即刻唤哈里斯前来？”本森先生这话带着结案定音的意味，仿佛就此打发了证人马卡姆太太；她便按铃唤管家。

===Original===
Harris came in, a perfect specimen of that type of butler that is so similar to a certain type of bishop.
Aside from the gravity of the occasion, he seemed to show a separate gravity of position, of importance, and of all-embracing knowledge.
“Your name is Harris?” said Mr. Benson.
“Yes, sir; James Harris, sir.”
“You have been employed in this house for some years?”
“Seventeen years and more, sir.”

===Chinese===
哈里斯进来了。某一类管家活脱脱像极了某一类主教，而他正是这类管家的完美标本。
场合本身已然庄重，他却似乎另具一重庄重——地位的庄重、身份的庄重，以及无所不晓的庄重。
“你名叫哈里斯？”本森先生说。
“是的，先生；詹姆斯·哈里斯，先生。”
“你在这宅里当差有些年头了？”
“十七年出头了，先生。”

===Original===
“Is it your duty to lock up the house at night?”
“It is, sir. Mr. Van Norman was most particular about it, sir, being as how the house is alone like in the grounds, and there being so much trees and shrubberies about.”
“There are strong bolts to doors and windows?”
“Most especial strong, sir. It was Mr. Van Norman’s wish to make it impossible for burglars to get in.”
“And did he succeed in this?”
“He did, sir, for sure. There are patent locks on every door and window, more than one on most of them; and whenever Mr. Van Norman heard of a new kind of lock, he’d order it at once.”

===Chinese===
“夜间锁闭门户是你的差事？”
“是，先生。范·诺曼先生对这事顶顶讲究，先生，因为这宅子孤零零地立在园子里，四下里树啊、灌木丛啊又多得很。”
“门窗上都有坚固的门闩？”
“结实得不能再结实了，先生。范·诺曼先生的心愿，就是要教贼人一丝一毫也进不来。”
“那他如愿了吗？”
“如愿了，先生，这错不了。每扇门、每扇窗上都装着专利锁，大多数还不止一把；范·诺曼先生但凡听说出了什么新式锁，马上就定购来装上。”

===Original===
“Is the house fitted with burglar alarms?”
“No, sir; Mr. Van Norman depended on his safety locks and strong bolts. He said he didn’t want no alarm, because it was forever getting out o’ kilter, and bolts were surer, after all.”
“And every night you make sure that these bolts and fastenings are all secured in place?”
“I do, sir, and I have done it for many years.”
“You looked after them last night, as usual?”

===Chinese===
“宅子里装没装防盗警报器？”
“没有装，先生；范·诺曼先生靠的是他的保险锁和坚固门闩。他说他不要警报器，那玩意儿动不动就出毛病，到底还是闩子牢靠。”
“每夜你都查验一遍，确信这些门闩插销全都闩牢到位？”
“是，先生，多年来我夜夜如此。”
“昨夜你也照例照料过了？”

===Original===
“Sure, sir; every one of them I attended to myself.”
“You can testify, then, that the house could not have been entered by a burglar last night?” asked Mr. Benson.
“Not by a burglar, nor by nobody else, sir, unless they broke down a door or cut out a pane of glass.”
“Yet Mr. Carleton came in.”
Harris looked annoyed. “Of course, sir, anybody could come in the front door with a latchkey. I didn’t mean that they couldn’t. But all the other doors and windows were fastened all right, and I found them all right this morning.”

===Chinese===
“照例，先生；每一处都是我亲手照看的。”
“那么你能作证，昨夜窃贼进不了这宅子？”本森先生问。
“窃贼进不来，旁人也一样进不来，先生，除非砸破门，或是割掉一块玻璃。”
“可卡尔顿先生就进来了。”
哈里斯面露不快。“当然啦，先生，谁拿了弹簧锁钥匙都能从前门进来。我不是说他们进不来。可其余的门窗全闩得好好的，今儿早上我查看时也都好好的。”

===Original===
“You made a careful examination of them?”
“Yes, sir. Of course we was all up through the night, and as soon as I learned that Miss Madeleine was﻿—was gone, sir, I felt I ought to look about a bit. And everything was as right as could be, sir. No burglar was into this house last night, sir.”
“How about the cellar?”
“We never bother much about the cellar, sir, as there’s nothing down there to steal, unless they take the furnace or the gas-meter. But the door at the top of the cellar stairs, as opens into the hall, sir, is locked every night with a double lock and a bolt besides.”
“Then no burglar could come up through the cellar way?”
“That he couldn’t, sir. Nor yet down through the skylight, for the skylight is bolted every night same as the windows.”

===Chinese===
“你仔细查验过了？”
“是的，先生。这一夜我们自然全都醒着，我一听说马德琳小姐已经——已经去了，先生，就觉得该四处瞧一瞧。结果样样都好好的，先生。昨夜没有贼进过这宅子，先生。”
“地窖呢？”
“地窖我们向来不大操心，先生，那里头没什么可偷的，除非把锅炉或者煤气表搬走。不过地窖楼梯顶上那扇通门厅的门，先生，每夜都上着双道锁，另外还插一道闩。”
“那么贼就没法从地窖那条道上来了？”
“上不来，先生。从天窗上也下不来，天窗每夜都同窗户一样插着闩。”

===Original===
“And the windows on the second floor﻿—are they fastened at night?”
“They are in the halls, sir. But of course in the bedrooms I don’t know how they may be. That is, the occupied bedrooms. When the guest rooms are vacant I always fasten those windows.”
“Then you can testify, Harris, that there was no way for anyone to enter this house last night except at the front door with a latchkey or through the window of some occupied bedroom?”
“I can swear to that, sir.”

===Chinese===
“还有二楼的窗户——夜里也都闩上吗？”
“走廊里的窗都闩，先生。至于卧室里的，情形如何我就说不准了。我是说有人住的卧室。客房空着的时候，那些窗户我总是闩好的。”
“那么你能作证，哈里斯：昨夜除了持弹簧锁钥匙走前门，或是从某间有人居住的卧室窗户进来，谁也没有别的路子进这宅子？”
“这一点我可以起誓，先生。”

===Original===
“You are sure you’ve overlooked no way? No back window, or seldom-used door?”
Harris was a little hurt at this insistent questioning, but the coroner recognized that this was a most important bit of evidence, and so pressed his questions.
“I’m sure of it, sir. Mr. Van Norman taught me to be most thorough about this matter, and I’ve never done different since Miss Madeleine has been mistress here.”
“That is all, thank you, Harris. You may go.”
Harris went away, his honest countenance showing a look of relief that his ordeal was over, and yet betokening a perplexed anxiety also.

===Chinese===
“你敢保没有遗漏任何路径？没有后窗，或哪扇不常开启的门？”
这样穷追不舍，哈里斯微觉委屈；但验尸官明白，这是一环至关紧要的证词，于是仍旧步步紧逼。
“我敢保，先生。范·诺曼先生教我此事务必周密；自马德琳小姐当家以来，我从未有半点走样。”
“就问到这儿，谢谢你，哈里斯。你可以去了。”
哈里斯退了出去，一张忠厚的脸上，既露出熬过难关的宽慰，又带着几分惶惑的忧色。

===Original===
Cicely Dupuy was next called upon to give her evidence, or rather to continue the testimony which she had begun in the library. The girl had a pleasanter expression than she had shown at the previous questioning, but a red spot burned in either cheek, and she was clearly trying to be calm, though really under stress of a great excitement.
“You were with Miss Van Norman in the library last evening?” began Mr. Benson, speaking more gently than he had been doing, for he feared an emotional outburst might again render this witness unavailable.
“Yes,” said Miss Dupuy, in a low tone; “when Mrs. Markham came upstairs she stopped at my door and said Miss Van Norman wanted me, and I went down immediately.”
“You have been Miss Van Norman’s secretary for some time?”
“For nearly five years.”

===Chinese===
接着轮到西塞莉·杜皮伊作证——或者不如说，是让她把先前在书房里开了头的证词续下去。姑娘的神情较上次受讯时和悦了些，可两颊各烧着一团红晕，分明在竭力持重，实则心潮激荡已极。
“昨夜你与范·诺曼小姐同在书房？”本森先生开口问道，语气较先前温和，因他深恐这位证人情绪一溃，又要无从问起。
“是的，”杜皮伊小姐低声说，“马卡姆太太上楼时在我门口停了停，说范·诺曼小姐找我，我立刻就下去了。”
“你给范·诺曼小姐当秘书有些时候了？”
“快五年了。”

===Original===
“What were your duties?”
“I attended to her social correspondence; helped her with her accounts, both household and personal; read to her, and often did errands and made calls for her.”
“She was kind to you?”
“She was more than kind. She treated me always as her social equal, and as her friend.”
Cicely’s blue eyes filled with tears, and her voice quivered as she spoke this tribute to her employer.

===Chinese===
“你职分内都做些什么？”
“我经管她的社交通信；帮她记账，家用私账都管；给她读书念报，还常替她跑腿办事、代为拜客。”
“她待你和善吗？”
“岂止和善。她一向拿我当社交场上的平辈，当她的朋友相待。”
说到这段对亡主的称许，西塞莉的蓝眼睛里噙满泪水，声音也颤抖起来。

===Original===
Again Mr. Benson feared she would break down, and changed his course of questioning.
“At what time did you go to the library last evening?”
“It could not have been more than a few minutes past ten.”
“What did you do there?”
“Miss Van Norman dictated some lists of matters to be attended to, and she discussed with me a few final arrangements for her wedding.”
“Did she seem about as usual in her manner?”

===Chinese===
本森先生又怕她撑不住，便换了问话的路数。
“昨晚你几点钟去的书房？”
“至多十点过几分。”
“你们在那儿做了些什么？”
“范·诺曼小姐口述了几张待办事项的单子，又同我商量了婚礼的几项最后安排。”
“她举止神态同往常差不多吗？”

===Original===
“Yes﻿—except that she was very tired, and seemed a little preoccupied.”
“And then she dismissed you?”
“Yes. She told me to go to bed, and said that she should sit up for an hour or so, and would write some notes herself.”
“Apparently she did not do so, as no notes have been found in the library.”
“That must be so, sir.”
But as she said this, a change came over Miss Dupuy’s face. She seemed to think that the absence of those notes was of startling importance, and though she tried not to show her agitation, it was clearly evident from the way she bit her lower lip, and clenched her fingers.

===Chinese===
“差不多——只是她疲惫得很，像是有几分心事重重。”
“随后她就让你走了？”
“是的。她叫我上床安歇，说她还要再坐个把钟头，亲自写几张便条。”
“看来她并未写，书房里连一张便条也没寻见。”
“想必如此，先生。”
可她话音才落，杜皮伊小姐的脸色便变了。她似乎觉得那几张便条的去向，关系重大得骇人；她虽极力不教慌乱外露，可看她咬着下唇、绞紧十指的模样，实在瞒不过人。

===Original===
“At what time did Miss Van Norman dismiss you?” asked Mr. Benson, seeming to ignore her embarrassment.
“At half-past ten.”
“Did you retire at once?”
“No; I had some notes to write for Miss Van Norman, and also some of my own, and I sat at my desk for some time. I don’t know just how long.”
“And then what happened?”
At this question Cicely Dupuy became more nervous and embarrassed than ever. She hesitated and then made two or three attempts to speak, each one of which resulted in no intelligible sound.

===Chinese===
“范·诺曼小姐是几点钟让你走的？”本森先生问，似乎全不在意她的窘态。
“十点半。”
“你随即就安歇了？”
“没有；我还有几件替范·诺曼小姐写的便条，自己也有几样要写，便在书桌前坐了好一阵。究竟多久，说不上来。”
“后来出了什么事？”
这一问之下，西塞莉·杜皮伊愈发紧张窘迫。她迟疑片刻，两三次张口想要作答，每一次都没能发出成句的声音。

## X Some Testimony / 十　几份证词

===Original===
“There is nothing to fear,” said Mr. Benson kindly. “Simply tell us what you heard while sitting there writing, that caused you to leave your room.”

Glancing around as if in search of someone, Cicely finally managed to make an audible reply. “I heard a loud cry,” she said, “that sounded as if somebody were frightened or in danger. I naturally ran out into the hall, and, looking over the baluster, I saw Mr. Carleton in the hall below. I felt sure then that it was he who had cried out, so I came downstairs.”

“At what time was this?”

“At half-past eleven exactly.”

“How do you know so accurately?”

“Because as I came downstairs the old clock on the middle landing chimed the half-hour. It has a deep soft note, and it struck just as I passed the clock, and it startled me a little, so of course I remember it perfectly.”

===Chinese===
“没有什么可怕的，”本森先生和颜悦色地说，“您只管讲讲，坐在那里写字的时候，您听到了什么，竟使您走出了自己的房间。”

西塞莉环顾四周，仿佛在搜寻什么人，好半晌才发出一句听得见的答话。“我听见一声高喊，”她说，“那声音听上去像是有人受了惊吓，或是遭了危险。我自然而然地跑出房门，进了走廊，从楼梯栏杆上望下去，看见卡尔顿先生在楼下大厅里。我当下便认定，方才喊叫的就是他，于是我下了楼。”

“那是几点钟的事？”

“十一点半，分毫不差。”

“您怎么知道得这样确切？”

“因为我下楼的时候，楼梯中段平台上那座老钟恰好敲响半点钟。它的钟声低沉柔和，就在我从钟旁经过时敲响，还让我小吃了一惊，所以我自然记得清清楚楚。”

===Original===
“And then?”

“And then”﻿—Cicely again hesitated, but with a visible effort resumed her speech﻿—“why, and then I came on down, and found Mr. Carleton nearly distracted. I could not guess what was the matter. He was turning on the lights and ringing the servants’ bells and acting like a man beside himself. Then in a moment Marie appeared, and gave one of her French shrieks that completely upset what little nerve I had left.”

“And what did you do next?”

“I﻿—I went into the library.”

“Why?”

Cicely looked up suddenly, as if startled, but after only an instant’s hesitation replied:

===Chinese===
“然后呢？”

“然后——”西塞莉又踌躇起来，但经过一番看得见的努力，重新接上了话头，“呃，然后我就继续下楼，发现卡尔顿先生几乎急疯了。我猜不出出了什么事。他正开着灯、按铃唤仆人，一副失魂落魄的样子。紧跟着玛丽出现了，发出一声她那种法国式的尖叫，把我残存的那点镇定彻底搅散了。”

“那接下来您做了什么？”

“我——我进了书房。”

“为什么进去？”

西塞莉猛地抬起头来，像是吃了一惊，但只迟疑了一瞬便答道：

===Original===
“Because Mr. Carleton pointed toward the doorway, and Marie and I went in together.”

“You knew at once that Miss Van Norman was not alive?”

“I was not sure, but Marie went toward her, and then turned away with another of her horrid screams, and I felt that Miss Van Norman must be dead.”

“What did Mr. Carleton say?”

“He said nothing. He﻿—he pointed to the written paper on the table.”

“Which you had written yourself?”

===Chinese===
“因为卡尔顿先生朝房门那边指了指，我便和玛丽一同进去了。”

“您当时立刻就知道范·诺曼小姐已经不在人世了？”

“我说不准，可是玛丽朝她走了过去，随即带着又一声可怕的尖叫转过身去，我便觉得范·诺曼小姐必定是死了。”

“卡尔顿先生说了什么？”

“他什么也没说。他——他指了指桌上的那张字条。”

“那张字条是您亲笔写的？”

===Original===
“Yes, but he didn’t know that.” Cicely spoke eagerly, as if saying something of importance. “He thought she wrote it.”

“Never mind that point for the moment. But I must now ask you to explain that written message which you have declared that you yourself wrote.”

At this Cicely’s manner changed. She became again the obstinate and defiant woman who had answered the coroner’s earlier questions.

“I refuse to explain it.”

“Consider a moment,” said Mr. Benson quietly. “Sooner or later﻿—perhaps at a trial﻿—you will be obliged to explain this matter. How much better, then, to confide in us now, and perhaps lead to an immediate solution of the mystery.”

Cicely pondered a moment, then she said, “I have nothing to conceal, I will tell you. I did write that paper, and it was the confession of my heart. I am very miserable, and when I wrote it I quite intended to take my own life. When I was called to go to Miss Van Norman in the library, I gathered up some notes and lists from my desk to take to her. In my haste I must have included that paper without knowing it, for when I reached my room I could not find it. And then﻿—then when I saw it﻿—there on the table﻿—I﻿—” Cicely had again grown nervous and excited. Her voice trembled, her eyes filled with tears, and, fearing a nervous collapse, Mr. Benson hurried on to other questions.

===Chinese===
“是的，可他并不知道。”西塞莉说得急切，仿佛这话干系重大，“他以为那是她写的。”

“这一点暂且不去管它。不过现在，我必须请您解释那张字条——您已声明那是您本人亲笔所写。”

听到这里，西塞莉的态度变了。她又变回了回答验尸官早先提问时那个固执倔强的女人。

“我拒绝解释。”

“请三思，”本森先生不动声色地说，“这件事您迟早——也许要等到开庭审案的时候——总得交代清楚。与其到那时，何不现在就向我们和盘托出，说不定立刻便能使这个谜团迎刃而解。”

西塞莉沉吟片刻，然后说道：“我没有什么要隐瞒的，我这就告诉你们。那张纸条确实是我写的，那是我心底的自白。我心里悲苦已极，写下它的时候，我打定了主意要自寻短见。后来有人来唤我去书房见范·诺曼小姐，我便从书桌上收拢了几张便条和单子，预备带给她。匆忙之中，我一定是浑然不觉地把那张纸也夹带在内了，因为等我回到自己房里，我再也找不到它。再后来——后来等我看见它——就摆在那张桌上——我——”西塞莉这时又变得紧张而激动起来。她声音发颤，眼中噙满泪水；本森先生生怕她神经承受不住，赶紧转问别的事情。

===Original===
“Whom does that S. in your note stand for.”

“That I shall never tell.” The determination in her voice convinced him that it was useless to insist on that point, so the coroner went on.

“Perhaps we have no right to ask. Now you must tell me some other things, and, believe me, my questions are not prompted by curiosity, but are necessary to the discovery of the truth. Why did Mr. Carleton point to that paper?”

“He﻿—he seemed so shocked and stunned that he was almost unable to speak. I suppose he thought that would explain why she had killed herself.”

“But she hadn’t killed herself.”

“But he thought she had, and he thought that paper proved it.”

===Chinese===
“您字条上的那个 S. 指的是谁。”

“这个我至死也不会说。”她话音里的决绝使验尸官明白，在这一节上再强逼也是徒劳，于是他接着问下去。

“也许这个问题我们无权过问。那么，另外有几件事您必须告诉我了。请相信，我的发问并非出于好奇，而是查明真相所必需。卡尔顿先生为什么指向那张字条？”

“他——他像是惊骇太过、吓懵了，几乎连话都说不出来。我猜想，他以为那张字条能说明她自寻短见的缘由。”

“可她并没有自寻短见。”

“可他以为她是自寻短见，还以为那张字条便是明证。”

===Original===
“But why had he need to prove it, and to you?”

“I don’t know. I don’t know *what* he thought! I don’t know what I thought myself after I reached the library door and looked in and saw that dreadful sight! Oh, I shall see it all my life!” At the memory Cicely broke down again and sank into her chair, shaking with convulsive sobs.

Mr. Benson did not disturb her further, but proceeded to question the others.

The account of Marie, the maid, merely served to corroborate what Cicely had said. Marie, too, had heard Carleton’s cry for help, and, throwing on a dressing-gown, had run downstairs to Madeleine’s room. Not finding her mistress there, she had hurried down to the first floor, reaching the lower hall but a few minutes after Cicely did. She said also that it was just about half-past eleven by the clock in her own room when she heard Mr. Carleton’s cry.

“You knew who it was that had called out so loudly?” asked Mr. Benson.

===Chinese===
“可他有什么必要证明这一点？又为什么要证明给您看？”

“我不知道。我不知道他究竟在想什么！连我自己走到书房门口、朝里望见那可怕景象之后在想些什么，我都不知道！啊，这辈子那景象都要缠着我不放了！”旧景重现，西塞莉又撑不住了，跌坐回椅中，抽噎得浑身发颤。

本森先生没有再去搅扰她，转而开始讯问其余的人。

女仆玛丽的供述，不过是对西塞莉所谈的印证。玛丽也听到了卡尔顿的呼救，她匆匆披上一件晨衣，跑下楼去马德琳的房间。她没有在那里找到女主人，便又赶下到一楼，比西塞莉只晚几分钟到达楼下大厅。她还说到，听见卡尔顿先生喊叫的时候，她自己房里的钟正指着十一点半光景。

“您可知道那声高喊是谁发出的？”本森先生问。

===Original===
“No, m’sieu; I heard only the shriek as of one in great disaster. I ran to Miss Van Norman’s room, as that was my first duty.”

“Were you not in attendance upon her?”

“No; she had sent me the message by Miss Dupuy, that I need not attend her when she retired.”

“Did this often occur?”

“Not often; but sometimes when Miss Van Norman sat up late, by herself, she would excuse me at an earlier hour. She was most kind and considerate of everybody.”

“Then when at last you saw Miss Van Norman in the library, what did you do?”

===Chinese===
“不知道，先生；我只听见一声惨叫，像是有人遭了大难。我便奔向范·诺曼小姐的房间，因为那是我的头等本分。”

“您不是该在跟前伺候她的吗？”

“不是；她托杜皮伊小姐给我捎了话，说她安寝时不必我伺候。”

“这种情形常有么？”

“不常有；不过范·诺曼小姐有时独自熬夜，就会早些打发我去歇着。她对谁都是那样和蔼体贴。”

“那么，当您终于在书房里见到范·诺曼小姐时，您做了什么？”

===Original===
“*Mon Dieu!* I shrieked! Why not? I was amazed, shocked, but, above all, desolated! It was a cruel scene. I knew not what to do, so, naturally, I shrieked.”

Marie’s French shrug almost convinced her hearers that truly that was the only thing to do on such an occasion.

“And now,” said Coroner Benson, “can you tell us of anything, any incident or any knowledge of your own, that will throw any light on this whole matter?”

Marie’s pretty face took on a strange expression. It was not fear or terror, but a sort of perplexity. She gave a furtive glance at Mr. Carleton and then at Miss Morton, and hesitated.

At last she spoke, slowly:

===Chinese===
“Mon Dieu！我叫喊了！为什么不呢？我又惊又骇，可最最要紧的是，我痛心极了！那场面太残酷了。我不知道该做什么，于是，很自然地，我叫喊了。”

玛丽那法国式的耸肩，几乎叫听的人都信了：遇上这种场合，那实在已是唯一可做的事。

“那么现在，”本森验尸官说，“您能否告诉我们些什么——任何一件小事，或您自己知道的任何情况——能为这整桩案子投下一线光亮？”

玛丽那张俏丽的脸上浮起一种古怪的神情。那既非畏惧，亦非惊恐，倒像是一种困惑。她偷偷觑了卡尔顿先生一眼，又觑了莫顿小姐一眼，迟疑不决。

末了她缓缓说道：

===Original===
“If monsieur could perhaps word his question a little differently﻿—with more of a definiteness﻿—”

“Very well; do you know anything of Miss Van Norman’s private affairs that would assist us in discovering who killed her.”

“No, monsieur,” said Marie promptly, and with a look of relief.

“Did Miss Van Norman ever, in the slightest way, express any intention or desire to end her life?”

“Never, monsieur.”

===Chinese===
“假如先生肯把问题稍稍换个问法——问得更确切一些——”

“好吧。您可知道范·诺曼小姐私事中的任何情形，能帮我们查出是谁杀害了她？”

“不知道，先生。”玛丽答得爽快，脸上还露出如释重负的神色。

“范·诺曼小姐可曾有过哪怕一星半点的表示，流露出结束自己性命的念头或意愿？”

“从来没有，先生。”

===Original===
“Do you think she was glad and happy in the knowledge of her fast-approaching wedding-day?”

“I am sure of it;” and Marie’s tone was that of one who well knew whereof she spoke.

“That is all, then, for the present;” and Marie, with another sidelong, curious glance at Miss Morton, resumed her seat.

Kitty French and Molly Gardner were questioned, but they told nothing that would throw any light on the matter. They had heard the cry, and while hastily dressing had heard the general commotion in the house. They had thought it must be a fire, and not until they reached the library did they know what had really happened.

“And then,” said Kitty indignantly, in conclusion of her own recital, “we were not allowed to stay with the others, but were sent to our rooms. So how can we give any evidence?”

===Chinese===
“您认为，对于转眼将至的婚期，她心里是欢喜快乐的么？”

“这一点我敢断定。”玛丽的口气，俨然是一个深知自家所言不虚的人。

“那么，眼下就问到这里。”玛丽又朝莫顿小姐投去好奇的一瞥，回了自己的座位。

基蒂·弗伦奇和莫莉·加德纳也受了讯问，但她们说不出任何有助于查明此案的东西。她们听到了那声喊叫，匆忙穿衣时又听见宅子里上上下下一片大乱。她们起初只当是失了火，直到走进书房，才知道究竟出了什么事。

“再说，”基蒂在讲完自己那番经历时愤愤不平地说，“他们还不许我们同其他人待在一起，把我们打发回了各自的房间。这样我们还能提出什么证词呢？”

===Original===
It was plain to be seen, Miss French felt herself defrauded of an opportunity that should have been hers, but Miss Gardner was of quite a different mind. She answered in whispered monosyllables the questions put by the coroner, and as she knew no more than Kitty of the whole matter, she was not questioned much.

Robert Fessenden smiled a little at the different attitudes of the two girls. He knew Kitty was eager to hear all the exciting details, while Molly shrank from the whole subject. However, as they were such minor witnesses, the coroner paid little serious attention to them or to their statements.

Miss Morton’s testimony came next. Fessenden regarded her with interest, as, composed and calm, she waited the coroner’s interrogations.

She was deliberate and careful in making her replies, and it seemed to the young detective as if she knew nothing whatever about the whole affair, but was trying to imply that she knew a great deal.

“You went to your room when the others did, at about ten o’clock?” asked Mr. Benson.

===Chinese===
一望便知，弗伦奇小姐觉得自己被剥夺了一个本该归她的机会；加德纳小姐的心思却全然两样。验尸官发问时，她只压着嗓子、一个字一个字地低声应答，而她对此案所知原不比基蒂多，也就没有多受盘问。

两位姑娘态度截然不同，罗伯特·费森登见了，不由得微微一笑。他知道基蒂巴不得听遍种种骇人的细节，而莫莉则对整个话题退避三舍。不过，她们终究只是些无足轻重的证人，验尸官对她们和她们的供述都没有认真多加理会。

接下来轮到莫顿小姐作证。她仪态安详、神色镇定地等候验尸官的讯问，费森登饶有兴味地端详着她。

她答话时字斟句酌、小心谨慎；在这位年轻的侦探看来，她对整桩事情仿佛一无所知，却又竭力要人觉出她知道得很多。

“您是同大家一起，大约十点钟回房去的？”本森先生问。

===Original===
“Yes, but I did not retire at once.”

“Did you hear any sounds that caused you alarm?”

“No, not alarm. Curiosity, perhaps, but that is surely pardonable to a naturally timid woman in a strange house.”

“Then you did hear sounds. Can you describe them?”

“I do not think they were other than those made by the servants attending to their duties. But the putting on of coal or the fastening of windows are noticeable sounds when one is not accustomed to them.”

===Chinese===
“是的，不过我没有立刻就寝。”

“您可曾听见什么声响，使您惊慌？”

“没有，谈不上惊慌。好奇，或许是有的；可对一个生性胆小、又身处陌生宅院的女人来说，这总归情有可原吧。”

“那么您确实听见了声响。能形容一下么？”

“我想那不过是仆人料理分内事务的动静罢了。只是添煤也好、闩窗也好，这类声响对一个听不惯的人而言，还是相当扎耳朵的。”

===Original===
“You could discern, then, that it was the shovelling of coal or the fastening of windows that you heard?”

“No, I could not. My hearing is extremely acute, but as my room is on the third floor, all the sounds I heard were faint and muffled.”

“Did you hear Mr. Carleton’s cry for help?”

“I did, but at that distance it did not sound loud. However, I was sufficiently alarmed to open my door and step out into the hall. I had not taken off my evening gown, and, seeing bright lights downstairs, of course I immediately went down. The household was nearly all assembled when I reached the library. I saw at once what had happened, and I saw, too, that Mrs. Markham and the younger women were quite frantic with fright and excitement. I thought it my duty therefore to take up the reins of government, and I took the liberty of telephoning for the doctor. I think there is nothing more of importance that I can tell you.”

At this Fessenden barely repressed a smile, for he could not see that Miss Morton had told anything of importance at all.

===Chinese===
“这么说，您能分辨出听见的正是铲煤添火或是闩窗的动静了？”

“不，分辨不出。我的听觉极其灵敏，可是我的房间在三楼，凡我所闻，都已又轻又闷。”

“您听见卡尔顿先生的呼救声了么？”

“听见了，不过隔着那样远，声音并不算响。饶是如此，我也惊得不轻，便开了门走进走廊。我还未曾换下晚礼服，望见楼下灯火通明，自然立刻就下去了。我到书房时，宅中上下几乎都已聚齐。我一眼便看明白出了什么事，也看见马卡姆太太和那几位年轻姑娘已经吓得魂飞魄散、乱作一团。因此我认为，由我接过执掌家政的缰绳乃是我的本分，便径自做主，打电话去请了医生。我想，再没有更要紧的事可以奉告了。”

这话险些叫费森登笑出声来，因为他实在看不出莫顿小姐讲出了任何要紧的东西。

===Original===
“I would like,” said Mr. Benson, “for you to inform us as to your relations with the Van Norman household. Have you been long acquainted with Miss Van Norman?”

“About two years,” replied Miss Morton, with a snapping together of her teeth, which was one of her many peculiarities of manner.

“And how did the acquaintance come about?”

“Her uncle and I were friends many years ago,” said Miss Morton. “I knew Richard Van Norman before Madeleine was born. We quarrelled, and I never saw him again. After his death Madeleine wrote to me, and several letters passed between us. At her invitation I made a short visit here about a year ago. Again, at her invitation, I came here yesterday to be present at her wedding.”

Miss Morton’s manner, though quiet, betokened repressed excitement rather than suppressed emotion. In no way did her hard, bright eyes show grief or sorrow, but they flashed in a way that indicated high nervous pressure.

===Chinese===
“我想请您，”本森先生说，“告诉我们您同范·诺曼一家的渊源。您同范·诺曼小姐相识很久了么？”

“大约两年。”莫顿小姐答道，牙关咯地一咬——这是她许多举止怪癖中的一种。

“这段交情是如何结下的？”

“她叔父和我多年前是朋友，”莫顿小姐说，“早在马德琳出世之前，我就认识理查德·范·诺曼了。后来我们吵翻了，从此再没有见过面。他去世之后，马德琳给我来信，我们之间通了好几封信。一年前，应她之邀，我来这里小住过些时日。昨天，又是应她之邀，我来出席她的婚礼。”

莫顿小姐的态度虽说安静，透出的却是强自按捺的亢奋，而非竭力抑制的哀伤。她那双冷硬明亮的眼睛里，看不出丝毫悲戚哀恸，只是灼灼闪动，显出神经已然绷到极处的光景。

===Original===
“Did you know that you were to inherit this house and a large sum of money at Miss Van Norman’s death?” The question was thrown at her so suddenly that Miss Morton almost gasped.

She hesitated for an appreciable instant, then with a sudden snap of her strong, angular jaw, she said, “No!”

“You had no intimation of it whatever?”

“No.” Again that excessive decision of manner, which to Fessenden’s mind, at least, stultified rather than corroborated the verity of her statement.

But Coroner Benson expressed no doubt of his witness, but merely said casually:

===Chinese===
“您可知道，范·诺曼小姐一旦亡故，这座宅子和一大笔钱就将归您继承？”这问题劈面掷来，莫顿小姐几乎倒抽一口冷气。

她迟疑了分明的一瞬，随即猛地一咬那坚强方正的下颚，说道：“不知道！”

“您事先没有得过半点风声？”

“没有。”又是那副斩钉截铁得过了头的做派——至少在费森登看来，这非但佐证不了她这番话的真实，反倒叫它显得更假。

但本森验尸官对他的证人未露半点疑心，只淡淡说道：

===Original===
“Yet, on the occasion of the tragedy last night, you at once assumed the attitude of the head of the house. You gave orders to the servants, you took up the reins of management, and seemed to anticipate the fact that the house was eventually to be your own.”

Miss Morton looked aghast. If one chose to think so, she looked as if detected in a false statement. Glancing round the room, she saw the eyes of Kitty French and of Marie, the maid, intently fixed on her. This seemed to unnerve her, and in a broken, trembling voice, almost a whine, she said:

“If I did so, it was only with a helpful motive. Mrs. Markham was so collapsed with the shock she had just sustained, that she was really incapable of giving orders. If I did so, it was only from a desire to be of service.”

This seemed indeed plausible, and the most casual observer would know that Miss Morton’s “helpfulness” could only be accomplished in a peremptory and dictatorial manner.

“Will you tell us why Miss Van Norman chose to leave you so large a bequest, when she had known you so slightly?” asked Mr. Benson.

===Chinese===
“然而，昨晚惨案发生之际，您立刻摆出了宅子女主人的架势。您向仆人发号施令，执掌起管理的缰绳，仿佛早已料到这座宅子终将归您所有。”

莫顿小姐登时面如土色。要是有人存心这么想，她那模样就像谎话被当场拆穿。她环顾四周，只见基蒂·弗伦奇和女仆玛丽的眼睛都紧紧盯在自己身上。这似乎叫她心慌意乱，她用断断续续、发颤的声音——近于一种哀鸣——说道：

“我若是那样做了，也纯粹出于一片想帮忙的心意。马卡姆太太方才受的那场惊吓太重，整个人都垮了，实在发不出号施令。我若是那样做了，也只是存心想尽一份力罢了。”

这话听来确也近情近理；而最漫不经心的旁观者也看得出，莫顿小姐这份“帮忙”，只有用颐指气使、独断专行的方式才行得通。

“您愿不愿意告诉我们，范·诺曼小姐同您交情这般浅，为何却把这么大一份遗赠留与您？”本森先生问。

===Original===
Fessenden thought Miss Morton would resent this question, but instead she answered, willingly enough:

“Because she knew that except for my unfortunate quarrel with Richard Van Norman, many years ago, the place would have been mine anyway.”

“You mean you were to have married Mr. Van Norman.”

“I mean just that.”

Miss Morton looked a little defiant, but also an air of pride tinged her statement, and she seemed to be asserting her lifelong right to the property.

===Chinese===
费森登原以为莫顿小姐听了这一问题必会愠怒，不料她回答得倒相当爽快：

“因为她知道，若不是多年前我同理查德·范·诺曼那场不幸的争吵，这份产业无论如何本来就会是我的。”

“您是说，您原是要嫁给范·诺曼先生的。”

“我正是此意。”

莫顿小姐的神情带着几分倨傲，但她的话音里同时也透着一层骄矜，仿佛在申明自己对这份产业享有一世的权利。

===Original===
“Miss Van Norman, then, knew of your friendship with her uncle, and the reason of its cessation?”

“She learned of it about two years ago.”

“How?”

“By finding some letters of mine among Mr. Van Norman’s papers, shortly after his death.”

“And in consequence of that discovery she willed you this house at her death?”

===Chinese===
“那么，范·诺曼小姐知道您同她叔父的旧谊，也知道那段情谊中辍的缘由了？”

“她大约在两年前获悉的。”

“如何获悉的？”

“范·诺曼先生去世后不久，她在他遗下的文件中间发现了我写的几封信。”

“于是，只因有了那一发现，她便立下遗嘱，把她死后这座宅子留与您？”

===Original===
“Yes; that is, I suppose she must have done so﻿—as she did so will it.”

“But you did not know of it, and the reading of the will was to you a surprise?”

“Yes,” declared Miss Morton, and though the coroner then dismissed her without comment on her statements, there were several present who did not believe the lady spoke veraciously.

Tom Willard was called next, and Fessenden wondered what could be the testimony of a man who had not arrived on the scene until more than two hours after the deed was done.

And indeed there was little that Tom could say. Mr. Benson asked him to detail his own movements after he left the house the night before.

===Chinese===
“是的；或者说，我想她必是因此才那样立下的——因为她确实这样立了遗嘱。”

“可您原先并不知情；遗嘱宣读之时，于您还是一件意外之事？”

“是的。”莫顿小姐一口断言。验尸官随即让她退下，对她的陈述未作任何评断；只是在场诸人中颇有几位，并不相信这位女士说的是实话。

下一个传到汤姆·威拉德。此人在案发两个多小时之后方才到场，能有什么证词？费森登心中好生纳闷。

其实汤姆也确实无可多言。本森先生请他细述前晚离宅之后的行踪。

===Original===
“There’s little to tell,” said Tom, “but I’ll try to be exact. I went away from this house about ten o’clock, taking with me a suitcase full of clothes. I went directly to the Mapleton Inn, and though I don’t know exactly, I should say I must have reached there in something less than ten minutes. Then I went to the office of the establishment, registered, and asked for a room. The proprietor gave me a good enough room, a bellboy picked up my bag, and I went to my room at once.”

“And remained there?”

“Yes; later I rang for some ice water, which the same boy brought to me. Directly after that I turned in. I slept soundly until awakened by a knocking at my door at about two o’clock in the morning.”

“The message from this house?”

“Yes. The landlord himself stood there when I opened the door, and told me I was wanted on the telephone. When I went to the telephone I heard Miss Morton’s voice, and she asked me to come over here. I came as quickly as possible, and﻿—”

Tom’s voice broke at this point, and, feeling that his story was finished, Mr. Benson considerately asked him no further questions.

===Chinese===
“没什么可讲的，”汤姆说，“不过我尽量说得确切。我大约十点钟离开这座宅子，随身带着一只装满衣物的手提箱。我径直去了梅普尔顿旅馆，虽然说不十分准，我估摸着不到十分钟便到了。随后我到旅馆的账房，登记了姓名，要了一个房间。老板给了我一间蛮不错的屋子，一个侍应生提起我的提包，我便径自上房间去了。”

“然后就一直待在房里？”

“是的；后来我按铃要了些冰水，还是那个侍应生送来的。打那以后我便睡下了。我睡得极沉，直到凌晨两点钟光景，被一阵敲门声惊醒。”

“是这座宅子打来的口信？”

“是的。我开了门，老板本人立在门外，说有我的电话。我走到电话跟前，听出是莫顿小姐的声音，她请我立即过来。我便尽快赶了过来，随后——”

说到此处，汤姆的声音哽住了。本森先生觉得他的话已讲完，便体贴地不再问他什么。

## XI “I Decline to Say” / 十一　「我拒绝回答」

===Original===
Schuyler Carleton was questioned next When Mr. Benson asked him to tell his story, he hesitated and finally said that he would prefer to have the coroner ask direct questions, which he would answer.

“Did you go away from this house with the other guests at about ten o’clock last evening?”

“No, I was not here at dinner. I left at about half-past five in the afternoon.”

“Where did you go?”

“I went directly home and remained there until late in the evening.”

“Mr. Fessenden was with you?”

===Chinese===
接下来受讯问的是斯凯勒·卡尔顿。本森先生请他陈述事情经过，他迟疑了一下，最后说，他宁愿请验尸官直接发问，由他来作答。

“昨晚十点钟前后，您是同其余客人一起离开这宅子的吗？”

“不是，我昨晚不在这里用晚餐。我是下午五点半左右离开的。”

“您去了哪里？”

“我径直回了家，一直待到深夜。”

“费森登先生和您在一起？”


===Original===
“He was with us at dinner. He is staying at my house, as he was invited to be best man at the wedding.”

Though this statement came calmly from Carleton’s lips, it was evident to all that he fully appreciated the tragic picture it suggested.

“He was with you through the evening?”

“Part of the time. He went early to his room, saying he had some business to attend to.”

“Why were you two not here to dinner with Miss Van Norman?”

===Chinese===
“他同我们一起用的晚餐。他眼下住在我家，因为受邀在婚礼上担任伴郎。”

这话虽从卡尔顿口中平静道出，但人人都看得分明：这话背后的悲剧图景，他自己心中全然有数。

“整个晚上他都与您在一起？”

“有一段时间是在的。他早早回了自己房间，说有些事要处理。”

“你们两位为何不来陪范·诺曼小姐用晚餐？”


===Original===
Fessenden looked up, surprised at this question. Surely Mr. Benson had gathered odd bits of information since morning.

Schuyler Carleton looked stern.

“I did not come because I did not wish to. Mr. Fessenden remained with me, saying he did not care to attend the dinner unless I did.”

Carleton looked casually at Fessenden as he said this, and though there was no question in the glance, Rob nodded his head in corroboration of the witness.

“You spent the entire evening at home, then?”

===Chinese===
费森登抬起头来，对这一问颇感意外。看来打从早晨起，本森先生零零碎碎已打听到不少消息了。

斯凯勒·卡尔顿神色严峻。

“我不来，是因为我不想来。费森登先生留下来陪我，说我不去赴宴，他也不愿去。”

说这话时，卡尔顿随意地瞥了费森登一眼；那目光虽无探询之意，罗布却点了点头，为证人的话作了印证。

“那么，整个晚上您都待在家里？”


===Original===
“Yes, until a late hour.”

“And then?”

“I returned here between eleven and twelve o’clock.”

“To make a call?”

“No, I came upon an errand.”

===Chinese===
“是的，直到很晚。”

“然后呢？”

“十一点到十二点之间，我回到了这里。”

“是来拜访的？”

“不，我是有事而来。”


===Original===
“What was the errand?”

“As it has no bearing upon the case, I think it is my privilege to decline to answer.”

“You entered the house with a latchkey.”

“I did.”

“Is that latchkey your own property?”

“For the time, yes. Mrs. Markham gave it to me a few days ago, for my convenience, because I have occasion to come to the house so frequently.”

===Chinese===
“什么事？”

“既然此事与本案无关，我认为我有权拒绝回答。”

“您是用弹簧锁钥匙进的宅子。”

“是的。”

“那把弹簧锁钥匙是您自己的东西吗？”

“眼下算是吧。那是马卡姆太太几天前给我的，图个方便，因为我常有事来这宅子。”


===Original===
“Was it your intention when you went away in the afternoon to return later?”

“It was.”

“Upon this secret errand?”

“Yes.”

===Chinese===
“您下午离开时，本来就打算稍后回来？”

“是的。”

“就是为着那件隐秘的差事？”

“是的。”


===Original===
“Did you expect to see Miss Van Norman when you entered the house with the latchkey?”

“I did not.”

“And when you entered you discovered the tragedy in the library?”

Schuyler Carleton hesitated. His dry lips quivered and his whole frame shook with intense emotion. “Y-yes,” he stammered.

===Chinese===
“您用弹簧锁钥匙进宅时，可曾料想会见到范·诺曼小姐？”

“不曾料想。”

“而您一进门，就在书房里发现了这桩惨剧？”

斯凯勒·卡尔顿迟疑了。他干涩的嘴唇颤抖着，整个身躯因激荡的情绪而发抖。“是——是的，”他讷讷地说道。


===Original===
But the mere fact of that hesitation instantly kindled a spark of suspicion in the minds of some of his hearers. Until that moment Carleton’s excessive agitation had been attributed entirely to his grief at the awful fate which had come to his fiancée; but now, all at once, the man’s demeanor gave an impression of something else.

Could it be guilt?

Fessenden looked at his friend curiously. In his mind, however, no slightest suspicion was aroused, but he wondered what it was that Carleton was keeping back. Surely the man must know that to make any mystery about his call at the Van Norman mansion the night before, was to invite immediate and justifiable suspicion.

The court had instructed the district attorney to be present at the inquest, and though that unobtrusive gentleman had taken notes, and otherwise shown a quiet interest in the proceedings, he now awakened to a more alert manner, and leaned forward to get a better look at the white, set face of the witness.

Carleton looked like a marble image. His refined, patrician features seemed even handsomer for their haggard agony. Surely he was in no way responsible for the awful deed that had been done, and yet just as surely he was possessed of some awful secret fear which kept every nerve strained and tense.

Endeavoring not to exhibit the surprise and dismay which he felt, Coroner Benson continued his questions.

===Chinese===
然而单是这迟疑本身，便立时在部分听者心中点燃了一星怀疑的火星。在那之前，卡尔顿的过分激越一直被完全归因于他悲恸于未婚妻的惨遭横祸；可此刻，此人的举止忽然给人一种别样的观感。

那会不会是心虚？

费森登好奇地望着自己的朋友。不过他心里并未生出丝毫疑窦，只是不明白卡尔顿究竟有什么话藏着掖着。此人总该明白：头天夜里造访范·诺曼宅邸一事若是故弄玄虚，那无异于自己招来怀疑，而且这怀疑来得理直气壮。

法庭已指派地方检察官（district attorney）出席验尸审讯。这位不显山露水的先生虽然一路记着笔记，对审讯的进行表现出一种安静的关注，此刻却振作起来，神色警觉了许多，身子前倾，想把证人那张苍白僵板的脸看得更真切些。

卡尔顿看上去像一尊大理石像。那张清贵儒雅的面容，因憔悴的痛楚反倒显得愈发俊美。他想必与那桩可怕的罪行毫无干系；然而同样可以肯定，某种可怕的秘密恐惧攫住了他，把他每一根神经都绷得紧紧的。

本森验尸官竭力不显出心中的惊愕与惶惑，继续发问。


===Original===
“And then, when you discovered Miss Van Norman, what did you do?”

Carleton passed his hand across his white brow. “I hardly know,” he said. “I was stunned﻿—dazed. I went toward her, and, seeing the dagger on the floor, I picked it up mechanically, scarcely knowing what I did. I felt intuitively that the girl was dead, but I did not touch her, and, not knowing what else to do, I cried out for help.”

“And turned on the lights?”

“I pushed several electric buttons, not knowing which were lights and which bells; my principal idea was to arouse the inmates of the house at once.”

“Who first appeared in answer to your call?”

“Miss Dupuy came running downstairs at once, followed by Miss Van Norman’s maid.”

===Chinese===
“那么，发现范·诺曼小姐之后，您做了什么？”

卡尔顿抬手抹过自己惨白的额头。“我自己也说不清，”他说，“我当时懵了——头昏眼花。我朝她走去，看见地上的匕首，便机械地拾了起来，几乎不知道自己在做什么。我直觉地感到姑娘已经死了，但我没有碰她；不知如何是好，便高声呼救。”

“然后开了灯？”

“我按了好几个电钮，分不清哪些管灯、哪些管铃；我心里主要想着立刻唤醒宅中众人。”

“谁最先应声赶来？”

“杜皮伊小姐立刻奔下楼来，后面跟着范·诺曼小姐的女仆。”


===Original===
“And then you pointed to the paper that lay on the table near Miss Van Norman’s hand.”

“Yes; I could not speak, and I thought that would tell Miss Dupuy that Miss Van Norman had taken her own life.”

“You thought, then, that Miss Van Norman wrote the message?”

“I thought so then﻿—and I think so now.”

This, of course, produced a sensation, but it was only evidenced by a deeper silence on the part of the startled audience.

===Chinese===
“接着，您便指向那张字条——它就摊在桌上，离范·诺曼小姐的手不远。”

“是的；我说不出话来，心想这样一来，杜皮伊小姐便能明白：范·诺曼小姐是自寻了短见。”

“那么您当时认为，字条是范·诺曼小姐写的？”

“我当时是这么想的——现在也还是这么想。”

这话自然引起了一阵震动，只是它仅仅表现为受惊的听众愈发深沉的寂静。


===Original===
“But Miss Dupuy asserts that she wrote it,” said the coroner.

To this Schuyler Carleton merely gave a slight bow of his handsome head, but it said as plainly as words that his belief was not altered by Miss Dupuy’s assertion.

“Granting for the moment, then,” went on Mr. Benson, “that Miss Van Norman did write it, is the message intelligible to you?”

“Intelligible, yes;” said Carleton, “but, as I have said before, inexplicable.”

This ambiguous speech meant little to most of the listeners, but it seemed to give Robert Fessenden food for thought, and he looked at Carleton with a new wonder in his eyes.

===Chinese===
“可是杜皮伊小姐一口咬定，字条是她写的。”验尸官说。

对此，斯凯勒·卡尔顿只是把他那颗俊美的头微微一颔，可这一颔分明如言语一般清楚：杜皮伊小姐的说法，丝毫没有动摇他的信念。

“那么，姑且认定，”本森先生接着说，“字条就是范·诺曼小姐写的——这些话您能读得懂吗？”

“读是读得懂，”卡尔顿说，“不过，正如我先前所言，无法解释。”

这句模棱两可的话，对大多数听者而言没有多少意味，却似乎给了罗伯特·费森登一番咀嚼的余地；他望着卡尔顿，眼中流露出一种新的诧异。


===Original===
“Mr. Carleton,” said the coroner, with a note of gravity in his voice, “I think it my duty to tell you that your own interests require you to state the nature of your errand to this house last night.”

“I decline to do so.”

“Then, will you state as exactly as you can the hour at which you entered the front door?”

“I don’t know precisely. But Miss Dupuy has testified that she came downstairs in response to my call at half-past eleven. I came into the house a﻿—a few moments before.”

“That is all,” said the coroner abruptly. “Mr. Hunt, if you please.”

===Chinese===
“卡尔顿先生，”验尸官说，语气凝重，“我认为我有责任告诉您：为您自身利益计，您理应说明昨晚来这宅子所为何事。”

“我拒绝奉告。”

“那么，请您尽量确切地说出，您进前门是在几点钟。”

“我说不准。不过杜皮伊小姐已经作证，她是十一点半应我的呼喊下的楼。我进宅子——是在那之前几分钟。”

“问完了，”验尸官突兀地说，“有劳亨特先生。”


===Original===
The man from headquarters, who had guarded the present room through the night, came in from the doorway where he had been standing.

“Will you tell what you know concerning Mr. Carleton’s entrance last night?” said the coroner, briefly.

“I was on guard in the present room from nine o’clock on,” said Mr. Hunt. “Of course I was on the watch-out for anything unusual, and alert to hear any sound. I heard the company go away at ten o’clock, I heard most of the people in the house go to their rooms right after that. I heard and I also saw Miss Dupuy go down to the library after that, and return to her room about half-past ten. I noticed all these things because that is my business, but they made no special impression on me, as they were but the natural proceedings of the people who belonged here. Of course I was only on the lookout for intruders. I heard the sound of a latchkey and I heard the front door open at exactly quarter after eleven. I stepped out into the hall, and, looking downstairs, I saw Mr. Carleton enter. I also saw Miss Dupuy in the upper hall looking over the banister. She, too, must have seen Mr. Carleton. But as all of this was none of my business, and as nobody had entered who hadn’t a right to, I simply returned to my post. At half-past eleven I heard Mr. Carleton’s cry, and saw the lights go up all over the house. Anything more, sir?”

“Not at present, Mr. Hunt. Miss Dupuy, did you hear Mr. Carleton come in?”

===Chinese===
那位警察总部派来的便衣昨夜一直看守着存放贺礼的屋子；此刻，他从方才站立的门口走了进来。

“请把你所知道的、有关卡尔顿先生昨晚进宅的情况说一说。”验尸官简短地说道。

“我从九点钟起就在存放贺礼的屋子里值守，”亨特先生说，“当然，我时刻留神着任何异常，竖起耳朵听任何声响。我听见客人们十点钟离去，随后又听见宅中大多数人紧接着便回房去了。在那之后，我听见、也看见杜皮伊小姐下楼进了书房，十点半左右回她自己房间。这些情形我都注意到了，因为这是我的本分；不过它们并未给我留下什么特别的印象，因为那些不过是宅中之人自然而然的动静。当然，我留意的只是有没有外人闯入。十一点一刻整，我听见弹簧锁钥匙的响动，听见前门开了。我跨出房门来到楼厅，朝楼下望去，看见卡尔顿先生进来。我还看见杜皮伊小姐在楼上楼厅里凭栏俯望。她也必定看见了卡尔顿先生。但这一切都与我的职责无涉，进来的人又都有权进来，我便径自回到了岗位上。十一点半，我听见卡尔顿先生的喊声，看见满宅的灯一下子全亮了。还有什么要问的吗，先生？”

“暂时没有了，亨特先生。杜皮伊小姐，您听见卡尔顿先生进来了吗？”


===Original===
Cicely Dupuy turned an angry face toward Mr. Hunt and fairly glared at the mild-mannered man. She waited a moment before answering the coroner’s question, and then as if with a sudden resolve she spoke a sharp, quick “Yes.”

“And that was at quarter after eleven?”

“It was later,” declared Cicely. “For Mr. Carleton told you himself that he went directly into the library as soon as he came into the house, and as I heard his cry at half-past eleven he must have entered only a few moments before.”

Schuyler Carleton stared at Cicely, and she returned his gaze.

His face was absolutely inscrutable, a pallid mask, that might have concealed emotion of any sort. But there was a suggestion of fear in the strange eyes, as they gazed at Cicely, and though it was quickly suppressed it had been noted by those most interested.

The girl looked straight at him, with determination written in every line of her face. It was quite evident to the onlookers that a mental message was passing between these two.

===Chinese===
西塞莉·杜皮伊转过头去，满脸愠色，简直是怒视着那位性情温和的亨特先生。她隔了片刻才回答验尸官的问题，然后仿佛横下一条心似的，又急又硬地迸出一个“听见了”。

“那是在十一点一刻？”

“要更晚一些，”西塞莉断然说道，“因为卡尔顿先生自己都对您说了，他一进宅就直接进了书房；而我听见他喊出声是在十一点半，可见他进门只是在那一刻之前几分钟。”

斯凯勒·卡尔顿直盯着西塞莉，西塞莉也毫不回避地回望着他。

他的脸全然高深莫测，像一张苍白的面具，底下什么情绪都可能藏着。可那双古怪的眼睛望着西塞莉时，隐隐掠过一丝惧意；虽然随即便压了下去，却已被那些最关心此事的人看在了眼里。

姑娘直直地望着他，脸上每一根线条都写着决绝。旁观者看得再清楚不过：这两人之间正有无声的讯息往来传递。


===Original===
“You are sure, Mr. Hunt, that your statement as to the time is correct?” said the coroner, turning again to him.

“Perfectly sure, sir. It is my business to be sure of the time.”

“Mr. Carleton,” said Mr. Benson, “there is an apparent discrepancy here, which it is advisable for you to explain. If you came into this house at quarter after eleven, and rang the bells for help at half-past eleven, what were you doing in the meantime?”

It was out at last. The coroner’s question, though quietly put, was equivalent to an accusation. Every eye in the room was turned toward Carleton, and every ear waited in suspense for his reply.

At last the answer came. The dazed, uncertain look had returned to Carleton’s face and his voice sounded mechanical, like that of an automaton, as he replied, “I decline to say.”

===Chinese===
“你肯定吗，亨特先生，你所说的时刻确凿无误？”验尸官又转向他问道。

“完全肯定，先生。把时刻弄准，就是我的本分。”

“卡尔顿先生，”本森先生说，“这里有一处明显的出入，您最好把它解释清楚。倘若您十一点一刻进的宅子，十一点半才按铃呼救，那么这段时间里，您在做什么？”

话终于挑明了。验尸官这一问，虽然问得平平静静，却无异于一项指控。满屋的目光齐刷刷投向卡尔顿，满屋的耳朵都悬着，屏息等他回答。

回答终于来了。卡尔顿脸上又现出那种恍惚失措的神情，他答话的声音空洞机械，仿佛出自一具自动人偶之口：“我拒绝回答。”


===Original===
“I think, Mr. Carleton, you can scarcely realize the gravity of the moment, or the mistake you are making in refusing to answer this question.”

“I have nothing to say,” repeated Carleton, and his pallor changed to a faint, angry flush of red.

“I am sorry,” said Mr. Benson gently. He seemed to have lost his pompous manner in his genuine anxiety for his witness, and he looked sorrowfully at Carleton’s impassive, yet stubborn face.

“As so much hinges on the question of who wrote that paper,” he resumed, “I will make a test now that ought to convince us all. Miss Dupuy, you say that you wrote it, I believe.”

===Chinese===
“我想，卡尔顿先生，您恐怕还没有意识到此刻的严重，也没有意识到拒绝回答这个问题是怎样的一步失策。”

“我无可奉告。”卡尔顿重复道；他的苍白转而变作一层淡淡的、愠怒的红潮。

“我很抱歉。”本森先生温和地说。他对证人由衷的关切，似乎洗尽了他平日那副虚浮的官派；他悲戚地望着卡尔顿那张冷漠而又执拗的脸。

“既然一切都系于那张字条出自何人之手，”他接着说，“我现在要做一项测验，它应当能叫我们大家全都信服。杜皮伊小姐，我想您是说，字条是您写的。”


===Original===
“I did, yes, sir,” said Cicely, stammering a little now, though she had been calm enough a few minutes before.

“Then you know the words on the paper﻿—by rote?”

“Yes, sir,” said Cicely, uncertain of where this was leading.

“I will ask you, then, to take this paper and pencil, your own pencil and write the same words in the same way once more.”

“Oh, don’t ask me to do that!” implored Cicely, clasping her hands and looking very distressed.

“I not only ask you, but I direct you to do it, and do it at once.”

===Chinese===
“是我写的，是的，先生。”西塞莉说。几分钟前她还相当镇定，这时却有些结巴了。

“那么，字条上的那些话——您已经背熟了？”

“是的，先生。”西塞莉说，心里拿不准这话要把她引到哪里去。

“那么，请您拿这张纸和这支笔——用您自己的那支笔——把同样的话，照原来的样子，再写一遍。”

“哦，别叫我写吧！”西塞莉哀求道，双手紧握，满面凄惶之色。

“我不仅请您这样做——我命令您这样做，而且即刻就做。”


===Original===
An attendant handed pencil and paper to Cicely, and, after a glance at Carleton, who did not meet it, she began to write.

Though evidently agitated, she wrote clearly and evenly, and the paper she handed to Coroner Benson a moment later was practically an exact duplicate of the one found on the library table.

“It does not require a handwriting expert,” said the coroner, “to declare that these two papers were written by the same hand. The penmanship is indeed similar to Miss Van Norman’s, of whose writing I have here many specimens, but it is only similar. It is by no means identical. You may all examine these at your leisure and can only agree to what I say.”

The district attorney, who had been comparing the papers, laid them down with an air of finality that proved his agreement with the statements made.

“And so,” went on Mr. Benson, “granting, as we must, that Miss Dupuy wrote the paper, we have nothing whatever to indicate that this case is a suicide. We are, therefore, seeking a murderer, and our most earnest efforts must be made to that end. I trust, Mr. Carleton, now that you can no longer think Miss Van Norman wrote the message, that you will aid us in our work by stating frankly how you were occupied during that quarter-hour which elapsed between your entering the house and your raising the alarm?”

===Chinese===
一名随员把纸笔递到西塞莉手里；她朝卡尔顿瞥了一眼——对方却没有承接这目光——她便动笔写了。

她分明心绪激荡，落笔却清楚匀净；片刻之后呈给本森验尸官的那张纸，几乎同书房桌上发现的那张一字不差、如出一辙。

“这用不着笔迹专家，”验尸官说，“便能断定这两张纸出自同一只手。笔迹确实像范·诺曼小姐的——我这里存有她的许多笔迹样本——但也只是像而已，绝非毫厘不爽。诸位尽可以从容细验这两张纸，看罢便只能同意我的话。”

那位一直在比对两张字条的地方检察官，此刻把它们放了下来，神色笃定，一锤定音，表明他赞同方才的论断。

“既然如此，”本森先生接着说道，“我们只能认定字条是杜皮伊小姐写的；如此一来，再无半点迹象表明此案是自杀。因而，我们要寻的是一名凶手，我们也必须为此竭尽全力。卡尔顿先生，如今您总不能再认为字条出自范·诺曼小姐之手了；我相信您会坦率说明——从进宅到呼救的那一刻钟里，您究竟在做些什么——从而助我们一臂之力。”


===Original===
But Carleton preserved his stony calm.

“There was no quarter-hour,” he said; “I may have stepped into the drawing-room a moment before going to the library, but I gave the alarm almost immediately on entering the house. Certainly immediately on my discovery of﻿—of the scene in the library.”

Cicely looked defiantly at Mr. Hunt, who, in his turn, looked perplexed. The man had no wish to insinuate anything against Mr. Carleton, but as he had said, it was his business to know the time, and he knew that Mr. Carleton came into the house at quarter after eleven, and not at half-past.

The pause that followed was broken by Coroner Benson’s voice. “There is nothing more to be done at present. The inquest is adjourned until tomorrow afternoon. But we have discovered that there has been a crime committed. There is no doubt that Miss Van Norman was murdered, and that the crime took place between half-past ten and half-past eleven last night. It is our duty to spare no effort to discover the criminal. As an audience you are now dismissed.”

===Chinese===
可卡尔顿依旧守着他那份冷硬如石的平静。

“根本没有什么一刻钟，”他说，“去书房之前，我兴许顺脚进过一下客厅，可我一进宅几乎立即就报了警。确切说，我一发现——一发现书房里的情形，就立刻呼救了。”

西塞莉一脸挑战地瞪着亨特先生；这回轮到亨特先生面露困惑了。这人无意影射卡尔顿先生半分，可是正如他自己所说，弄准时刻是他的本分；而他知道，卡尔顿先生进宅是在十一点一刻，而不是十一点半。

随后的静默由本森验尸官的声音打破。“眼下再没有别的可做了。本庭验尸审讯休会，延至明日下午继续。但我们已经查明：确有一桩罪案发生。范·诺曼小姐是被人谋杀的，这一点毫无疑问；案发时间在昨晚十点半到十一点半之间。不遗余力查出凶徒，是我们的职责。旁听诸君，现在可以退场了。”

## XII Dorothy Burt / 十二　多萝西·伯特

===Original===
The people rose slowly from their chairs, and most of them looked as if they did not quite comprehend what it all meant. Among these was Carleton himself. He seemed oblivious to the fact that he was﻿—at least tacitly﻿—an accused man, and stood quietly, as if awaiting any further developments that might come.
“Look at Schuyler,” said Kitty French to Fessenden. The two had withdrawn to a quiet corner to discuss the affair. But Kitty was doing most of the talking, while Fessenden was quiet and seemed preoccupied. “Of course I suppose he must have killed Madeleine,” went on Kitty, “but it’s so hard to believe it, after all. I’ve tried to think of a reason for it, and this is the only one I can think of. They quarrelled yesterday afternoon, and he went away in a huff. I believe he came back last night to make it up with her, and then they quarrelled again and he stabbed her.”
Fessenden looked at her thoughtfully. “I think that Hunt man testified accurately,” he said. “And if so, Carleton was in the house just fifteen minutes before he gave the alarm. Now, fifteen minutes is an awfully short time to quarrel with anybody so desperately that it leads to a murder.”

===Chinese===
众人慢慢从椅子上起身，多数人面上带着茫然，似乎还不大明白这一切究竟意味着什么。卡尔顿本人也在其列。他仿佛浑然不觉自己——至少是心照不宣地——已经成了被指认之人，只静静站在那里，仿佛静候着事态接下来的一切进展。
“你瞧斯凯勒。”基蒂·弗伦奇对费森登说。两人已经退到一个清静的角落里议论这件事。不过说话的多半是基蒂，费森登沉默少言，似乎心事重重。“我当然明白，必是他杀了马德琳，”基蒂接着说，“可事到如今，要相信这一层仍旧那么难。我一直想给这事寻个因由，想来想去只想到这一个。昨天下午他们吵了嘴，他怒冲冲地走了。我相信他昨夜回来，原是要同她言归于好，谁知两人又吵起来，他便一刀刺死了她。”
费森登若有所思地望着她。“我想亨特那人的证词，说得句句属实。”他说，“倘若如此，卡尔顿身在宅中，距他发出警讯不过一刻钟。要知道，一刻钟实在短得可怜，要同人口角到那般你死我活、竟至闹出人命的地步，可远远不够。”

===Original===
“That’s true; but they both have very quick tempers. At least Madeleine had. She didn’t often do it, but when she did fly into a fury it was as quick as a flash. I’ve never seen Mr. Carleton angry, but I know he can be, for Maddy told me so.”
“Still, a quarter of an hour is too short a time for a fatal quarrel, I think. If Carleton killed her he came here for that purpose, and it was done premeditatedly.”
“Why do you say ‘*if* he killed her’? It’s been proved she didn’t kill herself; it’s been proved that no one could enter the house without a latchkey, and it’s been proved that the deed was done in that one hour between half-past ten and half-past eleven. So it had to be Mr. Carleton.”
“Miss French, you have a logical mind, and I think you’d make a clever little detective. But you have overlooked the possibility that she was killed by someone in the house.”

===Chinese===
“这话不假；可他们两个都是火爆性子。至少马德琳是。她轻易不动怒，可一旦当真发作起来，便快如闪电。卡尔顿先生动怒的模样我没见过，可我知道他也会发火，玛迪亲口同我说过。”
“不过依我看，一刻钟终究太短，吵不出一桩致命的口角来。倘若真是卡尔顿杀的她，那他便是专为这件事而来，乃是蓄谋而为。”
“你为什么偏要说‘倘若’是他杀的？已经证明，她不是自杀；已经证明，没有弹簧锁钥匙谁也进不了这宅子；也已经证明，案发就在十点半到十一点之间那一小时里。那么，凶手就非卡尔顿先生莫属。”
“弗伦奇小姐，你的头脑条理分明，我看你准能当个机灵的小侦探。只是你漏想了一种可能：她兴许是被宅中之人所杀。”

===Original===
“Some of us?” Kitty’s look of amazement almost made Fessenden smile.
“Not you or Miss Gardner,” he said. “But a burglar might have been concealed in the house.”
“I never thought of that!” exclaimed Kitty, her eyes opening wide at the thought. “Why, he might have killed us all!”
“It isn’t a very plausible theory,” said Fessenden, unheeding the girl’s remark, “and yet I could think of nothing else. Every instinct of my mind denies Carleton’s guilt. Why, he isn’t that sort of a man!”

===Chinese===
“我们中间的人？”基蒂那副惊诧的神气，险些逗得费森登笑出声来。
“不是你，也不是加德纳小姐。”他说，“但也许有窃贼预先藏进了宅子。”
“这个我倒从没想到过！”基蒂嚷道，想到这一层，眼睛都瞪圆了，“哎呀，他说不定会把我们一个个全都杀了呢！”
“这说法也算不得十分站得住脚。”费森登说，全没理会姑娘这句话，“可除它之外，我又想不出别的来。我的全部本能都否认卡尔顿有罪。唉，他不是那种人啊！”

===Original===
“Perhaps he isn’t as good as he looks,” said Kitty, wagging her head wisely. “I know a lot about him. You know he wasn’t a bit in love with Maddy.”
“You hinted that before. And was he really a mere fortune-hunter? I can’t believe that of Carleton. I’ve known the man for years.”
“He must have been, or else why did he marry her? He’s in love with another girl.”
“He is! Who?”
“I don’t know who. But Madeleine hinted it to me only a few days ago. It made her miserable. And that’s why everybody thought she wrote that paper that said, ‘I love S., but he does not love me.’ ”

===Chinese===
“也许他并不像看上去那么好呢。”基蒂说，自作聪明地把小脑袋一点一点。“我知道他好些事儿。你是知道的，他对玛迪连一星半点的爱意都没有。”
“这话你从前就影影绰绰点过。难道他当真只是个贪图财产之徒？我可不肯这样看卡尔顿。这人我认识好些年了。”
“他准是如此，不然为什么娶她？他另爱着一个姑娘呢。”
“当真！是谁？”
“我不知道是谁。可就在几天之前，马德琳还向我隐隐提过。这事把她折磨得好苦。所以大家才都以为，那张字条是她写的——‘我爱S.，可他并不爱我。’”

===Original===
“And you don’t know who this rival is?”
“No, but I know what she’s like. She’s the ‘clinging rosebud’ effect.”
“What *do* you mean?”
“Just that. You know Madeleine was a big, grand, splendid type﻿—majestic and haughty; and she thought Schuyler loved better some little, timid girl, who would sort of look up to him, and need his protection.”

===Chinese===
“那么这位情敌是谁，你也不知道？”
“不知道，可我知道她是个什么样的人物。她就是‘缠人的玫瑰花蕾’那一类。”
“你这话究竟是什么意思？”
“就是这个意思。你知道，马德琳属于高大、华贵、光彩照人的类型——气度威严，神情高傲；而她以为，斯凯勒更喜爱的却是那种娇小怯懦的姑娘，会事事依仰着他，需要他的庇护。”

===Original===
Fessenden looked steadily at Miss French. “Are you imagining all this,” he said, “or is it true?”
“Both,” responded Kitty, with a charming little smile. “Maddy just hinted it to me, and I guessed the rest. You know, I have detective instinct too, as well as you.”
“You have, indeed;” and Rob gave an admiring glance to the pouting red lips, and roguish eyes. “But tell me more about it.”
“There isn’t much to *tell*,” said Kitty, looking thoughtful, “but there’s a lot to deduce.”
“Well, tell me what there is to tell, and then we’ll both deduce.”

===Chinese===
费森登目不转睛地望着弗伦奇小姐。“这些话，你是凭空臆想出来的呢，”他说，“还是确有其事？”
“两样都有。”基蒂答道，脸上绽出一个娇俏的浅笑。“玛迪只向我点了一两句，剩下的全是我猜出来的。你要知道，同你一样，我也有侦探的直觉呢。”
“可不是嘛。”罗布说着，钦羡地瞟了一眼那两片微微撅起的红唇和那双促狭的眼睛。“再给我多讲讲吧。”
“要说讲，其实没多少可讲的。”基蒂说，神情转为认真，“可要推敲的却多得很。”
“好，那就把可讲的都讲给我听，回头咱们俩一齐推敲。”

===Original===
It pleased Kitty greatly to imagine she was really helping Fessenden, and she went glibly on:
“Why, you see, Maddy was unhappy﻿—we all know that﻿—and it was for some reason connected with Schuyler. Yet they were to be married, all the same. But sometimes Maddy has asked me, with such a wistful look, if I didn’t think men preferred little, kittenish girls to big, proud ones like herself.”
“And you, being a little, kittenish girl, said yes?”
“Don’t be rude,” said Kitty, flashing a smile at him. “I am kittenish in name only. And I am not little!”
“You are, compared to Miss Van Norman’s type.”
“Oh, yes; she was like a beautiful Amazon. Well, she either had reason to think, or she imagined, that Schuyler pretended to love her, and was really in love with some dear little clinging rosebud.”

===Chinese===
基蒂一想到自己果真在给费森登帮忙，心里说不出的欢喜，便滔滔不绝地讲下去：
“嗳，你是知道的，玛迪心里不快活——这咱们都知道——而根子总是或多或少牵在斯凯勒身上。可婚事照样要办。只是玛迪有时会带着那么一副怅然的神情问我：男人是不是更喜欢娇小娇憨的姑娘，而不喜欢她那样高大骄傲的？”
“那么你呢，身为一个娇小娇憨的姑娘，就答了声是？”
“别失礼。”基蒂说着，朝他飞快地嫣然一笑。“我这份娇憨全在名字上，人是一点儿也沾不上的。再说，我也并不娇小！”
“同范·诺曼小姐那一类型一比，你就算娇小了。”
“哦，那是自然；她就像一位美丽的亚马逊女杰。总之，她要么有根有据地疑心，要么就是自家臆想，认定斯凯勒对她的爱全是装出来的，心里真正恋着的是哪个娇小可人、缠人依人的玫瑰花蕾。”

===Original===
“Clinging rosebud! What an absurd expression! And yet﻿—by Jove!﻿—it just fits her! And Miss Van Norman said to me﻿—oh, I say, Miss French, don’t you know who the rosebud is?”
“No,” said Kitty, wondering at his sudden look of dismay.
“Well, I do! Oh, this is getting dreadful. Come outside with me and let’s look into this idea. I *hope* it’s only an idea!”
Throwing a soft fawn-colored cape round her, and drawing its pink-lined hood over her curly hair, Kitty went with Fessenden out on the lawn and down to the little arbor where they had sat before.

===Chinese===
“缠人的玫瑰花蕾！这说法多荒唐！可是——天哪！——放在她身上偏偏再贴切不过！范·诺曼小姐还对我说过——啊，我说，弗伦奇小姐，你可知道那位玫瑰花蕾是谁？”
“不知道。”基蒂说，他骤然变色，叫她好生纳闷。
“可我知道！哎呀，这事越来越不祥了。跟我到外面来，把这层想法细细查究一番。我巴不得它只是个想法！”
基蒂把一件柔软的小鹿色披肩往肩上一拢，将那粉衬里的兜帽罩在鬈发上，便随费森登出了宅子，走上草坪，来到他们先前坐过的那座小凉亭。

===Original===
“Did you ever hear of Dorothy Burt?” he asked, almost in a whisper.
“No; who is she?”
“Well, she’s your ‘clinging rosebud,’ I’m sure of it! And I’ll tell you why.”
“First tell me who she is.”

===Chinese===
“你可听说过多萝西·伯特？”他问道，声音低得近乎耳语。
“没有；她是谁？”
“嘿，她就是你说的那位‘缠人的玫瑰花蕾’，我敢断定！我这就告诉你缘故。”
“先告诉我她是谁。”

===Original===
“She’s Mrs. Carleton’s companion. Schuyler’s mother, you know. She lives in the Carleton household, and she is the sweetest, prettiest, shyest little thing you ever saw! ‘Clinging rosebud’ just fits her.”
“Indeed!” said Kitty, who had suddenly lost interest in the conversation. And indeed, few girls of Kitty’s disposition would have enjoyed this enthusiastic eulogy of another.
“I don’t admire that sort, myself,” went on Rob, who was tactfully observant; “I like a little more spirit and vivacity.” Kitty beamed once more. “But she’s a wonder, of her own class. I was there at dinner last night, you know, and I saw her for the first time. And, though I thought nothing of it at the time, I can look back now and see that she adores Schuyler. Why, she scarcely took her eyes off him at dinner, and she ate next to nothing. Poor little girl, I believe she was awfully cut up at his approaching marriage.”
“And what was Schuyler’s attitude toward her?” Kitty was interested enough now.

===Chinese===
“她是卡尔顿太太的女伴。就是斯凯勒的母亲，你知道的。她住在卡尔顿家里，是你平生所见最甜美、最标致、最怕羞的小人儿！‘缠人的玫瑰花蕾’这话，安在她身上再贴切不过。”
“哦，是嘛！”基蒂说，她对这场谈话的兴趣陡然淡了下去。说实在的，像基蒂这脾性的姑娘，能有几个乐意听人家这样起劲地夸另一个姑娘呢。
“那一类姑娘，我自己可不欣赏。”罗布接着说，他向来乖觉，善于察言观色；“我喜欢爽气一些、活泼一些的。”基蒂这才重新眉开眼笑。“不过她呀，在她自己那一类里，也是个出挑的人物。昨晚我在那边用晚宴，你知道的，头一回见到她。虽说当时全没在意，如今回头想来才看明白，她一心恋慕着斯凯勒。嗳，晚饭时她简直目不转睛地望着他，东西也几乎没吃一口。可怜的小姑娘，我相信，眼看他婚期日近，她心里准伤透了。”
“那么斯凯勒待她是什么态度？”基蒂此刻又来了兴致。

===Original===
Fessenden looked very grave and was silent for a time.
“It’s a beastly thing to say,” he observed at last, “but if Schuyler had been in love with that girl, and wanted to conceal the fact, he couldn’t have acted differently from the way he did act.”
“Was he kind to her?”
“Yes, kind, but with a restrained air, as if he felt it his duty to show indifference toward her.”

===Chinese===
费森登神色十分凝重，一时没有作声。
“这话实在难于出口，”他末了说道，“可倘若斯凯勒当真爱上了那姑娘，又想隐瞒这层，那么他的所作所为，实在演不出另外一个样子来。”
“他待她和气吗？”
“和气，只是带着几分克制的神气，仿佛觉得对她摆出冷淡模样，才算尽了本分。”

===Original===
“Was she with you after dinner?”
Fessenden thought.
“I went to my room early; and Mrs. Carleton had then already excused herself. Yes﻿—I left Schuyler and Miss Burt in the drawing-room, and later I saw them from my window, strolling through the rose-garden.”
“On his wedding eve!” exclaimed Kitty, with a look akin to horror in her eyes.
“Yes; and I thought nothing of it, for I simply assumed that he was devoted to Miss Van Norman, and was merely pleasant to his mother’s companion. But﻿—in view of something Miss Van Norman said to me yesterday﻿—can it be it was only yesterday?﻿—the matter becomes serious.”

===Chinese===
“晚饭以后她也同你们在一处吗？”
费森登想了想。
“我早早回房去了；那时卡尔顿太太已经告退。是——我把斯凯勒和伯特小姐留在大客厅里，后来我从自己的窗口望见他们，两人正漫步穿过玫瑰园。”
“在婚礼前夜！”基蒂嚷道，眼里露出近乎惊骇的神色。
“是啊；我当时全没把这放在心上，因为我只当他一片真心都在范·诺曼小姐身上，对他母亲的女伴不过是客气客气罢了。可是——想到范·诺曼小姐昨天对我说的话——难道那不过是昨天的事？——这事便严重起来了。”

===Original===
“What did she say?”
“It seems like betraying a confidence, and yet it isn’t, for we *must* discover if it means anything. But she said to me, with real agitation, ‘Do you know Dorothy Burt?’ At that time, I hadn’t met Miss Burt, and had never heard of her, so I said: ‘No; who is she?’ ‘Nobody,’ said Miss Van Norman, ‘less than nobody! Never mention her to me again!’ Her voice, even more than her words, betokened grief and even anger, so of course the subject was dropped. But doesn’t that prove her anxious about the girl, if not really jealous?”
“Of course it does,” said Kitty. “I know that’s the one that has been troubling Madeleine. Oh, how dreadful it all is!”
“And then, too,” Fessenden said, still reminiscently, “Miss Van Norman said she wanted to go away from Mapleton immediately after her wedding, and never return here again.”
“Did she say that! Then, of course, it was only so that Schuyler should never see the Burt girl again. Poor, dear Maddy; she was so proud, and so self-contained. But how she must have suffered! You see, she knew Schuyler admired her, and respected her and all that, and she must have thought that, once removed from the presence of the rosebud girl, he would forget her.”

===Chinese===
“她说了什么？”
“说来像是辜负人家的私房话，其实不然，因为我们必须查明这话究竟有无深意。她当时心绪激荡地对我说：‘你认识多萝西·伯特吗？’那时候我还没见过伯特小姐，连听也没听说过她，便答道：‘不认识；她是谁？’‘谁也不是，’范·诺曼小姐说，‘比谁也不是还不如！往后别再向我提起她！’她的声气，比她的言语更见出悲戚，甚至愠怒，这个话头自然就此撂下了。可这不正说明，她为那姑娘悬着心么——即便算不上真正的妒忌？”
“当然说明，”基蒂说，“我知道，一直折磨着马德琳的正是这个人。唉，这一切多么可怕啊！”
“还有一层，”费森登说，语气里仍带着追忆，“范·诺曼小姐说过，婚礼一完她就要立刻离开梅普尔顿，从此再不回来。”
“她真说过这话！那么不用说，那无非是要斯凯勒从此再见不着那位伯特姑娘。可怜的、亲爱的玛迪；她那么高傲，又那么矜持自守。可她心里得熬得多苦啊！你是知道的，她明白斯凯勒敬慕她、敬重她等等；可她一定在想，只要叫他远离了那位玫瑰花蕾姑娘，他就会把她忘掉了。”

===Original===
“But I can’t understand old Schuyler marrying Miss Van Norman if he didn’t truly love her. You know, Miss French, that man and I have been stanch friends for years; and though I rarely see him, I know his honorable nature, and I can’t believe he would marry one woman while loving another.”
“He didn’t,” said Kitty in a meaning voice that expressed far more than the words signified.
Fessenden drew back in horror.
“Don’t!” he cried. “You *can’t* mean that Schuyler put Miss Van Norman out of the way to clear the path for Miss Burt!”
“I don’t mean anything,” said Kitty, rather contradictorily. “But, as I said, Maddy was not killed by anyone inside the house﻿—I’m sure of that﻿—and no one from outside could get in, except Schuyler﻿—and he had a motive. Don’t you always, in detective work, look for the motive?”

===Chinese===
“可我就是不明白，老斯凯勒倘若不是真心爱范·诺曼小姐，又为什么要娶她。你知道，弗伦奇小姐，那人与我是多年的至交；虽说难得见面，我却深知他天性高洁，我不信他会一面爱着一个女子，一面又娶另一个。”
“他倒没有。”基蒂说，话音意味深长，道出的远比字面为多。
费森登惊骇得往后一缩。
“别说下去了！”他喊道，“你总不会是想说，斯凯勒为了给伯特小姐扫清道路，竟把范·诺曼小姐给除掉了！”
“我什么意思也没有，”基蒂说，这话未免自相矛盾。“可是，正如我方才讲的，马德琳不是宅中之人杀的——这一点我有把握——宅外之人，除了斯凯勒谁也进不来——而他又有动机。你们干侦探这一行的，难道不是永远先找动机么？”

===Original===
“Yes, but this is too horrible!”
“All murders are ‘too horrible.’ But I tell you it *must* have been Schuyler﻿—it couldn’t have been Miss Burt!”
“Don’t be absurd! That little girl couldn’t kill a fly, I’m sure. I wish you could see her, Miss French. Then you’d understand how her very contrast to Miss Van Norman’s splendid beauty would fascinate Schuyler. And I know he was fascinated. I saw it in his repressed manner last evening, though I didn’t realize it then as I do now.”
“I have a theory,” said Kitty slowly. “You know Mr. Carleton went away yesterday afternoon rather angry at Maddy. She had carried her flirtation with Tom a little too far, and Mr. Carleton resented it. I don’t blame him﻿—the very day before the wedding﻿—but it was partly his fault, too. Well, suppose he went home, rather upset over the quarrel, and then seeing Miss Burt, and her probably mild, angelic ways (I’m sure she has them!)﻿—suppose he wished he could be off with Maddy, and marry Miss Burt instead.”

===Chinese===
“是，可这也太骇人了！”
“哪一桩凶杀案不‘太骇人’。可我告诉你，凶手必是斯凯勒无疑——总不会是伯特小姐！”
“别说傻话！那小姑娘连只苍蝇也下不去手，我敢担保。你要是能见她一面就好了，弗伦奇小姐。那样你就会明白，她与范·诺曼小姐那华贵之美恰成对照，单是这份对照，就尽够叫斯凯勒着迷了。而我敢说，他确是着了迷。昨晚他那副强自克制的做派里便看得分明，只是当时我不及如今看得透。”
“我有一套推想，”基蒂缓缓说道，“你知道，卡尔顿先生昨天下午离开时，对玛迪颇为着恼。她同汤姆调情，调得略微过了头，卡尔顿先生记恨在心。这也难怪他——大喜前一日呢——可这事一半也得怪他自己。嗳，试想他回了家，为那场口角心绪难平，随后见到伯特小姐，见到她那想必温婉如天使的做派（我敢保她准是那样的！）——试想他巴不得甩了玛迪，改娶伯特小姐。”

===Original===
“But he wouldn’t kill his fiancée, if he *did* think that!”
“Wait a minute. Then suppose, after the evening in the rose-garden with the gentle, clinging little girl, he concluded he never could be happy with Maddy, and suppose he came at eleven o’clock, or whatever time it was, to tell her so, and to ask her to set him free.”
“On the eve of the wedding day? With the house already in gala dress for the ceremony?”
“Yes, suppose the very nearness of the ceremony made it seem to him impossible to go through with it.”

===Chinese===
“可他纵然起了那样的念头，也不至于杀了未婚妻呀！”
“且慢。再试想，在玫瑰园里同那温婉缠人的小姑娘度过一个晚上之后，他认定了同玛迪在一起永无幸福可言；再试想他在十一点钟——管它几点钟——来到这里，要把这话对她言明，求她放他自由。”
“在婚礼前夜？在满宅都已装点得花团锦簇、只待行礼的时候？”
“是呀，试想恰因婚礼迫在眉睫，他反倒觉得这门亲事断无办下去的可能。”

===Original===
“Well?”
“Well, and then suppose he did ask Madeleine to free him, and suppose she refused. And she *would* refuse! I know her nature well enough to know she *never* would give him up to the other girl if she could help it. And then suppose, when she refused to free him﻿—you know he has a fearfully quick temper, and that awful paper-cutter lay right there, handy﻿—suppose he stabbed her in a moment of desperate anger.”
“I can’t think it,” said Rob, after a pause; “I’ve tried, and I can’t. But, suppose all you say is true as far as this; suppose he asked her to free him, because he loved another, and suppose she was so grieved and mortified at this, that in her own sudden fit of angry jealousy﻿—you know she had a quick temper, also﻿—suppose she picked up the dagger and turned it upon herself, as she had sometimes said she would do.”

===Chinese===
“后来呢？”
“后来，再试想他当真求马德琳放他自由，而她拒绝了。她是一定会拒绝的！她的性子我摸得透：只要拦得住，她断断不肯把他让给另一个姑娘。再试想，当她拒绝放他自由的时候——你知道他那火暴性子有多吓人，而那把骇人的裁纸刀就搁在手边——试想他在绝望的盛怒之下，一刀刺进了她的身子。”
“我无论如何想不下去，”罗布顿了顿，说，“我想过，可想不下去。不过，姑且承认你说的都对，到这一步为止；姑且他是求她放他自由，因为他另有所爱；再姑且她闻言伤心屈辱到那地步，一时妒火攻心——你知道她也是个急性子——姑且她抄起那把匕首，扎向了自己，一如她从前偶尔说起过的那样。”

===Original===
Kitty listened attentively. “It might be so,” she said slowly; “you may be nearer the truth than I. But I do believe that one of us must be right. Of course, this leaves the written paper out of the question entirely.”
“That written paper hasn’t been thoroughly explained yet,” exclaimed the young man. “Now, look here, Miss French, I’m not going to wait to be officially employed on this case, though I am going to offer Carleton my legal services, but I mean to do a little investigating on my own account. The sooner inquiries are made, the more information is usually obtained. Can you arrange that I shall have an interview with Miss Dupuy?”
“I think I can,” said Kitty; “but if you let it appear that you’re inquisitive she won’t tell you a thing. Suppose we just talk to her casually, you and I. I won’t bother you.”
“Indeed you won’t. You’ll be of first-class help. When can we see her?”

===Chinese===
基蒂凝神听着。“也许是那样吧，”她缓缓地说，“或许你比我更接近真相。可我确实相信，你我二人总有一个是对的。当然，这样一来，那张字条就全然无从说起了。”
“那张字条至今还没有个透彻的解释呢，”年轻人说道，“你听着，弗伦奇小姐，我不打算等谁正式延请来经办此案——不过我自会向卡尔顿献上我律师的一臂之力——我是要自行动手查一查的。查问动手越早，通常摸到的消息就越多。你能不能安排我同杜皮伊小姐面谈一次？”
“我想办得到，”基蒂说，“可你要是露出一丝刨根问底的样子，她连一个字也不会对你讲。不如你我两人一起，装作随随便便同她聊聊。我决不搅扰你。”
“你哪里会搅扰。你是头等的帮手。咱们什么时候能见她？”

===Original===
While they had been talking, other things had been happening in the drawing-room. The people who had been gathered there had all disappeared, and, under the active superintendence of Miss Morton, the florist’s men who had put up the decorations were now taking them away. The whole room was in confusion, and Kitty and Mr. Fessenden were glad to escape to some more habitable place.
“Wait here,” said Kitty, as they passed through the hall, “and I’ll be back in a moment.”
Kitty flew upstairs, and soon returned, saying that Miss Dupuy would be glad to talk with them both in Madeleine’s sitting-room.

===Chinese===
两人说话这工夫，大客厅里也另有一番动静。先前聚在那里的人早已散尽；在莫顿小姐雷厉风行的督率下，先前搭起装饰的那些花匠伙计，正把装饰一件件拆下来。满屋狼藉，乱作一团，基蒂和费森登先生乐得走避，寻个还像样能待人的地方去。
“在这儿等等，”穿过大厅时基蒂说，“我去去就来。”
基蒂飞奔上楼，去不多时便回来了，说杜皮伊小姐乐意在马德琳的起居室里，同他们两位一谈。

## XIII An Interview with Cicely / 十三　与西塞莉的一席谈

===Original===
This sitting-room was on the second floor, directly back of Madeleine’s bedroom, the bedroom being above the library. Miss Dupuy’s own room was back of this and communicated with it.
The sitting-room was a pleasant place, with large light windows and easy chairs and couches. A large and well-filled desk seemed to prove the necessity of a social secretary, if Miss Van Norman cared to have any leisure hours.
Surrounded by letters and papers, Cicely sat at the desk as they entered, but immediately rose to meet them.
Kitty’s tact in requesting the interview had apparently been successful, for Miss Dupuy was gracious and affable.
But after some desultory conversation which amounted to nothing, Fessenden concluded a direct course would be better.
===Chinese===
这间起居室位于二楼，正在马德琳卧室的正后方，而卧室又坐落在书房楼上。杜皮伊小姐自己的房间则在起居室后面，与之相通。
起居室是个惬意的所在，窗户宽大明亮，安乐椅、长靠椅一应俱全。一张塞得满满当当的大写字台，似乎足证社交秘书一职确有必要——倘若范·诺曼小姐还指望有片刻清闲的话。
他们进去时，西塞莉正坐在写字台前，被信函文件团团围住，见他们进来，随即起身相迎。
