基蒂求得这次会晤时手段圆熟，看来显然奏了效，杜皮伊小姐客客气气，和蔼可亲。
只是不着边际地闲扯了几句之后，费森登便断定，还是直截了当为好。

===Original===
“Miss Dupuy,” he said, “I’m a detective, at least in an amateur way.”
Cicely gave a start and a look of fear came into her eyes.
“I have the interests of Schuyler Carleton at heart,” the young man continued, “and my efforts shall be primarily directed toward clearing him from any breath of suspicion that may seem to have fallen upon him.”
“O, thank you!” cried Cicely, clasping her hands and showing such genuine gratitude that Fessenden was startled by a new idea.
“I’m sure,” he said, “that you’ll give me any help in your power. As Miss Van Norman’s private secretary, of course you know most of the details of her daily life.”
“Yes; but I don’t see why I should tell everything to that Benson man!”
===Chinese===
“杜皮伊小姐，”他说，“我是个侦探——至少，算个业余的。”
西塞莉猛地一惊，眼里顿时露出惧色。
“我心里系念的是斯凯勒·卡尔顿，”年轻人接着说，“我的努力首先便要用于涤清那看似落在他身上的种种嫌疑。”
“噢，谢谢您！”西塞莉喊道，双手交握在一处，那份感激如此真挚，反倒惊得费森登生出一个新的念头。
“我相信，”他说，“凡您力所能及，必肯助我一臂。您身为范·诺曼小姐的私人秘书，她日常起居的种种细节，自然十有八九都在您掌握之中。”
“这自然；可我不明白，凭什么要我把什么都倒给本森那个人！”

===Original===
“You should tell him only such things as may have a bearing on this mystery that we are trying to clear up.”
“Then I know nothing to tell. I know nothing about the mystery.”
“No, Cicely,” said Kitty, in a soothing voice, “of course you know nothing definite; but if you could tell us some few things that may seem to you unimportant, we﻿—that is, Mr. Fessenden﻿—might find them of great help.”
“Well,” returned Cicely slowly, “you may ask questions, if you choose, Mr. Fessenden, and I will answer or not, as I prefer.”
===Chinese===
“您该告诉他的，只是那些与我们正力图查清的这桩疑案有所关涉的事。”
“那我没什么可说的。这桩疑案，我一无所知。”
“嗳，西塞莉，”基蒂用好言宽解道，“你自然不知道什么确凿之事；可要是你能讲几件你自己觉得无关紧要的小事，我们——我是说费森登先生——或许会发现它们大有裨益。”
“好吧，”西塞莉慢吞吞地答道，“您要是愿意问，就问吧，费森登先生；至于答不答，全看我高兴。”

===Original===
“Thank you, Miss Dupuy. You may feel sure I will ask only the ones I consider necessary to the work I have undertaken. And first of all, was Miss Van Norman in love with Carleton?”
“She was indeed, desperately so.”
“Yet she seemed greatly attached to her cousin, Mr. Willard.”
“That was partly a cousinly affection, and partly a sort of coquetry to pique Mr. Carleton.”
“And was Carleton devoted to her?”
“Must I answer that?” Cicely’s eyes looked troubled.
===Chinese===
“多谢，杜皮伊小姐。您尽可放心，我只问那些我认为对我担起的这桩事必不可少的问题。那么首先——范·诺曼小姐爱卡尔顿吗？”
“她真爱，爱得死去活来。”
“可她对她那位表亲威拉德先生，似乎也十分亲厚。”
“那一份亲厚，一半是表亲间的情谊，一半是几分卖弄风情的心思，为的是刺一刺卡尔顿先生。”
“那么，卡尔顿一心爱着她吗？”
“这我非答不可么？”西塞莉的眼中露出为难之色。

===Original===
“Yes, you must.” Fessenden’s voice was very gentle.
“Then he was not devoted to her; in fact, he loved another.”
“Who is this other?”
“Dorothy Burt, his mother’s companion, who lives at the Carleton home.”
“Did Miss Van Norman know this?”
“Yes, she learned of it lately, and it broke her heart. That is why she was so uncertain and erratic in her moods; that is why she coquetted with Mr. Willard, to arouse Schuyler Carleton’s jealousy.”
===Chinese===
“是的，非答不可。”费森登的声音极为温和。
“那么，他并非一心爱她；其实，他另有所爱。”
“另有所爱的是谁？”
“多萝西·伯特，他母亲的女伴，就住在卡尔顿家里。”
“范·诺曼小姐知道这件事吗？”
“是的，她新近才觉察，心都碎了。正因如此，她的心绪才那样反复无常、飘忽不定；正因如此，她才同威拉德先生卖俏调情，好激起斯凯勒·卡尔顿的醋意。”

===Original===
“This throws a new light on it all,” said Fessenden gravely. “And this Miss Burt﻿—did she return Carleton’s regard?”
“I don’t know,” said Cicely, and her agitation seemed to increase, though she tried hard to conceal it. “Of course Miss Van Norman didn’t speak openly of this matter, but I knew her so well that I easily divined from her moods and her actions that she knew she had a rival in Mr. Carleton’s affections.”
“Then he cared more for her in time past?”
“Yes, until that girl came to live with his mother. She’s a designing little thing, and she just twisted Mr. Carleton round her finger.”
“Do you know her personally, Miss Dupuy?”
A look of intense hatred came over Cicely’s expressive face.
===Chinese===
“这么一来，事情全然换了一副光景，”费森登郑重地说，“那么这位伯特小姐——卡尔顿的这番情意，她可有回报？”
“我不知道，”西塞莉说。她的激动似乎有增无减，虽则她极力掩饰。“这种事，范·诺曼小姐当然不会明言，可我对她知之极深，从她的心绪与举止，不难窥知她已明白：在卡尔顿先生的情爱里，自己有了对手。”
“那么，从前他待她要好得多？”
“是啊，直到那姑娘搬来与他的母亲同住。她是个心机深沉的小东西，生把卡尔顿先生玩弄于股掌之上。”
“您本人认识她么，杜皮伊小姐？”
一阵深切的憎恨掠过西塞莉那张表情丰富的脸。

===Original===
“No! I wouldn’t meet her for anything. But I have seen her, and I know perfectly well that Mr. Carleton cares for her more than he did for Miss Van Norman.”
“Yet he was about to marry Miss Van Norman.”
“Yes; because they were engaged before he saw the Burt girl. Then, you see, he didn’t think it honorable to refuse to marry her, and she﻿—”
“He had asked her, then, to give him back his freedom?”
“Yes, he had. And Miss Van Norman very rightly refused to do so.”
“Oh, Cicely,” cried Kitty, “do you *know* this, or are you only surmising it?”
===Chinese===
“不！说什么我也不愿同她见面。可我见过她，而且我心知肚明：卡尔顿先生对她的情分，远过于从前对范·诺曼小姐。”
“可他还是要娶范·诺曼小姐。”
“是啊；因为他们订婚在先，在他见到那位伯特姑娘之前。您瞧，他后来便觉得，悔婚有亏道义，而她——”
“这么说，他曾求她还他自由？”
“是的，求过。而范·诺曼小姐一口回绝了，回绝得完全在理。”
“哦，西塞莉，”基蒂叫道，“这些你是确切知道呢，还是不过揣测揣测？”

===Original===
“I know it, Miss French. In her sorrow over the matter, Miss Van Norman often confided in me as in a friend.”
“And you were a good friend to her, I’m sure,” said Fessenden heartily. “Now, Miss Dupuy, do you think it could have been possible that Mr. Carleton came here late last night to ask Miss Van Norman once again to release him from the marriage?”
“He might have done so,” said Cicely in a noncommittal tone. “He was very much annoyed at her behavior with Mr. Willard in the afternoon.”
“But that was on purpose to annoy him?”
“Yes, and it succeeded.”
“How do you know all this?”
===Chinese===
“我知道，弗伦奇小姐。范·诺曼小姐为这事伤心的时候，常拿我当知己朋友，向我倾吐。”
“而我相信，您也正是她的好朋友，”费森登恳切地说。“那么，杜皮伊小姐——您以为有无这种可能：卡尔顿先生昨夜深宵来此，是想求范·诺曼小姐再放他一次，解除婚约？”
“他兴许来过，”西塞莉不置可否地说。“下午她同威拉德先生那番行止，叫他恼火得很。”
“可那正是存心气他呀？”
“正是，而且气着了。”
“这些您是怎么知道的？”

===Original===
“Miss Van Norman intimated as much just before dinner, when we were here alone. She feared Mr. Carleton was so angry he wouldn’t come to dinner at all.”
“And he didn’t.”
“No, he didn’t.”
“But, Miss Dupuy, it would scarcely be possible to think that if he did return later to ask his release﻿—it would *not* be possible to think that on Miss Van Norman’s refusal to release him he﻿—was so incensed against her that﻿—”
“Oh, no, *no*!” cried Cicely. “Of course he didn’t kill her! Of *course* he didn’t! She killed herself! I don’t care what anyone says﻿—I *know* she killed herself!”
“If so,” said Fessenden, “we must prove it by keeping on with our investigations. And now, Miss Dupuy, will you tell me what was your errand when you returned to the library late last night, when the two doctors were alone there in charge of the room?”
===Chinese===
“晚饭之前，就我们两个在这里的时候，范·诺曼小姐言语间透露过这个意思。她怕卡尔顿先生气狠了，索性连晚饭都不来吃了。”
“他也当真没来。”
“是啊，没来。”
“可是，杜皮伊小姐，倘若他当真深夜折返、前来求去——那便万万不能设想：范·诺曼小姐一回绝，他便——对她怀恨至此，竟至于——”
“噢，不，不！”西塞莉喊道，“他当然没有杀她！他当然没有！她是自杀的！随人家怎么说——我知道她是自杀的！”
“倘若果真如此，”费森登说，“我们更须查访不辍，将这一点证实。那么现在，杜皮伊小姐——昨夜深夜，两位医生独自留守书房之际，您又回书房去，所为何来？”

===Original===
“I didn’t!” declared Cicely, her cheeks flaming and her blue eyes fairly glaring at her interrogator.
“Please stick to the truth, Miss Dupuy,” said Fessenden coldly. “If you don’t, we can’t credit any of your statements. You opened the door very softly, and were about to enter, when you spied the doctors and withdrew.”
“I went to get that paper,” said Cicely, somewhat sulkily.
“Why did you want that?”
“Because it was mine. I had a right to it.”
“Then why didn’t you go on in and get it? The doctors’ presence need have made no difference.”
===Chinese===
“我没有！”西塞莉斩钉截铁，双颊烧得通红，一双蓝眼睛直瞪着盘问的人。
“请说实话，杜皮伊小姐，”费森登冷冷地说，“否则您的证词，我们一概无从采信。您当时把门开得极轻，正要进去，瞥见两位医生在里头，便退了回来。”
“我是去取那张纸的，”西塞莉说，话音里带着几分愠意。
“您要它何用？”
“因为那是我的。我有权要回它。”
“那么您何不径直进去取呢？两位医生在场，原不碍事的。”

===Original===
“I don’t know *why* I didn’t! I wish you’d stop asking questions!”
“I will, in a moment. You are sure you wrote that paper yourself?”
“Of course I am!” The answer was snapped out pertly.
“And you wrote it meaning yourself? You didn’t write it with the intent that it should be taken for Miss Van Norman’s message?”
Cicely eyes dropped involuntarily. Then she raised them, and stared straight at Fessenden. “What do you mean?” she asked haughtily.
“Just what I say. Was that written paper an expression of your own heart’s secret?”
===Chinese===
“我不知道我为什么没进去！但愿您别再问了！”
“这就问完了。您能断定，那张纸是您亲笔所写？”
“当然断定！”这一声答话，没好气地脱口而出。
“而且您写的时候，指的是您自己？并不是存心要让人把它认作范·诺曼小姐的遗言？”
西塞莉的眼睛不由自主地垂了下去。随后她抬起眼来，直盯着费森登。“您这话是什么意思？”她傲然问道。
“就是我说的这个意思。那张字条，可写的是您自己心底的秘密？”

===Original===
It must have been because of Fessenden’s magnetism, or compelling sympathy, but for some reason Cicely took no offense at this, and answered simply, “Yes.”
“Strange,” mused Rob, “how that man won so many women’s hearts.”
“No, it isn’t strange,” said Cicely, also in slow, thoughtful tones. And then, suddenly realizing the admission she had made, and seeing how she had revealed her own secret she flew into a rage.
“What do you *mean*?” she cried. “I didn’t refer to Mr. Carleton.”
“Yes, you did,” said Fessenden, so quietly that again Cicely was silent, and Kitty sat surprised almost to breathlessness.
“There is to be only truth between us,” went on Rob. “You did mean Mr. Carleton, by the letter *S*; but have no fear, your secret shall be respected. Now we will have only the truth﻿—remember that. So please tell me frankly at what time you saw Mr. Carleton come into the house last night?”
===Chinese===
这想必是费森登自有慑人的魅力，或者说，一种叫人无从抗拒的同情心使然；总之不知怎的，西塞莉听了这话竟未见怪，只简单地答了声：“是。”
“奇怪，”罗布沉吟道，“那个人怎么竟赢得了这么多女人的心。”
“不，这一点也不奇怪，”西塞莉说，语调同样缓慢而若有所思。随即她猛然醒悟自己招认了什么，看清自己如何把心底的秘密抖搂了出来，顿时勃然大怒。
“您这话是什么意思？”她嚷道，“我说的可不是卡尔顿先生。”
“您说的就是他，”费森登说，语气平静之至，西塞莉竟又哑口无言，基蒂坐在一旁，惊得几乎透不过气来。
“你我之间，往后只有真话，”罗布接着说，“你写那个字母S，指的确是卡尔顿先生；不过你别怕，你的秘密自会受人尊重。现在我们只要真话——记住这一点。那就请坦白告诉我，昨夜你看见卡尔顿先生是几时进宅子的？”

===Original===
“Just a few moments before half-past eleven.” Cicely said this glibly, as if reciting a carefully-conned lesson.
“Wait a moment﻿—you forget that Mr. Hunt fixed the time at quarter after eleven, and that he saw you looking over the baluster at the same time.”
With an agonized cry of dismay, Miss Dupuy fainted into utter unconsciousness.
Perplexed and baffled in his inquiries, Fessenden saw that for the moment Miss Dupuy’s physical condition was of paramount importance, and at Kitty’s request he rang for Marie. Even before she came the others had placed Cicely gently on a couch, and when the maid arrived Fessenden left the room, knowing that the girl was properly cared for.
===Chinese===
“就在十一点半前不多一会儿。”西塞莉答得溜熟，仿佛在背一课温得烂熟的功课。
“且慢——你忘了，亨特先生把时刻定在十一点一刻，还说就在那时，他瞧见你正凭着楼梯栏杆往下张望。”
杜皮伊小姐发出一声痛楚绝望的惊呼，登时昏厥过去，全然不省人事。
问讯受挫、一时无计可施的费森登看得分明，眼下最要紧的，是杜皮伊小姐的身体。应基蒂之请，他按铃唤玛丽来。不等她到，旁人已把西塞莉轻轻安置在一张躺椅上；女仆一到，费森登便退出房去，知道这姑娘已有人妥为照看。

===Original===
Going downstairs again, he was about to make his adieux to Mrs. Markham and leave the house, when Kitty French, coming down soon after him, asked him to stay a few minutes longer.
The sight of her pretty face drove more serious thoughts from his mind, and he turned, more than willing to follow where she led. “Oh, whistle, and I’ll come to you,” he whispered. But Kitty had weighty information to impart, and was in no mood for trifling. They found a quiet corner, and then Kitty told him that Cicely had regained consciousness almost immediately, but that just before she did so, she cried out sharply, “They must not think Schuyler did it! They must not!”
“And so,” said Kitty, astutely, “you see, it’s as I told you. Mr. Carleton *did* kill Maddy, and Cicely knows it, but she doesn’t want other people to find it out, because she’s in love with him herself!”
Rob Fessenden gave his companion an admiring glance.
“That’s good reasoning and sound logic,” he said; “and I’d subscribe to it if it were anybody but old Schuyler. But I can’t and won’t believe that man guilty without further evidence than that of a fainting, hysterical woman.”
“Everybody seems to be in love with Mr. Carleton,” said Kitty, demurely.
===Chinese===
他重新下楼，正要向马卡姆太太作别出宅，基蒂·弗伦奇紧随着也下了楼，请他再多留几分钟。
一见她那张俏脸，更严肃的念头顿时都被逐出脑海，他转过身去，她引到哪里，他便巴不得跟到哪里。“哦，吹一声口哨，我就来到你身旁。”他悄声道。可基蒂身负要讯，全无打趣的兴致。两人寻了个清静角落，基蒂这才告诉他：西塞莉几乎当即便恢复了知觉，只是恰在醒转之前，她尖声喊出：“千万别让他们以为是斯凯勒干的！千万不能！”
“这么一来，”基蒂伶俐地说，“你瞧，正像我说的吧。杀死玛迪的就是卡尔顿先生，西塞莉心知肚明，只是不愿让别人瞧破，因为她自己爱上他了！”
罗布·费森登向他的同伴投去赞佩的一瞥。
“这推论漂亮，逻辑也站得住，”他说，“若换作旁人，而不是老斯凯勒，我准会照单全收。可要信那人有罪，光凭一个动辄晕厥的歇斯底里女人的话，再无别的凭据，我不能信，也决不肯信。”
“瞧来人人都在恋着卡尔顿先生呢。”基蒂假作端庄地说。

===Original===
“You’re not, are you?” said Rob, so quickly that Kitty blushed.
“No, I’m not,” she declared. “He’s a stunning-looking man, and that superior, impassive way of his catches some women, but I don’t care for it. I prefer a more enthusiastic temperament.”
“Like mine,” said Rob casually.
“Have you a temperament?” said Kitty saucily. “It isn’t at all noticeable.”
“It will be, after you know me better. But Miss French, since you’ve raised this question of Miss Dupuy’s evidence, let me tell you what it means to me. Or, rather, what it seems to point to, for it’s all too vague for us to draw any real conclusions. But, as a first impression, my suspicion turns toward Miss Dupuy herself rather than Carleton.”
“Cicely! You don’t mean *she* killed Maddy! Oh, how *can* you?”
===Chinese===
“那你呢，你可不在其列吧？”罗布脱口便问，快得叫基蒂红了脸。
“没有，我才没有，”她一口声明，“他相貌确实英俊夺人，那副居高临下、冷淡无波的做派，也迷得住有些女人，可我不爱这一路。我更喜欢热忱些的性子。”
“比方说，像我这样的。”罗布随口道。
“你也算有性子么？”基蒂娇俏地说，“怎么一点儿也看不出来。”
“等你同我处熟了，就看得出来了。不过，弗伦奇小姐，你既然提起了杜皮伊小姐证词这一节，我不妨告诉你，它在我听来意味着什么。或者不如说，它似乎指向什么——眼下一切都太渺茫，还下不得真结论。只是作为第一印象，我的疑心与其转向卡尔顿，不如说转向了杜皮伊小姐本人。”
“西塞莉！你不会是说，杀死玛迪的是她吧！哎呀，你怎么能这样！”

===Original===
“Now, don’t fly into hysterics yourself. Wait a minute. I haven’t accused her at all. But look at it. Miss Van Norman was certainly killed by Carleton, *or* by someone already in the house. It has been proved that nobody outside could get in. Now if the criminal *is* someone in the house, we must consider each one in turn. And if by chance we consider Miss Dupuy first, we must admit a motive.”
“What motive?”
“Why, that of a jealous woman. Miss Van Norman was just about to marry the man Miss Dupuy is in love with. Perhaps﻿—do have patience, I’m merely supposing﻿—perhaps she has vainly urged Miss Van Norman to give him up, and, finding she wouldn’t do so, at the last minute she prevented the marriage herself﻿—putting that paper on the table to make it appear a suicide. This would explain her stealthy attempt to regain possession of the paper later.”
“Why should she want it?”
“So that it couldn’t be *proved* not to be in Miss Van Norman’s writing.”
“It’s ingenious on your part,” said Kitty slowly, “but it can’t be true. Cicely may be in love with Schuyler, but she wouldn’t kill Maddy because of that.”
===Chinese===
“好了，你自己可别也犯起歇斯底里。稍待片刻。我还根本没有指认她。你且这么看：范·诺曼小姐不是死于卡尔顿之手，便是死于宅中本就在场的某人之手。外人进不了这宅子，这一点已经查明。倘若凶手当真是宅中之人，我们就须逐个儿考量。而倘若我们碰巧先考量杜皮伊小姐，那就得承认，她是有动机的。”
“什么动机？”
“嗐，吃醋女人的动机呗。范·诺曼小姐眼看就要嫁给杜皮伊小姐的意中人了。也许——千万耐住性子，我不过是姑妄设之——也许她曾劝范·诺曼小姐把那人让出来，却是徒劳；眼见劝不动，便在最后关头亲自断了这桩婚事——把那张纸放在桌上，装出自杀的样子。这样一来，她后来鬼鬼祟祟想把那张纸弄回手中，也就有了说明。”
“她要那纸何用？”
“为的是叫人再无法证明那不是范·诺曼小姐的笔迹。”
“你这构想真是巧妙，”基蒂缓缓地说，“可它不可能是真的。西塞莉兴许爱着斯凯勒，可她断不会为这个去杀玛迪。”

===Original===
“Who can tell what a hysterical, jealous woman will do?” said Rob, with the air of an oracle. “And moreover, to my mind, that explains her half-conscious exclamation of which you just told me. When she said, ‘They must not think Schuyler did it,’ it meant that she knew he didn’t do it, but she didn’t want suspicion to rest on him. That’s why she insists it was a suicide.”
So in earnest was Fessenden that Kitty felt almost convinced there was something in his theory.
“But it can’t be,” she said, at last, with an air of finality. “It wouldn’t be *possible* for Cicely to do such a thing! I know *her* too well!”
“Then, Miss French, if that, to you, is a logical argument, you must admit mine. It wouldn’t be *possible* for Carleton to do such a thing! I know *him* too well!”
Kitty had to smile at the imitation of the strong inflections she had used, and, too, she had to admit that one opinion was as permissible as the other.
“You see,” went on Rob quietly, “we’re not really assuming Miss Dupuy’s guilt, we’re only seeing where these deductions lead us. Suppose, for the moment, that Miss Dupuy did, during that half-hour in the library, have an altercation with Miss Van Norman, and just suppose﻿—or imagine, if you prefer the word﻿—that she turned the dagger upon her friend and employer, wouldn’t her subsequent acts have been just as they were? At Mr. Carleton’s alarm, she came downstairs, fully dressed; later she tried to remove secretly that written paper; always at serious questioning she faints or flies into hysterics; and, naturally, when suspicion comes near the man she cares for, she tries to turn it off. And then, too, Miss French, a very strong point against her is that she was the last one, so far as we know, to see Miss Van Norman alive. Of course, the murderer was the last one; but I mean, of the witnesses, Miss Dupuy was the latest known to be with Miss Van Norman. Thus, her evidence cannot be corroborated, and it may or may not be true. If she is the guilty one, we cannot expect the truth from her, and so we must at least admit that there is room for investigation, if not suspicion.”
===Chinese===
“谁说得准，一个歇斯底里、妒火中烧的女人会干出什么来？”罗布说，那口气俨然神谕。“再者，依我看，这恰好解释了你方才说起的她半昏迷中的那声呼喊。她说‘千万别让他们以为是斯凯勒干的’，意思便是：她明知不是他干的，只是不愿嫌疑落在他身上。所以她才一口咬定，那是自杀。”
费森登说得这般恳切，基蒂几乎觉得，他的理论真有几分道理了。
“可这不可能，”她终于开口，一锤定音似的说，“西塞莉决计干不出这种事！我对她知根知底！”
“那么，弗伦奇小姐，倘若这在你看来算得上一条合乎逻辑的论证，你也得承认我这一条：卡尔顿决计干不出这种事！我对他知根知底！”
自己方才那斩钉截铁的重音，被他原样学了一遍，基蒂不由得笑了，也不得不承认，这一种见解与那一种见解，原是同样站得住的。
“你要知道，”罗布平静地接着说，“我们并不是真个认定杜皮伊小姐有罪，不过是瞧瞧这些推论会把我们引到哪里去。姑且假定，在那书房里的半个钟头中，杜皮伊小姐确曾同范·诺曼小姐起了一场争执；再姑且假定——或者随你挑个字眼，想象也成——她把匕首刺向了自己的朋友兼主人，那么她事后的一举一动，岂不正会同实际的一模一样？卡尔顿先生一喊，她衣裳齐整地下楼来；随后她偷偷想把那张字条弄走；每逢问到要紧处，她不是晕倒便是歇斯底里大发作；待到嫌疑逼近她的意中人，她自然要设法引开。再者，弗伦奇小姐，对她很不利的一点是：就我们所知，她是最后一个见到活着的范·诺曼小姐的人。当然，真说起来，凶手才是最后见到的那个；可我的意思是，在众证人之中，已知同范·诺曼小姐在一起最晚的，便是杜皮伊小姐。如此一来，她的证词无从对证，真假难辨。她若果真是凶手，我们便休想从她嘴里听到真话；所以至少我们得承认：纵然还说不上嫌疑，调查的余地总是有的。”

===Original===
“I suppose you are right,” said Kitty slowly; “a man’s mind is said to be more logical. A woman depends more on her intuition. Now, my intuition tells me that Cicely Dupuy can *not* be the guilty one.”
“At risk of tiresome repetition,” returned Fessenden, “I must say again that that is no more convincing than *my* ‘intuition’ that Carleton can *not* be the guilty one.”
Kitty’s smile showed her quick appreciation of this point, and Rob went on:
“Though suspicion, so far, is cast in no other direction, it is only fair to consider all the others in the house. This will, of course, be done in due time. I approve of Mr. Benson, and I think, though his manners are pompous and at times egotistical, he has a good mind and a quick intelligence. He will do his part, I am sure, and then, if necessary, others will be brought into the case. But, as Carleton’s friend, I shall devote all my energies to clearing him from what I know is an unjust suspicion.”
===Chinese===
“我想你是对的，”基蒂缓缓说道，“都说男人的头脑更长于逻辑，女人则更多倚仗直觉。而我的直觉告诉我，西塞莉·杜皮伊决不会是凶手。”
“恕我鹦鹉学舌、不怕惹厌，再说一遍，”费森登答道，“这话并不比我的‘直觉’——卡尔顿决不会是凶手——更有说服力。”
基蒂莞尔一笑，可见她立时领会了这一层，罗布接着往下说：
“虽然迄今为止，嫌疑还没有指向别的方向，但把宅中其余诸人一一考量到，方才算公平。这自然会在适当的时候办到。我对本森先生是赞许的；我想，他派头固然浮夸，有时还自负得可以，头脑却好，心思也敏锐。他的本分他自会尽到，我深信不疑；往后若有必要，还会有旁人插手此案。但作为卡尔顿的朋友，我必将竭尽全部心力，为他洗刷我深知并不公正的嫌疑。”

===Original===
And then Rob Fessenden went away. Mrs. Markham asked him to remain to dinner, but he declined, preferring to go home with Carleton. He said he would return next morning, and said too that he meant to stay in Mapleton as long as he could be of any service to any of his friends.
This decision was, of course, the result of his great friendship for Carleton, and his general interest in the Van Norman case, but it was also partly brought about by the bewitching personality of Kitty French and the impression she had made on his not usually susceptible heart.
And being master of his own time, Fessenden resolved to stay for a few days and observe developments along several lines.
===Chinese===
随后罗布·费森登便告辞去了。马卡姆太太留他吃晚饭，他辞谢了，情愿随卡尔顿一同回去。他说次日早晨还要再来，又说只要对哪位朋友还有一分用处，他便打算在梅普尔顿住下去。
这一决定，固然缘于他对卡尔顿的深挚友情，以及对范·诺曼一案的全部关切，却也有几分是拜基蒂·弗伦奇那迷人的性情风姿所赐——她已在他那颗素来不轻动的心上，留下了印象。
而费森登的时间又全归自己做主，他便决意盘桓数日，从好几条线上观察事态的发展。

## XIV The Carleton Household / 十四　卡尔顿家

===Original===
Mrs. Carleton’s dinner table that evening presented a very different atmosphere from the night before.

The hostess herself was present only by a strong effort of will power. Mrs. Carleton had been greatly overcome by the shock of the dreadful news, and, aside from the sadness and horror of the tragedy, she was exceedingly disappointed at what seemed to her the ruin of her son’s future.

The Carletons were an old and aristocratic family, though by no means possessed of great fortune.

The alliance, therefore, with the wealth of the Van Norman estate, and the power of the Van Norman name, seemed to Mrs. Carleton the crowning glory of her son’s career, and she had been devoutly thankful when the wedding-day was set.

Though stubbornly unwilling to believe it, she had of late been forced to notice the growing attachment between Schuyler and her own companion, Miss Burt, and had it not been for the surety of the approaching wedding, she would have dismissed the girl. But so certain was she that her son’s ambitions, like her own, were centred on the Van Norman name, she could not believe that Schuyler would let himself become greatly interested in Dorothy Burt.

===Chinese===
这天晚上，卡尔顿太太的餐桌气氛，与前一夜已是大不相同。

女主人之所以还能到场，全凭一股坚强的意志强撑着。那可怕的消息给卡尔顿太太的打击极重；撇开这桩惨剧的悲哀与骇怖不谈，在她看来，儿子的前程就此毁于一旦，这尤其令她失望之至。

卡尔顿家是古老的名门望族，只是决算不上家资豪富。

因此，同范·诺曼家产业的财富、范·诺曼姓氏的威势结亲，在卡尔顿太太看来，便是儿子前程上无上的荣耀；婚期定下之时，她曾由衷地感激不尽。

她执拗地不肯相信，近来却不得不注意到，斯凯勒同她自己的女伴伯特小姐之间情愫日增；若不是婚期已是指日可待、板上钉钉，她早就把那姑娘辞退了。只是她认定，儿子的抱负同她自己的心愿一样，都系在范·诺曼这个姓氏上，所以她无法相信，斯凯勒会任凭自己对多萝西·伯特动起真情。

===Original===
But she did not allow for that mischievous Imp of Romance who plays havoc with hearts without saying “by your leave.”

And partly because of her own dainty charm, partly because of her contrast to Madeleine’s magnificence, Dorothy Burt crept into Schuyler Carleton’s affections before either of them realized it, and when they did discover the surprising fact, it did not seem to dismay them as it should have done.

But it troubled them; for Schuyler well knew that honor, expediency, and good judgment all held him bound to Miss Van Norman, and Dorothy Burt knew it equally well.

And, whether or not with an ulterior motive, she had made no claim on him from the first. She had admitted her love for him, but in the same breath had avowed her appreciation of its hopelessness. Even if he hinted at a possible transfer of his allegiance, she had hushed him at once, saying it was impossible for him to do otherwise than to be true to his troth, and that he must forget her, as she should﻿—try to﻿—forget him.

===Chinese===
可她千算万算，偏偏漏算了那个促狭的‘浪漫小鬼’——它搅乱起人的心来，是从不先问一声‘可否’的。

一半缘于她自身娇俏可人的风致，一半缘于她同马德琳的雍容华贵恰成对照，多萝西·伯特就这样悄悄潜入了斯凯勒·卡尔顿的心，当初两人竟都不曾察觉；等到发觉这桩出人意料的事实，他们又并未像理所应当的那样惊惶失措。

只是这事终究令他们苦恼；因为斯凯勒深知，道义、利害与见识全都把他系在范·诺曼小姐身上，而多萝西·伯特对此同样一清二楚。

而且，无论是否别有用心，她从一开始就不曾向他有过半点索取。她承认自己爱他，可话音未落又坦言，深知此事无望。即便他隐约吐露移情之意，她也立刻拦住，说他除了恪守婚约之外别无他路，说他必须忘掉她，正如她自己也该——尽力——忘掉他一样。

===Original===
This nobility on her part only made Carleton love her more, and though continuing to admire his beautiful fiancée, his real affection was all for little Dorothy.

She came to dinner that night, soft and lovely in a simple white frock, her pathetic eyes wide open in grief and sorrow, her rosebud mouth drooping and tremulous at the corners.

Fessenden watched her. Without appearing to do so, he noted every expression that flitted across her baby face.

And he was greatly disturbed.

===Chinese===
她这份高尚的心肠，反倒叫卡尔顿爱她更深。他对美丽的未婚妻依旧倾慕，可他真正的深情，尽数系在小多萝西身上。

当晚她来用晚餐，一袭素净的白色连衣裙，温婉可人；一双哀婉的眼睛悲戚地睁着，玫瑰花蕾般的小嘴低垂着，嘴角微微颤动。

费森登望着她。他不动声色，却把掠过她那张娃娃脸的每一种神情都看在眼里。

而他心里大为不安。

===Original===
The night before he had paid slight attention to her. To be sure, Miss Van Norman had spoken her name in the afternoon, but it had meant little to him, and, thinking of her merely as Mrs. Carleton’s companion, or secretary, he wasn’t sure which, he had been conventionally polite and no more. But tonight she was a factor in the case, and must be reckoned with.

As Fessenden watched her, he saw, with a growing conviction, as sure as it was awful, that she was relieved at Miss Van Norman’s death.

Gentle, tender little girl as she seemed, it was nevertheless true that the removal of the obstacle between Carleton and herself gave her only joy. She tried to hide this. She cleverly simulated grief, horror, surprise, interest﻿—all the emotions called forth by the conversation, which unavoidably pursued only one course. In fact, Miss Burt took her cue every time from Mrs. Carleton, and expressed opinions that invariably coincided with hers.

It began to dawn upon Fessenden that the girl was unusually clever, the more so, he thought, that she was consciously concealing her cleverness by a cloak of demure innocence, and careful unostentation. Never did she put herself forward; never did she show undue interest in Schuyler, personally.

===Chinese===
前一夜，他几乎没怎么留意过她。诚然，范·诺曼小姐下午提起过她的名字，可那对他意义寥寥；他只当她不过是卡尔顿太太身边的女伴，或是秘书——究竟是哪个他也说不清——对她照例客气几句而已。可到了今晚，她已是案中一个要素，不能不加以掂量。

费森登望着她，心里生出一个越来越确凿、却也越发可怖的确信：范·诺曼小姐死了，她如释重负。

别看她似乎是个温柔娇怯的小姑娘，横在卡尔顿与自己之间的障碍一旦除去，她心中确乎只有欢喜——这毕竟是实情。她极力把这份欢喜掩藏起来。她机灵地扮出悲恸、骇然、惊讶、关切——凡席间交谈引出的种种情绪，她一一装得逼真；而那交谈，绕来绕去又只能是同一桩事。事实上，伯特小姐每次都看卡尔顿太太的眼色行事，发表的意见无一不与太太如出一辙。

费森登渐渐省悟，这姑娘聪明得异乎寻常；更叫他这么想的，是她分明有意用娴静天真的外衣、处处收敛的不事张扬，把自己的聪明掩起来。她从不抢着出风头；对斯凯勒本人，也从不显出过分的关切。

===Original===
Fessenden reasoned that the game being now in her own hands, she could afford to stand back and await developments.

Then came the next thought: how came the game so fortuitously into her own hands? Was it, even indirectly, due to her own instigation?

“Pshaw!” he thought to himself. “I’m growing absurdly suspicious. I won’t believe wrong of that girl until I have some scrap of a hint to base it on.”

And yet he knew in his own heart if Dorothy Burt had wanted to connive in the slightest degree in the removal of her rival, she was quite capable of doing so, notwithstanding her very evident effect of pretty helplessness.

===Chinese===
费森登推想，如今这局牌已攥在她自己手里，她尽可退后一步，静待事态发展。

紧跟着又来一个念头：这局牌怎会这般凑巧地落入她手中？这里头会不会——哪怕只是间接地——也有她自己的挑动？

“啐！”他暗自想道，“我这疑心病滋长得太可笑了。没有一星半点的凭据，我决不往坏处想这位姑娘。”

然而他心知肚明：倘若多萝西·伯特当真想在她情敌的消失上勾连一丝一毫，她是全然做得出来的——任凭她那副娇柔无助的模样装得多么到家。

===Original===
“When an excessively clever young woman assumes an utterly inefficient air,” he thought, “it must be for some undeclared purpose;” and he felt an absurd thrill of satisfaction that though Kitty French was undeniably clever, she put on no ingénue arts to hide it.

Then Kitty’s phrase of “a clinging rosebud” came to his mind, and he realized its exceeding aptness to describe Dorothy Burt. Her appealing eyes and wistful, curved mouth were enough to lure a man who loved her to almost any deed of daring.

“Even murder?” flashed into his brain, and he recoiled at the thought. Old Schuyler might have been made to forget his fealty; he might have been unable to steel his heart against those subtle charms; he might have thrown to the winds his honor and his faith; but surely, never, *never*, could he have committed that dreadful deed, even for love of this angel-faced siren.

“Could she?”

The words fairly burned into Fessenden’s brain. The sudden thought set his mind whirling. *Could* she? Why, no, of course not! Absurd! Yes, but *could* she? What? That child? That baby-girl? Those tiny, rose-leaf hands! Yes, but *could* she?

===Chinese===
“一个聪明过人的年轻女子，若装出一副全然不中用的样子，”他想，“背后必定藏着不便明说的目的。”想到基蒂·弗伦奇虽聪明得无可否认，却从不摆出天真少女的做派来遮掩，他心里掠过一阵莫名的小小快意。

接着，基蒂那句‘缠人的玫瑰花蕾’浮上他的心头，他这才发觉，这话用来形容多萝西·伯特再贴切不过。那双楚楚动人的眼睛，那张哀恳似的弯弯小嘴，足以诱使爱她的男人去干几乎任何胆大妄为的事。

“连谋杀也肯？”这念头电光石火般掠过脑际，他不由得悚然一缩。老斯凯勒或许会被弄得忘了自己的誓约；或许抵不住那种种幽微的魅力，硬不起心肠；或许会把名誉与信义一概抛向九霄云外；可是那桩可怕的罪行，他必定是决计、决计干不出来的，纵然是为了爱这位生着天使面孔的海妖。

“她干得出来么？”

这几个字简直烙进了费森登的脑子。这突如其来的念头搅得他心念翻腾。她干得出来么？嗐，当然干不出来！荒唐！是啊，可她到底干不干得出来？什么？那孩子？那个娃娃似的小丫头？那双玫瑰花瓣般的小手！是啊，可她到底干不干得出来？

===Original===
“No!” said Fessenden angrily, and then realized that he had spoken aloud, and his hearers were looking at him with indulgent curiosity.

“Forgive me,” he said, smiling as he looked at Mrs. Carleton. “My fancy took a short but distant flight, and I had to speak to it sternly by way of reproof.”

“I didn’t know a lawyer could be fanciful,” said Mrs. Carleton. “I thought that privilege was reserved for poets.”

“Thank you for a pretty compliment to our profession,” said Rob. “We lawyers are too often accused of giving rein to our fancy, when we should be strapped to the saddle of slow but sure Truth.”

“But can you arrive anywhere on such a prosaic steed?” asked Miss Burt, smiling at his words.

“Yes,” said Rob; “we can arrive at facts.”

===Chinese===
“不！”费森登怒声说道，随即察觉自己竟出了声，席间众人正带着宽容的好奇望着他。

“恕我失礼，”他望着卡尔顿太太，含笑说道，“我的遐想方才飞出去一小会儿，却飞得老远，我不得不厉声呵斥它一句。”

“我还不知道律师也会想入非非呢，”卡尔顿太太说，“我原以为这份特权是留给诗人的。”

“多谢您对我们这一行的妙赞，”罗布说，“人家动辄责怪我们放任想象、信马由缰，其实我们本该把自己牢牢捆在真理的马鞍上——那匹马虽慢，却稳当可靠。”

“可骑着这样一匹乏味的驽马，你们还到得了什么地方么？”伯特小姐听得莞尔，问道。

“到得了，”罗布说，“我们能抵达事实。”

===Original===
What prompted him to speak so curtly, he didn’t know; but his speech did not at all please Miss Burt. Her color flew to her cheeks, though she said nothing, and then, as Mrs. Carleton rose from the table, the two ladies smiled and withdrew, leaving Rob alone with his host.

“It’s all right, old boy, of course,” said Carleton, “but did you have any reason for flouting poor little Dorothy like that?”

“No, I didn’t,” said Fessenden honestly and apologetically. “I spoke without thinking, and I’m sorry for it.”

“All right﻿—it’s nothing. Now, Rob, old fellow, you can’t deceive me. I saw a curious expression in your eyes as you looked at Miss Burt tonight, and﻿—well, there is no need of words between us, so I’ll only tell you you’re all wrong there. You look for hidden meanings and veiled allusions in everything that girl says, and there aren’t any. She’s as frank and open-natured as she can be, and﻿—forgive me﻿—but I want you to let her alone.”

Fessenden was astounded. First, at Carleton’s insight in discovering his thoughts, and second, at Carleton’s mistaken judgment of Miss Burt’s nature.

===Chinese===
是什么引得他说得这般生硬，他自己也不知道；可这话着实不中伯特小姐的意。她双颊倏地飞红，却一言不发；随后卡尔顿太太起身离席，两位女士含笑退出，留下罗布同男主人独对。

“自然没什么，老伙计，”卡尔顿说，“可你那样讥刺可怜的小多萝西，总有个缘由吧？”

“没有，”费森登诚恳而抱歉地说，“我那是随口而出，心里很过意不去。”

“行了——这不算什么。我说，罗布，老伙计，你是骗不过我的。今晚你望着伯特小姐时，眼里有一种说不出的古怪神情，而且——唉，咱们之间用不着多话，我只需告诉你：在这一层上你全错了。那姑娘说的每一句话，你都要从中寻出暗藏的深意、含蓄的影射，其实一样也没有。她的性子再率真坦荡不过，所以——恕我直言——我要你别再去琢磨她。”

费森登愕然。一来惊于卡尔顿竟看穿了他的心思，二来惊于他对伯特小姐天性的判断错得这样离谱。

===Original===
But he only said, “All right, Schuyler; what you say, goes. Would you rather not talk at all about the Van Norman affair?” Fessenden spoke thus casually, for he felt sure it would make it easier for Carleton than if he betrayed a deeper interest.

“Oh, I don’t care. You know, of course, how deeply it affects me and my whole life. I know your sympathy and good-fellowship. There’s not much more to say, is there?”

“Why, yes, Carleton; there is. As your friend, and also in the interests of justice, I am more than anxious to discover the villain who did the horrid deed, and though the inquest people are doing all they can, I want to add my efforts to theirs, in hope of helping them﻿—and you.”

“Don’t bother about me, Rob. I don’t care if they never discover the culprit. Miss Van Norman is gone; it can’t restore her to life if they do learn who killed her.”

===Chinese===
但他只说：“好吧，斯凯勒，你说怎样就怎样。那么范·诺曼家这桩案子，你是不是索性不想谈？”费森登故意说得这般随口，因为他确信，比起显出更深切的关注来，这样说能让卡尔顿好受些。

“哦，我无所谓。这案子对我、对我一生的影响有多深，你自然知道。你的情义我也明白。此外还有什么可说的，不是么？”

“哪里，卡尔顿，还有呢。作为你的朋友，也为了正义，我急切地想查出干下这桩可怖罪行的恶棍；验尸审讯那边的人虽已竭尽所能，我仍想添上自己的一份力，盼着能帮衬他们——也帮衬你。”

“别为我操心了，罗布。他们就是永远查不出凶手，我也无所谓。范·诺曼小姐已经去了；就算查出是谁杀的她，也不能叫她起死回生。”

===Original===
Fessenden looked mystified.

“That’s strange talk, Schuyler﻿—but of course you’re fearfully upset, and I suppose just at first it isn’t surprising that you feel that way. But surely﻿—as man to man, now﻿—you want to find and punish the wretch that put an end to that beautiful young life.”

“Yes﻿—I suppose so;” Carleton spoke hesitatingly, and drew his hand across his brow in the same dazed way he did when in the witness box.

“You’re done up, old man, and I’m not going to bother you tonight. But I’m on the hunt, if you aren’t, and I’m going ahead on a few little trails, hoping they’ll lead to something of more importance. By the way, what *were* you doing in those few minutes last night between your entering the house and entering the library?”

===Chinese===
费森登一脸困惑。

“这话可就怪了，斯凯勒——不过你受的惊吓自然非同小可，我想起初有这样的心境，原也不足为奇。可是——咱们男人对男人，说句真心话——那个终结了那条美丽年轻生命的恶棍，你总想把他找出来、绳之以法吧。”

“是——大概吧。”卡尔顿说得迟疑，抬手抹过额角，那副茫然的神态，同他坐在证人席上时如出一辙。

“你已疲惫不堪，老兄，今晚我就不搅扰你了。不过，你不去追查，我去；我正循着几条小小的线索往下走，指望它们能引出更要紧的东西来。对了，昨晚你进宅之后、进书房之前，那几分钟里你到底在做什么？”

===Original===
Carleton stared at his guest.

“I don’t know what you mean,” he said.

“Yes, you do. You went in at eleven-fifteen, and you called for help at eleven-thirty.”

“No﻿—it didn’t take as long as that.” Carleton’s eyes had a faraway look, and Rob grasped his arm and shook him, as he said:

===Chinese===
卡尔顿瞪着他的客人。

“我不明白你的意思。”他说。

“你明白。你是十一点一刻进的宅，十一点半喊的人。”

“不——用不了那么久。”卡尔顿的眼神又飘远了，罗布一把抓住他的胳膊摇了摇，说道：

===Original===
“Drop it, man! Drop that half-dazed way of speaking! Tell me, clearly, what did you do in that short interval?”

“I refuse to state,” said Carleton quietly, but with a direct glance now that made Fessenden cease his insistence.

“Very well,” he said; “it’s of no consequence. Now tell me what you were doing last evening before you went over to the house?”

===Chinese===
“别来这套，老兄！收起那半是茫然的说话腔调！明明白白告诉我，那短短一阵子你干了些什么？”

“我拒绝回答。”卡尔顿平静地说，只是这一回他目光直视过来，倒教费森登就此住口，不再追问。

“很好，”他说，“这事无关紧要。那么请告诉我，昨晚你去那宅子之前，都在做些什么？”

===Original===
At this Carleton showed a disposition to be both haughty and ironical.

“Am I being questioned,” he said, “and by you? Well, before I went to Miss Van Norman’s I was walking in the rose-garden with Miss Burt. You saw me from your window.”

“I did,” said Rob gravely. “Were you with Miss Burt until the time of your going over to the Van Norman house?”

“No,” said Carleton, with sarcastic intonation. “I said good night to Miss Burt about three-quarters of an hour before I started to go over to Miss Van Norman’s. Do you want to know what I did during *that* interval?”

===Chinese===
听了这话，卡尔顿露出一副既傲慢又讥诮的神气。

“我这是在受讯问么，”他说，“而且还是受你的讯问？好吧，去范·诺曼小姐那儿之前，我同伯特小姐在玫瑰园里散步来着。你在窗口都看见了。”

“看见了。”罗布正色道，“你同伯特小姐一直待在一起，直到动身去范·诺曼宅的时候么？”

“没有，”卡尔顿应道，语调里带着讥讽，“我动身去范·诺曼小姐家之前大约三刻钟，就同伯特小姐道过晚安了。那段时间里我干了什么，你也想知道么？”

===Original===
“Yes.”

“I was in my own room﻿—my den. I did what many a man does on the eve of his wedding. I burned up a few notes﻿—perhaps a photograph or two﻿—and one withered rosebud﻿—a ‘keepsake.’ Does this interest you?”

“Not especially, but, Schuyler, do drop that resentful air. I’m not quizzing you, and if you don’t want to talk about the subject at all, we won’t.”

“Very well﻿—I don’t.”

“Very well, then.”

===Chinese===
“想。”

“我在自己的房间里——我的私室。我做了许多男人在婚礼前夜都会做的事。我烧掉了几纸短笺——也许还有一两张照片——还有一朵枯萎的玫瑰花蕾——一件‘纪念物’。这你有兴趣么？”

“谈不上；不过，斯凯勒，快把那副怨气冲冲的神气收起来吧。我不是在盘问你，这个话题你若压根儿不愿谈，咱们就不谈。”

“很好——我不愿谈。”

“那就不谈。”

===Original===
The two men rose, and as Carleton held out his hand Rob grasped it and shook it heartily, then they went to the drawing-room and rejoined the ladies.

The Van Norman affair was not mentioned again that evening.

All felt a certain oppression in the atmosphere, and all tried to dispel it, but it was not easy. Uninteresting topics of conversation were tossed from one to another, but each felt relieved when at last Mrs. Carleton rose to go upstairs and the evening was at an end.

===Chinese===
两人起身。卡尔顿伸出手来，罗布紧紧握住，热诚地摇了摇；随后两人走进客厅，回到女士们中间。

那天晚上，范·诺曼家的案子再没有人提起。

人人都觉出空气里有一种说不出的沉郁，也都想把它驱散，却谈何容易。一个个索然无味的话题被你一言我一语地抛来抛去；直到卡尔顿太太终于起身要上楼去、这一晚就此收场，人人方才松了一口气。

===Original===
Fessenden went to his room, his brain a whirlwind of conflicting thoughts.

He sat down by an open window and endeavored to classify them into some sort of order.

First, he was annoyed at Carleton’s inexplicable attitude. Granting he was in love with Miss Burt, he had no reason to act so unconcerned about the Van Norman tragedy. And yet Schuyler’s was a peculiar nature, and doubtless all this strange behavior of his was merely the effort to hide his real sorrow.

But again, if he were in love with Miss Burt, his sorrow for the loss of Madeleine was for the loss of her fortune and not herself. This Fessenden refused to believe, but the more he refused to believe it, the more it came back to him. Then there was his new notion, that came to him at dinner, about Miss Burt. Carleton said she was the ingenuous, timid girl she looked, but Rob couldn’t believe it. Executive ability showed in that determined little chin. Veiled cunning lurked in the shadows of those innocent eyes. And the girl had a motive. Surely she wanted her rival out of her way. Then she had said good night to Schuyler nearly an hour before he went over to Madeleine’s. Could she have﻿—but, nonsense! Even if she had been so inclined, how could she have entered the house? Ah, that settled it! She couldn’t. And Fessenden was honestly glad of it. Honestly glad that he had proved to himself that Miss Burt﻿—lovely, alluring little Dorothy Burt﻿—was not the hardened criminal for whom he was looking!

===Chinese===
费森登回到自己房里，脑中思绪纷乱冲撞，如同一阵旋风。

他在敞开的窗前坐下，想把它们理出个条理来。

首先，卡尔顿那副无从索解的态度令他恼火。就算他爱上了伯特小姐，也犯不着对范·诺曼家的惨剧这般漠然。然而斯凯勒的性子本就古怪，他这种种反常的做派，多半不过是想遮掩心底真正的哀伤。

可是话又说回来，倘若他真爱上了伯特小姐，那么他为失去马德琳而哀恸的，便是失去了她的财产，而不是她本人。这个念头费森登不肯相信；可他越不肯信，它越是萦回不去。再者，便是晚餐时生出的、关于伯特小姐的那番新想头。卡尔顿说她就是外表看去那样天真胆怯的姑娘，罗布却无法相信。那小巧而坚毅的下巴上，透着办事的干练；那双无辜眼睛的阴影里，潜藏着遮掩的狡黠。何况这姑娘有动机。她自然巴不得情敌让路。还有，斯凯勒去马德琳那边之前将近一个钟头，她就同他道了晚安。她会不会——罢了，胡思乱想！就算她存了这个心，她又怎么进得了那宅子？啊，这就结了！她进不去。费森登打心底里为此高兴。他打心底里高兴：他向自己证明了，伯特小姐——娇美动人的小多萝西·伯特——并不是他要寻找的那个心狠手辣的凶徒！

===Original===
Then it came back to Schuyler. No! Never Schuyler! But if not he, then who? And what was he doing in that incriminating interval, and why wouldn’t he tell?

And then, idly gazing from his window Rob saw again two figures walking in the rose-garden. And they were the same two that he had seen there the evening before.

Schuyler Carleton and Dorothy Burt were strolling﻿—no, now they were standing, standing close to each other in earnest conversation.

Rob was no eavesdropper, and of course he couldn’t hear a word they said, but somehow he found it impossible to take his eyes from those two figures.

Steadily they talked﻿—so engrossed in their conversation that they scarcely moved; then Schuyler’s arm went slowly round the girl’s shoulders.

===Chinese===
接着，思绪又绕回斯凯勒身上。不！决不会是斯凯勒！可若不是他，又能是谁？在那段足以定罪的间隙里，他到底在做什么，又为什么不肯说？

就在这时，罗布漫不经心地朝窗外望去，又看见两个人影在玫瑰园里走动。还是前一晚他在那儿见过的那两个。

斯凯勒·卡尔顿和多萝西·伯特本在缓步而行——不，此刻他们站住了，紧挨着站在一处，正谈得恳切。

罗布不是偷听墙根的人，他们说的话他自然一个字也听不见；可不知怎的，他就是没法把目光从那两个人影身上挪开。

两人絮絮地谈着——谈得那样专注，几乎一动不动；后来，斯凯勒的手臂慢慢拢上了姑娘的肩头。

===Original===
Gently she drew away, and he did not then again offer a caress.

Rob sat looking at them, saying frankly to himself that he was justified in doing so, since his motive effaced all consideration of puerile conventions. If that girl were really the designing young woman he took her to be﻿—more, if she could be the author, directly or indirectly, of that awful crime﻿—then Fessenden vowed he would save Schuyler from her fascinations at the risk of breaking their own lifelong friendship.

After further rapt and earnest conversation, Carleton took Miss Burt gently in his arms and kissed her lightly on the forehead. Then, drawing her arm through his own, they turned and walked slowly to the house.

A few moments later Rob heard the girl’s light footsteps as she came up to her room, but Carleton stayed down in the library until long after all the rest of the household were sleeping.

===Chinese===
她轻轻挣开去，他便没有再作亲近之举。

罗布坐在那里望着他们，坦然对自己说，他这样做是问心无愧的，因为他的动机压倒了一切对琐碎俗礼的顾虑。倘若那姑娘真是他认定的那种工于心计的女子——更进一步，倘若她可能直接或间接地是那桩可怖罪行的主使——那么费森登立誓，哪怕赔上两人一辈子的交情，他也要把斯凯勒从她的魅力中救出来。

又倾心恳谈了一阵之后，卡尔顿轻轻将伯特小姐揽入怀中，在她额上轻轻一吻。随后，他把她的小臂挽进自己臂弯，两人转身朝宅子缓缓走去。

片刻之后，罗布听见姑娘轻盈的脚步声上楼回房去了；卡尔顿却仍留在楼下的书房里，直到宅中其余的人尽皆入睡许久之后。

## XV Fessenden’s Detective Work / 十五　费森登的侦探功夫

===Original===
Next morning Rob went over to the Van Norman house with a clearly developed plan of action. He declared to himself that he would allow no circumstance to shake his faith in his friend, that he would hold Carleton innocent of all wrongdoing in the affair, and that he would put all his ingenuity and cleverness to work to discover the criminal or any clue that might lead to such a discovery.

Although some questions he had wished to ask Cicely Dupuy were yet unanswered, Fessenden had discovered several important facts, and, after being admitted to the house, he looked about him for a quiet spot to sit down and tabulate them in black and white. The florist’s men were still in the drawing-room, so he went into the library. Here he found only Mrs. Markham and Miss Morton, who were apparently discussing a question on which they held opposite opinions.

“Come in, Mr. Fessenden,” said Mrs. Markham, as he was about to withdraw. “I should be glad of your advice. Ought I to give over the reins of government at once to Miss Morton?”

“Why not?” interrupted Miss Morton, herself. “The house is mine; why should I not be mistress here?”

Fessenden repressed a smile. It seemed to him absurd that these two middle-aged women should discuss an issue of this sort with such precipitancy.

===Chinese===
次日清晨，罗布揣着一套已然成形的行动计划，来到范·诺曼宅邸。他暗自立誓：无论出现什么情由，都决不容它动摇自己对朋友的信任；在这桩案子里，他要认定卡尔顿绝无不端、清白无辜；他还要使出自己全部的机敏巧思，去查出真凶，或寻得任何足以引向这一发现的线索。

他原想向西塞莉·杜皮伊讨教的几个问题虽还悬而未答，费森登却已摸清了几桩要紧的事实。进得宅门之后，他环顾四周，想寻个清静去处坐下，把这些事实一条条白纸黑字列出来。花匠伙计们还在大客厅里忙活，他便走进了书房。在这里，他只见到马卡姆太太和莫顿小姐，两人显然正议论着一个各执一端的问题。

“请进吧，费森登先生。”见他正要退出去，马卡姆太太说道，“我很乐意听听您的高见。这当家的权柄，我是不是该即刻交到莫顿小姐手里？”

“为什么不呢？”莫顿小姐竟亲自抢白道，“宅子是我的；我为什么不该当这里的主妇？”

费森登忍住了笑。这两位中年妇人，竟以这般火急火燎的架势议论起这样一个问题，在他看来未免有些荒唐。

===Original===
“It seems to me a matter of good taste,” he replied. “The house, Miss Morton, is legally yours, but as its mistress, I think you’d show a more gracious manner if you would wait for a time before making any changes in the domestic arrangements.”

Apparently undesirous of pursuing the gracious course he recommended, Miss Morton rose abruptly and flounced out of the room.

“Now she’s annoyed again,” observed Mrs. Markham placidly. “The least little thing sets her off.”

“If not intrusive, Mrs. Markham, won’t you tell me how it comes about that Miss Morton inherits this beautiful house? Is she a relative of the Van Normans?”

===Chinese===
“依我看，这是个风度教养的问题。”他答道，“这座宅子，莫顿小姐，在法律上归您所有；不过说到做它的主妇，我想您若肯暂缓些时日，再更动家政上的种种安排，气度上会显得更雍容些。”

莫顿小姐显然无意循行他所举荐的那条雍容之道，猛地站起身，一甩身子冲出了房间。

“瞧，她又恼了。”马卡姆太太心平气和地说，“芝麻绿豆大点的事，就能把她点着。”

“倘若不算冒昧，马卡姆太太，您可愿意告诉我，莫顿小姐怎么会继承了这座漂亮的宅子？她是范·诺曼家的亲戚么？”

===Original===
“Not a bit of it. She was Richard Van Norman’s sweetheart, years and years and *years* ago. They had a falling-out, and neither of them ever married. Of course he didn’t leave her any of his fortune. But only a short time ago, long after her uncle’s death, Madeleine found out about it from some old letters. She determined then to hunt up this Miss Morton, and she did so, and they had quite a correspondence. She came here for the wedding, and Madeleine intended she should make a visit, and intended to give her a present of money when she went away. In the meantime Madeleine had made her will, though I didn’t know this until today, leaving the place and all her own money to Miss Morton. I’m not surprised at this, for Tom Willard has plenty, and as there was no other heir, I know Madeleine felt that part of her uncle’s fortune ought to be used to benefit the woman he had loved in his youth.”

“That explains Miss Morton, then,” said Fessenden. “But what a peculiar woman she is!”

“Yes, she is,” agreed Mrs. Markham, in her serene way. “But I’m used to queer people. Richard Van Norman used to give way to the most violent bursts of temper I ever saw. Maddy and Tom are just like him. They would both fly into furious rages, though I must say they didn’t do it often, and never unless for some deep reason.”

===Chinese===
“一点儿也不沾亲。她是理查德·范·诺曼的旧情人，那是好多年、好多年、好多年以前的事了。两人闹了一场别扭，从此谁也没有成家。当然，他一分家财也没有留给她。可就在不久之前——她叔叔去世很久之后——马德琳从几封旧信里得知了这段旧情。她那时便打定主意要寻访这位莫顿小姐，也当真寻着了，两人还通了好一阵子的信。她这次是来参加婚礼的，马德琳本有意留她小住，还打算她临走时送她一笔钱作礼。与此同时，马德琳立下了遗嘱——这事我也是今天才知道——把这座宅子和她自己所有的钱都留给了莫顿小姐。这我并不诧异，因为汤姆·威拉德家境殷实，而又没有别的继承人；我知道马德琳心里觉得，叔叔的家财理当分出一部分，用来周济他年轻时爱过的那个女子。”

“这么说，莫顿小姐这一层便解释明白了。”费森登说，“可这女人多么古怪啊！”

“可不是嘛。”马卡姆太太以她一贯恬静的神气附和道，“不过古怪人我见惯了。理查德·范·诺曼发起脾气来，那股暴风骤雨，是我平生仅见的。玛迪和汤姆活脱脱像他。两人都会怒火陡生、大发雷霆——不过我得说句公道话，他们轻易不发作，若非有甚深缘由，从不如此。”

===Original===
“And Mr. Carleton﻿—has he a high temper?”

Mrs. Markham’s brow clouded. “I don’t understand that man,” she said slowly. “I don’t think he has a quick temper, but there’s something deep about him that I can’t make out. Oh, Mr. Fessenden, do you think he killed our Madeleine?”

“Do you?” said Fessenden suddenly, looking straight at her.

“I do,” she said, taken off her guard. “That is, I couldn’t believe it, only, what else can I think? Mr. Carleton is a good man, but I know Maddy never killed herself, and I know the way this house is locked up every night. No burglar or evildoer could possibly get in.”

===Chinese===
“那么卡尔顿先生——他也是烈性子么？”

马卡姆太太的眉头罩上阴云。“这个人我捉摸不透。”她缓缓地说，“我想他不是个一点就着的性子，可他身上有一种深沉的东西，我怎么看也看不明白。唉，费森登先生，您说，害死咱们马德琳的，会是他么？”

“您说呢？”费森登突然发问，直直地望着她。

“我说是的。”她猝不及防，脱口答道，“这就是说，我本不肯相信，只是，不这样想，我还能怎样想呢？卡尔顿先生是好人，可我知道玛迪断断不是自杀，我也知道这座宅子每夜是怎样锁门闩户的。什么窃贼呀、歹徒呀，万万进不来。”

===Original===
“But the murderer may have been concealed in the house for hours beforehand.”

“Nonsense! That would be impossible, with a house so full of people, and the wedding preparations going on, and everything. Besides, Mr. Hunt would have heard any intruder prowling around; and then again, how could he have gone out? Everything was bolted on the inside, except the front door, and had he gone out that way he must surely have been heard.”

“Well reasoned, Mrs. Markham! I think, with you, we may dismiss the possibility of a burglar. The time was too short for anything except a definitely premeditated act. And yet I cannot believe the act was that of Schuyler Carleton. I know that man very well, and a truer, braver soul never existed.”

“I know it,” declared Mrs. Markham, “but I think I’m justified in telling you this. Mr. Carleton didn’t love Madeleine, and he did love another girl. Madeleine worshipped him, and I think he came last night to ask her to release him, and she refused, and then﻿—and then﻿—”

===Chinese===
“可凶手也许事先就在宅子里藏了好几个钟头呢。”

“胡说！那断不可能——满宅子都是人，婚礼又在张罗着，样样事都在忙。再说，真有歹人潜进来四处转悠，亨特先生还能听不见？退一步说，他又怎么出得去呢？除了正门，处处都从里面闩死了；他若从正门出去，断没有不叫人听见的道理。”

“推论得头头是道，马卡姆太太！我同您想的一样，窃贼作案的可能，咱们尽可以撇开了。案发前后那段时间太短，除了确经预谋的行为，容不下别的。然而我仍旧不能相信，这事会是斯凯勒·卡尔顿做的。那人我知之甚深，世上再没有比他更真诚、更勇敢的心了。”

“这我知道，”马卡姆太太正色说道，“可我想，这话我照实告诉您并不为过。卡尔顿先生并不爱马德琳，他爱的是另一个姑娘。马德琳把他奉若神明，我想他昨夜前来，是求她放他自由，她不肯，于是——于是——”

===Original===
Something about Mrs. Markham’s earnest face and sad, distressed voice affected Fessenden deeply, and he wondered if this theory she had so clearly, though hesitatingly, stated, could be the true one. Might he, after all, be mistaken in his estimate of Schuyler Carleton, and might Mrs. Markham’s suggestion have even a foundation of probability?

They were both silent for a few minutes, and then Mr. Fessenden said, “But you thought it was suicide at first.”

“Indeed I did; I looked at the paper through glasses that were dim with tears, and it looked to me like Madeleine’s writing. Of course Miss Morton also thought it was, as she was only slightly familiar with Maddy’s hand. But now that we know someone else wrote that message, of course we also know the dear girl did not bring about her own death.”

Mrs. Markham was called away on some household errands then, and Fessenden remained alone in the library, trying to think of some clue that would point to someone other than Carleton.

===Chinese===
马卡姆太太那副恳切的面容、那悲切沉痛的语声，深深触动了费森登。他不禁暗想：她这套推断虽说得迟疑，却又是那般分明，难道竟会是实情？莫非到头来，他对斯凯勒·卡尔顿的品评终究看走了眼？莫非马卡姆太太的这一揣想，竟也真有几分可能的根基？

两人都沉默了几分钟，随后费森登先生开口道：“可是起初，您原以为那是自杀。”

“我确实那样想过；当时我是透过噙满泪水而昏花模糊的眼镜，去瞧那张字条的，在我眼里，那笔迹就像出自马德琳之手。莫顿小姐自然也当它是的，因为她对玛迪的笔迹本就只识得个大概。可如今既然知道那字条是旁人写的，我们自然也就明白，那亲爱的姑娘并没有亲手了结自己的性命。”

这时马卡姆太太因几件家务琐事被人唤走了，费森登独自留在书房里，苦苦思索有没有哪条线索，能把嫌疑引向卡尔顿以外的人。

===Original===
“I’m sure that man is not a murderer,” he declared to himself. “Carleton is peculiar, but he has a loyal, honest heart. And yet, if not, who can have done the deed? I can’t seem to believe it really was either the Dupuy woman or the Burt girl. And I *know* it wasn’t Schuyler! There must have been some motive of which I know nothing. And perhaps I also know nothing of the murderer. It need not necessarily have been one of these people we have already questioned.” His thoughts strayed to the under-servants of the house, to common burglars, or to some powerful unknown villain. But always the thought returned that no one could have entered and left the house unobserved within that fatal hour.

And then, to his intense satisfaction, Kitty French came into the room.

“Good morning, Rose of Dawn,” he said, looking at her bright face. “Are you properly glad to see me?”

“Yes, kind sir,” she said, dropping a little curtsey, and smiling in a most friendly way.

===Chinese===
“我敢断定，那人绝不是凶手。”他暗自申言道，“卡尔顿固然古怪，可他有一颗忠义诚实的心。然而，倘若不是他，这桩事又是谁做下的呢？要说真是杜皮伊家那女人、或是伯特家那小姑娘干的，我怎么也没法相信。而我分明知道，那绝不是斯凯勒！这里面必有一个我全然不知的动机。说不定，那凶手其人，我也全然不识。未必就出在咱们已经讯问过的这几个人里头。”他的思绪又飘到宅里干粗活的仆人身上，飘到寻常窃贼身上，或是什么来历不明、神通广大的恶徒身上。可有一个念头总归要绕回来：在那致命的一小时里，无论何人，都不可能进出这座宅子而不被人察觉。

正在这时，基蒂·弗伦奇走进屋来，叫他心花怒放。

“早安啊，破晓的玫瑰。”他望着她那张明艳的脸，说道，“见到了我，你可有十足十的欢喜？”

“有的，仁厚的先生。”她答道，浅浅行了个屈膝礼，笑得说不出的亲热。

===Original===
“Well, then, sit down here, and let me talk to you, for my thoughts are running riot, and I’m sure you alone can help me straighten them out.”

“Of course I can. I’m wonderful at that sort of thing. But, first I’ll tell you about Miss Dupuy. She’s awfully ill﻿—I mean prostrated, you know; and she has a high fever and sometimes she chatters rapidly, and then again she won’t open her lips even if anyone speaks to her. We’ve had the doctor, and he says it’s just overstrained nerves and a naturally nervous disposition; but, Mr. Fessenden, I think it’s more than that; I think it’s a guilty conscience.”

“And yesterday, when I implied that Miss Dupuy might know more about it all than she admitted, you wouldn’t listen to a word of it!”

“Yes, I know it, but I’ve changed my mind.”

===Chinese===
“好，那就坐到这儿来，听我对你讲讲。我这满脑子的念头已经闹翻了天，我敢说，唯有你能帮我理出个头绪来。”

“这个我自然办得到。干这类事我可是个中好手。不过，我先得跟你说说杜皮伊小姐。她病得厉害——我是说整个人都垮了，你知道的；她发着高烧，有时叽里咕噜说个不停，可有时任凭谁同她讲话，连嘴唇也不肯张一张。医生我们请过了，说是神经过度劳损，加上天生的神经质脾气；可是费森登先生，我觉得不止于此；我觉得，那是良心有鬼。”

“昨天我暗示说，杜皮伊小姐对这一切的内情，也许比她承认的知道得还多，你可是一个字也听不进去！”

“是，我知道，可我改了主意啦。”

===Original===
“Oh, you have; just for a change, I suppose.”

“No,” said Kitty, more seriously; “but because I’ve heard a lot of Cicely’s ranting﻿—for that’s what it is﻿—and while it’s been only disconnected sentences and sudden exclamations, yet it all points to a guilty knowledge of some sort, which she’s trying to conceal. I don’t say I suspect her, Mr. Fessenden, but I do suspect that she knows a lot more important information than she’s told.”

“Miss Dupuy’s behavior has certainly invited criticism,” began Rob, but before he could go further, the French girl, Marie, appeared at the door, and seemed about to enter.

“What is it, Marie?” said Kitty kindly. “Are you looking for me?”

“Yes, mademoiselle,” said Marie, “and I would speak with monsieur too. I have that to say which is imperative. Too long already have I kept the silence. I must speak at last. Have I permission?”

===Chinese===
“哦，是嘛；我猜不过是为了换个花样吧。”

“不是的，”基蒂说，神色郑重了些，“是因为西塞莉的那些呓语我听了许多——那分明就是呓语——虽说不过是些前言不搭后语的句子和没头没脑的惊呼，可全都指向一件事：她心里藏着某种自知理亏的隐情，正竭力遮掩。我并不是说我疑心她，费森登先生，可我确实疑心，她知道的重要内情，远比她讲出来的多。”

“杜皮伊小姐的做派，确实招人非议。”罗布刚一开腔，那法国姑娘玛丽却出现在门口，看样子正要进来。

“什么事，玛丽？”基蒂和颜悦色地问，“你是来找我的么？”

“是的，小姐，”玛丽说，“我还想同先生也讲句话。我有一件势在必讲的事。我的沉默已经保持得太久了。我终究必须开口。不知可否容我？”

===Original===
“Certainly,” said Fessenden, who saw that Marie was agitated, but very much in earnest. “Tell us what you have to say. Do not be afraid.”

“I am afraid,” said Marie, “but I am afraid of one only. It is the Miss Morton, the stranger lady.”

“Miss Morton?” said Kitty, in surprise. “She won’t hurt you; she has been very good to you.”

“Ah, yes, mademoiselle; but *too* good. Miss Morton has been too kind, too sweet, to Marie! It is that which troubles me.”

===Chinese===
“当然可以。”费森登说。他看得出玛丽心绪激荡，却是一片至诚。“你想说什么，就讲给我们听吧。别害怕。”

“我是害怕，”玛丽说，“可我害怕的只有一个人。是那位莫顿小姐——那位外来的太太。”

“莫顿小姐？”基蒂诧异地说，“她不会伤害你的；她待你一直很好啊。”

“啊，是的，小姐；只是好过了头。莫顿小姐待玛丽太和气、太甜蜜了！叫我心里不安的，正是这个。”

===Original===
“Well, out with it, Marie,” said Rob. “Close that door, if you like, and then speak out, without any more beating around the bush.”

“No, monsieur, I will no longer beat the bush; I will now tell.”

Marie carefully closed the door, and then began her story:

“It was the night of the﻿—of the horror. You remember, Miss French, we sat all in this very room, awaiting the coming of the great doctor﻿—the doctor Leonard.”

===Chinese===
“好了，爽爽快快说出来吧，玛丽。”罗布说，“愿意的话，把门关上，然后放胆讲出来，别再绕弯子了。”

“是，先生，我不再绕弯子了；我这便讲出来。”

玛丽小心翼翼地关上门，随后便开始讲她的经历：

“那是出事的——是那桩骇祸的一夜。您记得么，弗伦奇小姐，我们大家都坐在这间屋子里，等候那位大名鼎鼎的医生驾临——伦纳德医生。”

===Original===
“Yes,” said Kitty, looking intently at the girl; “yes, I know most of you stayed here waiting﻿—but I was not here; Doctor Hills sent Miss Gardner and me to our rooms.”

“Yes; it is so. Well, we sat here, and Miss Morton rose with suddenness and left the room. I followed, partly that I thought she might need my services, and partly﻿—I confess it﻿—because I trusted her not at all, and I wished to assure myself that all was well. I followed her﻿—but secretly﻿—and I﻿—shall I tell you what she did?”

Kitty hesitated. She was not sure she should listen to what was, after all, servants’ gossip about a guest of the house.

But Fessenden looked at it differently. He knew Marie had been the trusted personal maid of Miss Van Norman, and he deemed it right to hear the evidence that she was now anxious to give.

===Chinese===
“是的。”基蒂说着，目不转睛地端详着这姑娘，“是的，我知道你们多数人都留在这儿等着——可我不在这儿；希尔斯医生把加德纳小姐和我打发回房去了。”

“是的；正是这样。嗯，我们坐在这里，莫顿小姐忽然间站起身，离开了屋子。我跟了上去，一半是想她兴许用得着我伺候，一半——我招认——是因为我对她信不过，我想亲眼证实一切无恙。我跟了她——可我是悄悄跟的——然后我——要不要告诉你们，她做了什么？”

基蒂迟疑了。这毕竟是仆人背地里议论宅中的宾客，该不该听下去，她拿不准。

费森登的看法却不一样。他知道玛丽一直是范·诺曼小姐贴身信任的女仆，如今她急于要提供证言，他认为理应听取。

===Original===
“Go on, Marie,” he said gravely. “Be careful to tell it exactly as it happened, whatever it is.”

“Yes, m’sieur. Well, then, I softly followed Miss Morton, because she did not go directly to her own room, but went to Miss Van Norman’s sitting-room and stood before the desk of Miss Madeleine.”

“You are sure, Marie?” said Kitty, who couldn’t help feeling it was dishonorable to listen to this.

“Please, Miss French, let her tell the story in her own way,” said Rob. “It is perhaps of the utmost importance, and may lead to great results.”

===Chinese===
“讲下去，玛丽。”他神情郑重地说，“不管是什么事，都务必原原本本、分毫不差地照实讲出来。”

“是的，先生。嗯，于是我便悄悄跟上了莫顿小姐，因为她没有径直回自己房里，却去了范·诺曼小姐的起居室，站到了马德琳小姐的书桌跟前。”

“你拿得准么，玛丽？”基蒂说，她总免不了觉得，听这些有失忠厚。

“弗伦奇小姐，请你让她照自己的路数讲吧。”罗布说，“这事兴许关系极其重大，说不定会引出天大的后果。”

===Original===
Then Marie went uninterruptedly on.

“She stood in front of the desk, m’sieur; she searched eagerly for papers, reading and discarding several. Then she found some, which she saw with satisfaction, and hastily concealed in her pocket. Miss Morton is a lady who yet has pockets in her gowns. With the papers in her pocket, then, Miss Morton looks about carefully, and, thinking herself unobserved, creeps, but stealthily, to her own room. There﻿—m’sieur, I was obliged to peep at the keyhole﻿—there she lighted a fire in her grate, and burned those papers. With my eyes I saw her. Never would I have told, for it was not my affair, but that I fear for Miss Dupuy. It is in the air that she knows secrets concerning Miss Van Norman’s death. Ah, if one would know secrets, one should question Miss Morton.”

“This is a grave charge you bring against the lady, Marie,” said Fessenden.

===Chinese===
于是玛丽便一气讲了下去。

“她站到书桌跟前，先生；她急切切地翻寻文件，读了几张，又撂开几张。后来她寻着了几份，见了面上顿露满意之色，慌忙揣进了口袋。莫顿小姐是位长裙上还缝着口袋的太太。于是，把文件揣进了口袋，莫顿小姐便四下里仔细张望，自以为无人瞧见，蹑手蹑脚、悄悄地溜回了自己房里。在那里——先生，我只得凑到锁孔上偷看——她在壁炉里生起火来，把那些文件烧了。是我亲眼看见的。我本来决计不会说出去，因为那不干我的事，只是我为杜皮伊小姐害怕。如今满宅风言风语，都说她晓得有关范·诺曼小姐之死的秘密。啊，谁要想知道秘密，就该去盘问莫顿小姐。”

“玛丽，你对这位太太提出的，可是桩严重的指控。”费森登说。

===Original===
“Yes, monsieur, but it is true.”

“I know it is true,” said Kitty; “I have not mentioned it before, but I saw Miss Morton go to Madeleine’s room that night, and afterward go to her own room. I knew nothing, of course, of the papers, and so thought little of the whole incident, but if she really took papers from Madeleine’s desk and burned them, it’s indeed important. What could the papers have been?”

“You know she inherited,” began Fessenden.

“Oh, a will!” cried Kitty.

===Chinese===
“是的，先生，可它是真的。”

“我知道是真的，”基蒂说，“这事我先前没有提起过，可那天夜里，我亲眼看见莫顿小姐进了马德琳的房间，随后又回她自己房里去。文件的事我自然一无所知，所以整件事也没往心里去；可她倘若当真从马德琳的书桌里拿走文件烧掉了，那可真是要紧得很。那些文件会是什么呢？”

“你可知道，她继承了遗产——”费森登开了个头。

“哦，遗嘱！”基蒂失声叫道。

===Original===
“Marie, you may go now,” Rob interrupted; “you did right to tell us this, and rest assured you shall never be blamed for doing so. You will probably be questioned further, but for the present you may go. And thank you.”

Marie curtseyed and went away.

“She’s a good girl,” said Kitty. “I always liked her; and she must have heard, as I did, so much of Cicely’s chatter, that she feared some sort of suspicion would fall on Cicely, and she wanted to divert it toward Miss Morton instead.”

“As usual, with your quick wits, you’ve gone right to the heart of her motive,” said Rob; “but it may be more serious than you’ve yet thought of. Miss Morton inherits, you know.”

===Chinese===
“玛丽，你现在可以去了。”罗布插话说，“你把这件事告诉我们，做得对；放心吧，你决不会因此受到责怪。往后多半还会有人来问你的话，不过眼下你可以走了。多谢你。”

玛丽行了个屈膝礼，走开了。

“她是个好姑娘。”基蒂说，“我一向喜欢她；她想必也同我一样，听了西塞莉那么许多的絮叨，生怕某种嫌疑要落到西塞莉头上，便想把它引开，转到莫顿小姐身上去。”

“你不愧一贯机敏，一语中的，直说到她动机的要害。”罗布说，“不过事情也许比你想的还要严重。你可别忘了，莫顿小姐是得了遗产的。”

===Original===
“Yes, *now*,” said Kitty significantly, “since she burnt that other will.”

“What other will?”

“Oh, don’t you see? The will she burnt was a later one, that *didn’t* give her this house. She burnt it so the earlier one would stand.”

“How do you know this?”

===Chinese===
“是啊，‘如今’才得着的。”基蒂意味深长地说，“打从她烧掉了那另外一份遗嘱起。”

“哪来的另外一份遗嘱？”

“嗳，你还不明白么？她烧掉的那份是后立的，那份遗嘱可没有把这座宅子给她。她烧了它，前头那份才好作数。”

“这些你是怎么知道的？”

===Original===
“I don’t know it, except by common sense! What else would she take from Maddy’s desk and burn except a will? And, of course, a will *not* in her favor, leaving the one that *did* bequeath the house to her to appear as the latest will.”

“Does this line of argument take us any further?” said Rob, so seriously that Kitty began to think.

“You don’t mean,” she whispered, “that Miss Morton﻿—in order to﻿—”

“To receive her legacy﻿—”

===Chinese===
“我哪里知道，还不是凭常识想出来的！她从玛迪的书桌里拿走烧掉的，除了遗嘱还能是什么？当然咯，必定是一份于她不利的遗嘱；烧了它，好叫那份把宅子遗赠给她的，顶上最新遗嘱的名分。”

“照这条思路推论下去，咱们能走得更远么？”罗布说，说得那样郑重，基蒂不由得思索起来。

“你总不是想说，”她低声说，“莫顿小姐——为了——”

“为了承继她的遗产——”

===Original===
“Could﻿—no, she couldn’t! I won’t even think of it!”

“But you thought of Miss Dupuy. Miss French, as I told you yesterday, we must think of every *possible* person, not every *probable* one. These suggestions are not suspicions﻿—and they harm no one who is innocent.”

“I suppose that is so. Well, let us consider Miss Morton then, but of course she didn’t really kill Maddy.”

“I trust not. But I must say I could sooner believe it of a woman of her type than Miss Dupuy’s.”

===Chinese===
“竟会——不，她不会的！我连想都不愿想！”

“可你方才不就疑心过杜皮伊小姐么。弗伦奇小姐，正如我昨天说的，我们必须想到一切‘有可能’的人，而不是一切‘多半可能’的人。这些设想并不是猜疑——它们伤不着任何一个清白的人。”

“我想是这样。好吧，那咱们就来琢磨琢磨莫顿小姐——当然，她总不至于当真杀了玛迪。”

“但愿不至于。不过说句实话，比起杜皮伊小姐那一类型的人，她这一类型的女人做出这种事来，我倒还更容易相信些。”

===Original===
“But Cicely didn’t either! Oh, how *can* you say such dreadful things!”

“We won’t say them any more. They *are* dreadful. But I thought you were going to help me in my detective work, and you balk at every turn.”

“No, I won’t,” said Kitty, looking repentant. “I *do* want to help you; and if you’ll let me help, I’ll suspect everybody you want me to.”

“I want you to help me, but this story of Marie’s is too big for me to handle by myself. I must put that into Mr. Benson’s hands. It is really more important than you can understand.”

“I suppose so,” said Kitty, so humbly that Rob smiled at her, and had great difficulty to refrain from kissing her.

===Chinese===
“可西塞莉也没有哇！唉，你怎么说得出这样可怕的话来！”

“这些话咱们不再说了。它们确实可怕。可我原以为你要帮我查案的，你却回回打退堂鼓。”

“不，我不打退堂鼓了。”基蒂说，一脸悔意，“我是真心想帮你的；只要你肯让我帮，你要我疑心谁，我就疑心谁。”

“我要你帮我的忙，不过玛丽讲的这件事，干系太大，不是我个人担待得起的。我必须把它交到本森先生手里。它实在比你所能领会的还要紧。”

“我想是这样吧。”基蒂说，说得那般谦顺，罗布不禁朝她莞尔一笑，又费了好大的劲，才忍住没有吻她。

## XVI Searching for Clues / 十六　搜寻线索

===Original===
Believing that Marie’s information about Miss Morton was of deep interest, Rob started off at once to confer with Coroner Benson about it.

As he walked along he discussed the affair with himself, and was shocked to realize that for the third time he was suspecting a woman of the murder.

“But how can I help it?” he thought impatiently. “The house was full of women, and not a man in it except the servants, and no breath of suspicion has blown their way. And if a woman did do it, that unpleasant Morton woman is by far the most likely suspect. And if she was actuated by a desire to get her inheritance, why, there’s the motive, and she surely had opportunity. It’s a tangle, but we must find something soon to guide us. A murder like that can’t have been done without leaving some trace somewhere of the criminal.” And then Fessenden’s thoughts drifted away to Kitty French, and he was quite willing to turn the responsibility of his new information over to Mr. Benson. On his way to the coroner’s office he passed the Mapleton Inn. An impulse came to him to investigate Tom Willard’s statements, and he turned back and entered the small hotel.

He thought it wiser to be frank in the matter than to attempt to obtain underhand information. Asking to speak with the proprietor alone, he said plainly:
===Chinese===
罗布认定，玛丽透露的有关莫顿小姐的消息事关重大，当即动身去与本森验尸官商议。

一路走着，他在心里把这件事翻来覆去地掂量，猛然惊觉这已是他第三次疑心凶手是个女人，不由得吓了一跳。

“可我又有什么法子呢？”他不耐烦地想。“宅子里满是女人，除了仆人之外再没有一个男人，而一丝嫌疑的气息也从不曾朝仆人那边吹过。倘若真是女人下的手，那个讨人嫌的莫顿女人便是最有可能的凶嫌，远比任何人都可疑。她若果真受了那份要得到遗产的心思的驱使，喏，动机这不就有了么，机会她更是断然不缺。这团乱麻是够乱的，可我们得赶紧寻出点什么来引路才是。那样一桩凶案，凶手总不至于干得干干净净，不在哪里留下些痕迹吧。”想着想着，费森登的心思又飘到了基蒂·弗伦奇身上，他巴不得把这份新消息的担子干脆卸给本森先生。去验尸官事务所的路上，他经过梅普尔顿旅馆，忽然起意要查一查汤姆·威拉德那番话的虚实，便折身回来，走进了这家小旅馆。

他寻思此事与其拐弯抹角地打探，不如开诚布公为上，便请求与老板单独一谈，直截了当地说道：

===Original===
“I’m a detective from New York City, and my name is Fessenden. I’m interested in investigating the death of Miss Van Norman. I have no suspicions of anyone in particular, but I’m trying to collect a few absolute facts by way of making a beginning. I wish you, therefore, to consider this conversation confidential.”

Mr. Taylor, the landlord of the inn, was flattered at being a party to a confidential conversation with a real detective, and willingly promised secrecy in the matter.

“Then,” went on Fessenden, “will you tell me all you know of the movements of Mr. Willard last evening?”

Mr. Taylor looked a bit disappointed at this request, for he foresaw that his story would be but brief. However, he elaborated the recital and spun it out as long as he possibly could. But after all his circumlocution, Fessenden found that the facts were given precisely as Willard had stated them himself.
===Chinese===
“我是纽约城来的侦探，姓费森登。我正在查访范·诺曼小姐的死因。眼下并没有特定疑心哪一个人，只是想先搜罗几件确凿无疑的事实，权作开端。因此，今日这番谈话，还请阁下视作机密。”

旅馆老板泰勒先生（Mr. Taylor）能与一位货真价实的侦探共参机密，不由得受宠若惊，满口应承此事决不外泄。

“那么，”费森登接着说，“昨天傍晚威拉德先生的行踪，还请您把知道的都讲给我听，可好？”

这要求让泰勒先生面露几分失望，他早料到自己的叙述长不了。饶是如此，他仍旧添枝加叶，把它尽力拉长。可绕了半天圈子，费森登听出的种种事实，与威拉德本人所言分毫不差。

===Original===
The bellboy who had carried up the suitcase was called in, and his story also agreed.

“Yessir,” said the boy; “I took up his bag, and he gimme a quarter, just like any nice gent would. ’N’en I come downstairs, and after while the gent’s bell rang, and I went up, and he wanted ice water. He was in his shirt sleeves then, jes’ gittin’ ready for bed. So I took up the water, and he said, ‘Thank you,’ real pleasant-like, and gimme a dime. He’s a awful nice man, he is. He had his shoes off that time, ’most ready for bed. And that’s all I know about it.”

All this was nothing more nor less than Fessenden had expected. He had asked the questions merely for the satisfaction of having verbal corroboration of Tom’s own story.

With thanks to Mr. Taylor, and a more material token of appreciation to the boy, he went away.
===Chinese===
那个搬手提箱上楼的侍应生也给叫了来，他的说法同样对得上。

“是，先生，”那孩子说。“我把他的箱子送上去，他赏了我两毛五，体面的老爷都是这么赏的。后来我就下了楼。过了一阵，这位老爷按铃，我又上去一趟，他要冰水。那会儿他只穿着衬衫，正要上床呢。我把水送上去，他客客气气地说了声‘多谢’，又赏了我一毛。他可是位大好人，真是。那会儿他鞋都脱了，差不多就要睡了。我知道的就这么些。”

这一切不出费森登所料，半点不差。他发问，无非是想听人亲口印证汤姆本人的说法，图个踏实而已。

他谢过泰勒先生，又给了那孩子一点更为实惠的酬谢，便走了。

===Original===
On reaching the coroner’s office, he was told that Mr. Benson was not in. Fessenden was sorry, for he wanted to discuss the Morton episode with him. He thought of going to Lawyer Peabody’s, who would know all about Miss Van Norman’s will, but as he sauntered through one of the few streets the village possessed, he was rather pleased than otherwise to see Kitty French walking toward him.

She greeted him with apparent satisfaction, and said chummily, “Let’s walk along together and talk it over.”

Immediately coroner and lawyer faded from Rob’s mind, he willingly fell into step beside her, and they walked along the street which soon merged itself into a pleasant country road.

Fessenden told Kitty of his conversation at the inn, but she agreed that it was unimportant.
===Chinese===
到了验尸官事务所，人家告诉他本森先生不在。费森登好生遗憾，他本想同他商量莫顿小姐这一节。他又想到皮博迪律师那里去一趟——范·诺曼小姐的遗嘱，他必然知之甚详——可当他信步穿过镇上仅有的几条街道时，远远瞧见基蒂·弗伦奇迎面走来，心头与其说不快，毋宁说欢喜。

她招呼他时一脸欢喜，亲亲热热地说：“咱们一块儿走走，把这事儿好好聊聊。”

霎时间，验尸官和律师都从罗布的脑海里消失了。他欣然与她并肩而行，沿街走去，街道到后来化作一条宜人的乡间小路。

费森登把旅馆里那番谈话讲给基蒂听，她也觉得无甚要紧。

===Original===
“Of course,” she said, “I suppose it was a good thing to have someone else say the same as Tom said, but as Tom wasn’t even in the house, I don’t see as he is in the mystery at all. But there’s no use of looking further for the criminal. It was Schuyler Carleton, just as sure as I stand here.”

Kitty very surely stood there. They had paused beneath an old willow tree by the side of the road, and Kitty, leaning against a rail fence, looked like a very sweet and winsome Portia, determined to mete out justice.

Though he was himself convinced that he was an unprejudiced seeker after truth, at that moment Robert Fessenden found himself very much swayed by the opinions of the pretty, impetuous girl who addressed him.
===Chinese===
“当然啦，”她说，“有旁人说的跟汤姆说的一样，总归是件好事。可汤姆根本就不在宅子里，我看不出这桩疑案跟他有什么相干。不过，再往别处找凶手也是徒劳。就是斯凯勒·卡尔顿干的，千真万确，就像我此刻站在这儿一样确凿。”

基蒂千真万确地站在那里。两人已在路旁一株老柳树下驻足，基蒂倚着一道横木栅栏，活脱脱是一位娇俏动人的鲍西娅（Portia），铁了心要秉公裁断。

罗伯特·费森登虽自命是个不带成见的求真之人，此刻却发觉，自己全然被这位向他陈词的、又漂亮又性急的姑娘的见解左右了。

===Original===
“I believe I’m going to work all wrong,” he declared. “I can’t help feeling sure that Carleton didn’t do it, and so I’m trying to discover who did.”

“Well, why is that wrong?” demanded Kitty wonderingly.

“Why, I think a better way to do would be to assume, if only for sake of argument, as they say, or rather for sake of a starting-point﻿—to assume that you are right and that Carleton is the evildoer, though I swear I don’t believe it.”
===Chinese===
“我看我这路子怕是全错了，”他宣称。“我总禁不住认定卡尔顿不是凶手，于是净顾着去查究竟是谁干的。”

“咦，这怎么就错了？”基蒂诧异地问。

“呃，我想更好的办法是姑且假定——就像人家常说的，为了便于立论，或者不如说，为了有个着手点——姑且假定你说得对，卡尔顿就是那作恶之人，尽管我发誓决不相信。”

===Original===
Kitty laughed outright. “You’re a nice detective!” she said. “Are you assuming that Schuyler is the villain, merely to be polite to me?”

“I am not, indeed! I feel very politely inclined toward you, I’ll admit, but in this matter I’m very much in earnest. And I believe, by assuming that Carleton is the man, and then looking for proof of it, we may run across clues that will lead us to the real villain.”

Kitty looked at him admiringly, and for Kitty French to look at any young man admiringly was apt to be a bit disturbing to the young man’s peace of mind.

It proved so in this case, and though Fessenden whispered to his own heart that he would attend first to the vindication of his friend Carleton, his own heart whispered back that after that, Miss French must be considered.
===Chinese===
基蒂放声大笑。“你这侦探当得可真妙！”她说。“假定斯凯勒是恶人，就为了对我讲客气？”

“真的不是！不瞒您说，我对您实在怀有十分的敬意，可在这件事上，我是万分认真的。而且我相信，先假定卡尔顿是那人，再循此去寻找证据，我们兴许反倒会撞见些线索，引我们寻到真正的恶人。”

基蒂满怀钦慕地望着他。而基蒂·弗伦奇要满怀钦慕地望着哪个年轻人，那位年轻人的心神多半就得乱上几分。

眼下果然如此。费森登虽然暗暗叮嘱自己的心：先得为朋友卡尔顿洗冤；他的心却悄悄回话：那之后，弗伦奇小姐就该考虑了。

===Original===
“And so,” said Rob, as they turned back homeward, “I’m going to work upon this line. I’m going to look for clues; real, material, tangible clues, such as criminals invariably leave behind them.”

“Do!” cried Kitty. “And I’ll help you. I know we can find something.”

“You see,” went on Fessenden, his enthusiasm kindling from hers, “the actual stage of the tragedy is so restricted. Whatever we find must be in the Van Norman house.”
===Chinese===
“这么说，”转身往回走时，罗布说道，“我就照这个路子进行。我要去找线索；实实在在、有形可据的线索，罪犯们照例总要留下的那种。”

“快找吧！”基蒂喊道。“我帮你一块儿找。我知道咱们准能找出点什么来。”

“你瞧，”费森登接着说，他的热劲也被她鼓了起来，“这出悲剧发生的实际场地就那么窄窄一块。我们找出的任何东西，必定都在范·诺曼家的宅子里。”

===Original===
“Yes, and probably in the library.”

“Or the hall,” he supplemented.

“What kind of a thing do you expect to find?”

“I don’t know, I’m sure. In the Sherlock Holmes stories it’s usually cigar ashes or something like that. Oh, pshaw! I don’t suppose we’ll find anything.”
===Chinese===
“是啊，多半就在书房里。”

“要不就在大厅里。”他补充道。

“你指望找到什么样的东西？”

“说实在的，我也不知道。在夏洛克·福尔摩斯的故事里，找到的通常是雪茄烟灰之类。嗐，算了！我看咱们什么也找不着。”

===Original===
“I think in detective stories everything is found out by footprints. I never saw anything like the obliging way in which people make footprints for detectives.”

“And how absurd it is!” commented Rob. “I don’t believe footprints are ever made clearly enough to deduce the rest of the man from.”

“Well, you see, in detective stories, there’s always that ‘light snow which had fallen late the night before.’ ”

“Yes,” said Fessenden, laughing at her cleverness, “and there’s always some minor character who chances to time that snow exactly, and who knows when it began and when it stopped.”
===Chinese===
“我看侦探小说里，什么都靠脚印查出来。那些人给侦探留脚印时的那份殷勤劲儿，我可是从来没有见识过。”

“再说这是多么荒唐！”罗布议论道。“我不信脚印真能清晰到那个地步，足以教人把那人身上其余的一切推演出来。”

“嗯，你瞧，侦探小说里总少不了那场‘前一天深夜落下的薄雪’。”

“可不是，”费森登说，被她的机灵逗笑了，“而且总有某个无足轻重的小角色，偏偏把那场雪的时刻掐得准准的，几时开始、几时停住，他全知道。”

===Original===
“Yes, and then the principal characters carefully plant their footprints, going and returning﻿—overlapping, you know﻿—and so Mr. Smarty-Cat Detective deduces the whole story.”

“But we’ve no footprints to help us.”

“No, we couldn’t have, in the house.”

“But if it was Schuyler﻿—”

“Well, even if﻿—he couldn’t make footprints without that convenient ‘light snow’ and there isn’t any.”
===Chinese===
“可不，然后那些主要人物便仔仔细细地布下脚印，去时一趟，回来又一趟——你懂的，层层交叠——于是那位自作聪明的猫儿大侦探，便把全部案情演绎了出来。”

“可咱们没有脚印帮忙。”

“是啊，宅子里的事，想有也不成。”

“可要是那是斯凯勒干的——”

“嗳，就算是吧——没有那场方便的‘薄雪’，他也留不下脚印，何况压根儿就没有雪。”

===Original===
“And besides, Schuyler didn’t do it.”

“No, I know he didn’t. But you’re going to assume that, you know, in order to detect the real criminal.”

“Yes, I know I said so; but I don’t believe that game will work, after all.”

“I don’t believe you’re much of a detective, anyway,” said Kitty, so frankly that Fessenden agreed.
===Chinese===
“再说，斯凯勒本来就不是凶手。”

“对，我知道他不是。可你不是说了要这样假定么，为的就是查出真正的凶手。”

“是，我知道我说过；可我到底不信这一招真能奏效。”

“反正我看你呀，当侦探也不大成。”基蒂说得如此坦率，费森登竟也认同了。

===Original===
“I don’t believe I am,” he said honestly. “With the time, place, and number of people so limited, it ought to be easy to solve this mystery at once.”

“I think it’s just those very conditions that make it so hard,” said Kitty, sighing.

And so completely under her spell was Fessenden by this time that he emphatically agreed with her.
===Chinese===
“我也觉得我不成。”他老老实实地说。“时间、地点、人数都限定得这样窄，这桩疑案本该一下子就解开才对。”

“我看，恰恰是这些条件，才把它弄得这么难。”基蒂叹了口气说。

这时费森登已全然拜倒在她的魅力之下，于是斩钉截铁地表示赞同。

===Original===
When they reached the Van Norman house they found it had assumed the hollow, breathless air that invades a house where death is present.

All traces of decoration had been removed from the drawing-room, and it, like the library, had been restored to its usual immaculate order. The scent of flowers, however, was all through the atmosphere, and a feeling of oppression hovered about like a heavy cloud.

Involuntarily Kitty slipped her hand in Rob’s as they entered.
===Chinese===
两人回到范·诺曼宅邸时，只觉得宅子里已笼上一层空落滞闷的气息——死亡在座的宅邸，总要被这种气息侵袭。

客厅里的装饰痕迹已撤除一空，同书房一样，恢复了素日一尘不染的秩序。只是花香仍弥漫在空气之中，一股沉郁之感像浓云一般四下徘徊。

进门时，基蒂不由自主地把手滑进了罗布的手里。

===Original===
Fessenden, too, felt the gloom of the place, but he had made up his mind to do some practical work, and detaining Harris, who had opened the door for them, he said at once, “I want you to open the blinds for a time in all the rooms downstairs. Miss French and I are about to make a search, and, unless necessary, let no one interrupt us.”

“Very good, sir,” said the impassive Harris, who was becoming accustomed to sudden and unexpected orders.

They had chosen their time well for the search, and were not interrupted. Most of the members of the household were in their own rooms; and there happened to be no callers who entered the house.
===Chinese===
费森登也感到了这地方的阴郁，但他已打定主意做点切实的事。他叫住替他们开门的哈里斯，当即说道：“我要你把楼下各个房间的百叶窗都打开一阵子。我和弗伦奇小姐要搜查一番，非属必要，别让任何人来打搅我们。”

“好的，先生。”不动声色的哈里斯答道，他对这类突如其来的意外差遣已经习以为常。

搜查的时辰他们挑得正合适，果然无人打扰。宅中大多数人都在自己房里；又恰好没有进宅来的访客。

===Original===
Molly Gardner had gone away early that morning. She had declared that if she stayed longer she should be downright ill, and, after vainly trying to persuade Kitty to go with her, had returned alone to New York.

Tom Willard and Lawyer Peabody were in Madeleine’s sitting-room, going over the papers in her desk, in a general attempt to learn anything of her affairs that might be important to know. They had desired Miss Dupuy’s presence and assistance, but that young woman refused to go to them, saying she was still too indisposed, and remained, under care of Marie, in her own room.

Fessenden suggested that Kitty should make search in the library while he did the same in the drawing-room; and that afterward they should change places.
===Chinese===
莫莉·加德纳当天一早就走了。她声言再待下去自己准要病倒，劝基蒂同她一起走也没劝动，便独自回纽约去了。

汤姆·威拉德和皮博迪律师正在马德琳的起居室里翻检她写字台里的文件，想把她的事务中凡须知晓之处弄个大概。他们本想要杜皮伊小姐到场相助，无奈那位姑娘推说身子仍大不适，不肯过去，只由玛丽看护着，待在自己房里。

费森登提议，由基蒂搜书房，他搜客厅，随后两人再互相调换。

===Original===
Kitty shivered a little as she went into the room that had been the scene of the tragedy, but she was really anxious to assist Fessenden, and also she wanted to do anything, however insignificant, that would help in the least toward avenging poor Maddy’s death.

And yet it was seemingly a hopeless task. Though she carefully and systematically scrutinized walls, rugs and furniture, not a clue could she find.

She was on her hands and knees under a table when Tom Willard came into the room.
===Chinese===
走进这间惨案发生的屋子时，基蒂微微打了个寒噤，但她实在一心想助费森登一臂之力，再说，凡能为可怜的玛迪之死报仇雪恨出上一点力的，无论多么微不足道，她也愿意去做。

然而这差事看上去着实无望。墙壁、地毯、家具，她都仔仔细细、有条不紊地逐一检视，却连一条线索也寻不见。

她正手脚并用地趴在一张桌子底下，汤姆·威拉德走进了房间。

===Original===
“What are you doing?” he said, unable to repress a smile as Kitty, with her curly hair a bit dishevelled, came scrambling out.

“Hunting for clues,” she said briefly.

“There are no clues,” said Tom gravely. “It’s the most inexplicable affair all ’round.”

“Then you have no suspicion of anyone?”
===Chinese===
“你这是在干什么？”他问。基蒂一头鬈发微微散乱，手脚并用地从桌下爬出来，他忍不住露出了一丝笑意。

“找线索。”她简短地答道。

“没有什么线索。”汤姆正色道。“这桩事情从头到尾，都是最不可究诘的。”

“那么你没有疑心任何人？”

===Original===
“My dear Miss French,” said Tom, looking at her kindly, as one might at a child, but speaking decidedly; “don’t let the *amusement* of amateur detective work lead you into making unnecessary trouble for people. If detective work is to be done, leave it to experienced and professional hands. A girl hunting for broken sleeve-links or shreds of clothing is foolishly theatrical.”

Willard’s grave but gentle voice made Kitty think that she and Fessenden were acting childishly, but after Tom, who had come on an errand, had left the room, Kitty confided to herself that she would rather act foolishly at Rob Fessenden’s bidding than to follow the wise advice of any other man.

This was saying a good deal, but as she said it only to herself, she felt sure her confidence would not be betrayed.
===Chinese===
“亲爱的弗伦奇小姐，”汤姆说。他望着她，目光和善，像望一个孩子，说起话来却斩钉截铁；“别让业余侦探工作的那点‘趣味’，引得您去给人们添不必要的麻烦。侦探的工作若真要做，就交给有经验的专业人手。一位姑娘家到处搜寻断落的袖扣、扯碎的衣片，未免愚蠢得近乎做戏。”

威拉德庄重而温和的语调，倒让基蒂觉得自己和费森登行事未免孩子气。不过，等因事而来的汤姆离开房间之后，基蒂私下对自己承认：她宁可听罗布·费森登的差遣去办傻事，也不肯听从旁的任何男人的明智忠告。

这话说得可着实不轻，好在只是说给自己听的，她笃定这桩心事决不会外泄。

===Original===
Not half an hour had elapsed when Kitty appeared at the drawing-room door with a discontented face, and said, “There’s positively nothing in the library that doesn’t belong there. It has been thoroughly swept, and though there may have been many clues, they’ve all been swept and dusted away.”

“Same here,” said Fessenden dejectedly. “However, let’s change rooms, so we can both feel sure.” Then Kitty searched the drawing-room, and Rob the library, and they both scrutinized every inch of the hall.

“I didn’t find so much as a thread,” said Kitty, as they sat down on a great carved seat in the hall to compare notes.

“I didn’t either,” said Rob, “with one insignificant exception; in the drawing-room I found this, but it doesn’t mean anything.”
===Chinese===
不到半个钟头，基蒂便一脸怏怏地出现在客厅门口，说道：“书房里但凡不属于那儿的东西，实在一件也没有。屋子已经彻底打扫过，纵然从前有过许多线索，如今也都扫的扫、掸的掸，一概没了踪影。”

“我这边也一样。”费森登沮丧地说。“不过，咱们还是换一换房间，好教两个人都死心。”于是基蒂搜客厅，罗布搜书房，末了两人又把大厅一寸一寸细察了一遍。

“我连一根线头都没找着。”基蒂说。这时两人在大厅里一张雕花大坐榻上坐下，交换各自所得。

“我也没找着，”罗布说，“只有一样无关紧要的东西例外。我在客厅里捡到了这个，可它什么也说明不了。”

===Original===
As he spoke he drew from his pocket a tiny globule of a silver color.

“What is it?” asked Kitty, taking it with her fingertips from the palm of his hand.

“It’s a cachou.”

“And what in the world is a cachou? What is it for?”
===Chinese===
说话间，他从口袋里摸出一粒银白色的小圆珠。

“这是什么？”基蒂问，用指尖从他掌心里把它拈起。

“是一粒香口珠（cachou）。”

“香口珠究竟是个什么东西？做什么用的？”

===Original===
“Why, it’s a little confection filled with a sort of spice. Some men use them after smoking, to eradicate the odor of tobacco.”

“Eat them, do you mean? Are they good to eat?” and impulsive Kitty was about to pop the tiny thing into her mouth, when Rob caught her hand.

“Don’t!” he cried. “That’s my only clue, after all this search, and it may be of importance.”

He rescued the cachou from Kitty’s fingers, and then, slipping it into his pocket, he continued to hold the hand from which he had taken it.
===Chinese===
“哦，那是一种小小的糖食，里头裹着一味香料。有些男士抽完烟含上一粒，好除净嘴里的烟草气味。”

“你是说吃的？好吃么？”性急的基蒂眼看就要把那小东西丢进嘴里，罗布一把抓住了她的手。

“别！”他喊道。“找了这半天，这是我仅有的一条线索，说不定大有干系。”

他从基蒂的指间把香口珠抢救下来，塞进自己口袋，却仍旧握着那只刚刚交出东西的手，没有放开。

===Original===
And then, somehow, detective work seemed for a moment to lose its intense interest, and Rob and Kitty talked of other things.

Suddenly Kitty said: “Tom Willard thinks we’re foolish to hunt for clues.”

“I think he’s right,” said Fessenden, smiling, “since we didn’t find anything.”

“Oh, he didn’t exactly say you were foolish, but he said I was. He said it was silly for a girl to hunt around under tables and chairs.”
===Chinese===
接着，不知怎么，侦探的活计仿佛一时失去了它强烈的意趣，罗布和基蒂聊起了旁的事情。

基蒂忽然说道：“汤姆·威拉德觉得咱们找线索是犯傻。”

“我看他说得对。”费森登微笑着说，“既然咱们什么也没找着。”

“嗳，他倒没有明说你是傻瓜，只说我是。他说姑娘家在桌子椅子底下来回钻寻，实在傻气。”

===Original===
“He had no right to say so. It isn’t silly for you to do anything you want to do. But I know what Willard meant. He thinks, as lots of people do, that there’s no sense in expecting to find material evidences of crime﻿—or, rather, of the criminal. And I suppose he’s right. Whoever murdered Miss Van Norman certainly left no tangible traces. But I’m glad we hunted for them, for now I feel certain there were none left; otherwise, I should always have thought there might have been.”

“How much more sensible you are than Mr. Willard,” said Kitty, with an admiring glance that went straight to the young man’s heart, and stayed there. “And, too, you always make use of ‘clues’ if you do find them. Look how cleverly you deduced about the soft and hard lead pencils.”

“Oh, that was nothing,” said Fessenden modestly, though her praise was ecstasy to his soul.

“Indeed it *was* something! It was great work. And I truly believe you’ll make as great a deduction from that little thing you found this morning. What do you call it?”
===Chinese===
