It was too late to go up there that evening, and so the amateur detective was forced to curb his impatience until the next morning. He was afraid the bird might have flown by that time, but there was no help for it. He thought of telephoning, but he didn’t know the name of the people Cicely had gone to, and too, even if he could succeed in getting the call, such a proceeding would only startle her. So he devoted the rest of the evening to writing a letter to Kitty French, ostensibly to thank her for her assistance, but really for the pleasure of writing her. This he posted at midnight, thinking that if he should be detained longer than he anticipated, she would then understand why.

Next morning the eager young man ate his breakfast, and read his paper, a bit impatiently, while he waited for it to be late enough to start.

Soon after nine, he called a taxicab and went to the address Kitty had sent him.

Only the house number had been told in the message, so when Fessenden found himself in the vestibule of an apartment house, with sixteen names above corresponding bells, he was a bit taken aback.

“I wish I’d started earlier,” he thought, “for it’s a matter of trying them all until I strike the right one.”

===Chinese===
当晚再往那边去，为时已晚，这位业余侦探只得按捺性子，等到次日早晨。他怕到那时鸟儿早已飞走，却也无可如何。他想过打电话，可他不知道西塞莉投奔的那家人姓什么；再说，纵然电话打得通，这般举措也只会把她惊动。于是他把晚上余下的时光，全都用来给基蒂·弗伦奇写信，名义上是谢她相助，实在是为了享受给她写信的乐趣。信在午夜寄出，他想，倘若自己耽搁得比预期更久，她见了信便明白缘由了。

第二天早晨，性急的年轻人一面用早餐、看报纸，一面微微不耐地等待着动身的时辰早点到来。

九点刚过，他叫了一辆出租汽车，照基蒂电告的地址去了。

电报上只说了个门牌号，待费森登站到一座公寓的门厅里，面对一排铃钮上方的十六个名字时，不由得有点发怵。

“早知道该早点动身，”他心想，“这可得一个名字一个名字按过去，才碰得上对的那个。”

===Original===
But he fancied he could deduce something from the names themselves, at least, for a start.

Eliminating one or two Irish sounding names, also a Smith and a Miller, he concluded to try first two names which were doubtless French.

The first gave him no success at all, but, undiscouraged, he tried the other.

“I wish to see Miss Dupuy,” he said, to the woman who opened the door.

“She is not here,” was the curt answer. But the intelligence in the woman’s eye at the mention of the name proved to Fessenden that at least this was the place.

===Chinese===
不过他想，单从名字本身，至少也能推断出点眉目来，不妨由此入手。

他剔除了听上去像爱尔兰人的一个两个名字，又除去一个史密斯、一个米勒，决定先试那两个一看便知是法国姓氏的。

头一个全无结果，他并不气馁，又试第二个。

“我想见杜皮伊小姐，”他对开门的女人说。

“她不在这儿。”回答干巴巴的。可一提这个名字，那女人眼里闪过的一丝了悟，向费森登证明：至少，地方是找对了。

===Original===
“Don’t misunderstand,” he said gently. “I want to see Miss Dupuy merely for a few moments’ friendly conversation. It will be for her advantage to see me, rather than to refuse.”

“But she is not here,” repeated the woman. “There is no person of that name in my house.”

“When did she go?” asked Rob quietly﻿—so quietly that the woman was taken off her guard.

“About half an hour ago,” she said, and then, with a horror-stricken look at her own thoughtlessness, she added hastily, “I mean my friend went. Your Miss Dupuy I do not know.”

“Yes, you do,” said Rob decidedly, “and as she has gone, you must tell me at once where she went.”

The woman refused, and not until after a somewhat stormy scene, and some rather severe threats on Fessenden’s part did she consent to tell that Cicely had gone to the Grand Central Station. More than this she would not say, and thinking he was wasting valuable time on her, Rob turned and, racing down the stairs, for there was no elevator, he jumped in his cab and whizzed away to the station.

===Chinese===
“请别误会，”他温声说，“我想见杜皮伊小姐，只不过想同她友好地谈上几句。见我，于她有好处；拒而不见，反倒于她不利。”

“可她不在这儿，”那女人又说了一遍，“我家里没有叫这个名字的人。”

“她什么时候走的？”罗布静静地问——问得那么轻声细气，那女人竟冷不防失了戒备。

“半个钟头以前，”她说，随即惊觉自己失言，脸上现出骇然之色，慌忙找补一句：“我是说我的朋友走了。你说的那个杜皮伊小姐，我不认得。”

“不，你认得，”罗布斩钉截铁地说，“她既然走了，你就得马上告诉我她去了哪儿。”

那女人执意不肯，直闹得一场不小的风波，费森登又放了几句相当严厉的狠话，她才肯说出西塞莉是往大中央车站去了。再多的话，她一个字也不肯吐。罗布心想在她身上只是白白耗着宝贵的工夫，便转身冲下楼梯——楼里没有电梯——跳上出租汽车，风驰电掣般直奔车站而去。

## XXI A Successful Pursuit / 二十一　一场成功的追踪

===Original===
Before he entered the station he looked through the doorway, and to his delight saw the girl for whom he was looking.

He did not rush madly into the station, but paused a moment, and then walked in quietly, thinking that if his quest should be successful he must not frighten the excitable girl.

Cicely sat on one of the benches in the waiting-room. In her dainty travelling costume of black, and her small hat with its black veil, she looked so fair and young that Rob felt sudden misgivings as to his errand. But it must be done, and, quietly advancing, he took a seat beside her.

===Chinese===
进站之前，他先从门洞里朝里望了一眼，欣喜地看见了他正要找的那个姑娘。

他没有莽莽撞撞地冲进车站，而是停了一停，才安安静静地走了进去；他心想，这一趟若想马到成功，就万不可惊吓了这个容易激动的姑娘。

西塞莉正坐在候车室的一条长椅上。她一身素雅的黑衣旅行装，小帽上罩着黑面纱，看上去那样娇嫩年轻，罗布一时竟对自己的来意生出几分踌躇。可这事非办不可；他悄悄走上前去，在她身旁坐下了。

===Original===
“Where are you going, Miss Dupuy?” he asked in a voice which was kinder and more gentle than he himself realized.

She looked up with a start, and said in a low voice, “Why do you follow me? May I not be left alone to go where I choose?”

“You may, Miss Dupuy, if you will tell me where you are going, and give me your word of honor that you will return if sent for.”

“To be put through an examination! No, thank you. I’m going away where I hope I shall never see a detective or a coroner again!”

===Chinese===
“您这是要去哪儿，杜皮伊小姐？”他问道。那语气之和气温存，连他自己也没有觉察。

她吓了一跳，抬起头来，低声说：“您干吗跟着我？为什么不能让我一个人爱去哪儿就去哪儿？”

“可以，杜皮伊小姐，只要您告诉我您要去哪儿，并且以名誉担保：一旦有人来请您，您就回来。”

“回去听人盘问吗！不，多谢了。我要走，走到一个地方去，但愿从今往后再也见不着侦探，也见不着验尸官！”

===Original===
“Are you afraid of them, Miss Dupuy?”

The girl gave him a strange glance; but it showed anxiety rather than fear. However, her only reply was a low spoken “Yes.”

“And why are you afraid?”

“I am afraid I may tell things that I don’t want to tell.” The girl spoke abstractedly and seemed to be thinking aloud rather than addressing her questioner.

===Chinese===
“您怕他们吗，杜皮伊小姐？”

姑娘奇怪地瞟了他一眼；那一眼里透着焦灼，倒不像是害怕。然而她的回答只有低低的一声：“怕。”

“那您怕的是什么呢？”

“我怕自己会说出些我不想说的话来。”姑娘说得心不在焉，倒像是在自言自语，而不像在答她面前的问话人。

===Original===
It may be that Fessenden was influenced by her beauty or by the exquisite femininity of her dainty contour and apparel, but aside from all this he received a sudden impression that what this girl said did not betoken guilt. He could not have explained it to himself, but he was at the moment convinced that though she knew more than she had yet told, Cicely Dupuy was herself innocent.

“Miss Dupuy,” he said very earnestly, “won’t you look upon me as a friend instead of a foe? I am quite sure you can tell me more than you have told about the Van Norman tragedy. Am I wrong in thinking you are keeping something back?”

“I have nothing to tell,” said Cicely, and the stubborn expression returned to her eyes.

===Chinese===
也许是费森登受了她的美貌的影响，受了她那娇好的身段衣装间流溢的精致女性风韵的感染；但撇开这一切不谈，他蓦地得了一个印象：这姑娘方才那番话，并不像是有罪的表露。这里头的缘故他自己也说不清楚，可此刻他就是认定：西塞莉·杜皮伊虽然知道的比她说出来的多，她本人却是清白的。

“杜皮伊小姐，”他十分恳切地说，“您能不能别把我当仇敌，把我当个朋友？我十分有把握，关于范·诺曼家的这场惨祸，您能告诉我的，比您已经说出来的多。您藏着话没有讲——我这样想，是不是想错了？”

“我没有什么可讲的。”西塞莉说，眼里又浮起那副执拗的神色。

===Original===
It did not seem a very appropriate place in which to carry on such a personal conversation, but Fessenden thought perhaps the very publicity of the scene might tend to make Miss Dupuy preserve her equanimity better than in a private house. So he went on:

“Yes, you have several things to tell me, and I want you to tell me now. The last time I talked to you about this matter I asked you why you gave false evidence as to the time that Mr. Carleton entered the Van Norman house that evening, and you responded by fainting away. Now you must tell me why that question affected you so seriously.”

“It didn’t. I was nervous and overwrought, and I chanced to faint just then.”

Fessenden saw that this explanation was untrue, but had been thought up and held ready for this occasion. He saw, too, that the girl held herself well in hand, so he dared to be more definite in his inquiries.

===Chinese===
在这种公共场所谈这样涉及私衷的话，似乎不很合适；可费森登心想，也许正因场面公开，杜皮伊小姐反倒比在私人宅子里更容易保持镇定。于是他接着说：

“有，您有好几件事要告诉我，而且我要您现在就讲。上回同您谈这件事，我问您，那天晚上卡尔顿先生进范·诺曼家的时刻，您为什么要作伪证；您听了，当场就晕了过去。如今您必须告诉我，那个问题为什么会叫您受这样大的震动。”

“并没有震动。我本来神经紧张、心绪恶劣，恰巧那一刻就晕过去了。”

费森登看得出，这套解释并不真，而是预先编好、专等这个时候拿出来的；他还看得出，这姑娘把持得住自己，于是他敢把问话逼得更切实一些了。

===Original===
“Do you know, Miss Dupuy, that you are seriously incriminating yourself when you give false evidence?”

“I don’t care,” was the answer, not flippantly given, but with an earnestness of which the speaker herself seemed unaware.

And Fessenden was a good enough reader of character to perceive that she spoke truthfully.

The only construction he could put upon this was that, as he couldn’t help believing, the girl was innocent and therefore feared no incriminating evidence against her.

But in that case what was she afraid of, and why was she running away?

===Chinese===
“您可知道，杜皮伊小姐，您这样作伪证，是在重重地给自己招上犯罪的嫌疑吗？”

“我不在乎。”这是她的回答，语气并不轻佻，倒带着一种连说话人自己似乎都未觉察的郑重。

而费森登是善于鉴察心性的，他看得出这话出自真心。

对此他所能作的唯一解释便是：正如他不由自主地相信的那样，这姑娘是清白的，所以不怕任何于她不利的罪证。

可是既然如此，她怕的又是什么？她干吗要逃走呢？

===Original===
“Miss Dupuy,” he began, starting on a new tack, “please show more confidence in me. Will you answer me more straightforwardly if I assure you of my belief in your own innocence? I will not conceal from you the fact that not everyone is so convinced of that as I am, and so I look to you for help to establish it.”

“Establish what? My innocence?” said Cicely, and now she looked bewildered, rather than afraid. “Does anybody think that *I* killed Miss Van Norman?”

“Without going so far as to say anyone thinks so, I will tell you that they think there are indications that point to such a thing.”

“How absurd!” said Cicely, and the honesty of her tone seemed to verify Fessenden’s conviction that whatever guilty knowledge this girl might possess, she herself was innocent of crime.

===Chinese===
“杜皮伊小姐，”他换了一条路子开口说，“请再信我一些。倘若我向您担保，我相信您本人是清白的，您肯不肯答得再直率一些？我不瞒您，并非人人都像我这样深信这一点；所以我指望您出力，帮我把这一点证实出来。”

“证实什么？证实我清白？”西塞莉说；这一下她脸上现出的是惶惑，倒不是害怕。“难道有人当真以为，是我杀了范·诺曼小姐？”

“人心里怎么想的，我且不去说死；我只告诉您，他们认为，确有一些迹象指向这种可能。”

“真是荒唐！”西塞莉说。她语气的坦诚，似乎印证了费森登的信念：这姑娘无论掌握了什么见不得人的内情，她本人绝没有犯罪。

===Original===
“If it is an absurd idea, then why not return to Mapleton and answer any queries that may be put to you? You are innocent, therefore you have nothing to fear.”

“I have a great deal to fear.”

The girl spoke gently, even sadly, now. She seemed full of anxiety and sorrow, that yet showed no trace of apprehension for herself.

All at once a light broke upon Fessenden. She was shielding somebody. Nor was it hard to guess who it might be!

===Chinese===
“既然这念头荒唐，那干吗不回梅普尔顿去，人家问什么就答什么呢？您是清白的，所以您没什么可怕的。”

“我要怕的东西多着呢。”

这时姑娘说话的声气变得温婉，甚至凄婉。她仿佛满心是焦灼与哀伤，然而那焦灼哀伤里，没有一丝一毫是替自己担忧。

蓦地，一线亮光在费森登心头闪过：她是在庇护什么人！而那个人是谁，也不难猜着！

===Original===
“Miss Dupuy,” began Rob again, eagerly this time, “I have succeeded in establishing, practically, Mr. Carleton’s innocence. May I not likewise establish your own?”

“Mr. Carleton’s innocence!” repeated the girl, clasping her hands. “Oh, is that true? Then who did do it?”

“We don’t know yet,” went on Rob, hastening to make the most of the advantage he had gained; “but having assured you that it was not Schuyler Carleton, will you not tell me what it is you have been keeping secret?”

“How do you know Mr. Carleton is innocent? Have you proved it? Has someone else confessed?”

“No, no one has confessed. And, indeed, I may as well own up that no one is quite so sure of Mr. Carleton’s innocence as I am myself. But I *am* sure of it, and I’m going to prove it. Now, will you not help me to do so?”

===Chinese===
“杜皮伊小姐，”罗布又开口了，这一回语气急切，“我事实上已经把卡尔顿先生的清白证实出来了。可不可以也照样证实您的清白呢？”

“卡尔顿先生的清白！”姑娘双手紧握，重复道，“哦，这是真的吗？那么，究竟是谁干的呢？”

“我们还不知道，”罗布接着说，急于乘胜把这个得来不易的优势用足；“不过我既然向您担保了那不是斯凯勒·卡尔顿干的，您肯不肯告诉我，您一直守口藏私的到底是什么？”

“您怎么知道卡尔顿先生是清白的？您证实过了？还是有别人招认了？”

“不，没有人招认。说实在的，我不妨坦白：论确信卡尔顿先生清白，谁也不如我自己；可我确实信定了，而且我要把它证明出来。怎么样，您不肯帮我这个忙吗？”

===Original===
“How can I help you?”

“By explaining that discrepancy in time, so far as you can. You testified that Mr. Carleton entered the house at half-past eleven, and Mr. Hunt said he came in at quarter-past. What made you tell that falsehood, and stick to it?”

“Why, nothing,” exclaimed Cicely, “except that I thought I saw Mr. Carleton come into the house some little time before he cried out for help. I was looking over the baluster when Mr. Hunt said he saw me, and I, too, thought it was Mr. Carleton who came in then.”

“It was Mr. Carleton, but he has satisfactorily explained why he came in, and what he was doing until the time when he called out for help. Why did you not tell us about this at first?”

===Chinese===
“我能怎么帮您呢？”

“把那个时间上的出入解释解释，能解释多少是多少。您作证说，卡尔顿先生是十一点半进宅的，亨特先生却说他是十一点一刻进来的。您为什么编了那句假话，还一口咬定不改？”

“呃，没有什么缘故，”西塞莉嚷道，“只因为我自以为看见卡尔顿先生在高声呼救之前一小会儿进了宅子。亨特先生说他瞧见我的时候，我正扒在楼梯栏杆上朝下望；我当时也以为，那时进来的就是卡尔顿先生。”

“进来的人确实是他；可他为什么进来、直到呼喊救命之前又做了些什么，如今都已解释得清清楚楚。这些，您起初为什么不告诉我们呢？”

===Original===
“I was afraid﻿—afraid they might connect Mr. Carleton with the murder, and I was afraid﻿—”

“You were afraid that he really had done the deed?”

“Yes,” said Cicely in a very low voice, but with an intonation that left no doubt of her truthfulness.

“Then,” said Rob in his kindest way, “you may set your mind at rest. Mr. Carleton is no longer under actual suspicion, and you may go away, as you intended, for a few days’ rest. I should be glad to have your address, though I trust it will not be necessary for me to send for you; and I know you will not be called to witness against Schuyler Carleton.”

===Chinese===
“我怕——怕人家把卡尔顿先生同这桩凶杀扯在一处，我又怕——”

“您是怕他当真就是凶手？”

“是的，”西塞莉说，声音压得极低，可那语调却不容置疑，听得出她说的是真话。

“那么，”罗布用他最温和的口气说，“您可以安心了。如今再没有人实际疑心卡尔顿先生；您可以照原来的打算出门去，休憩几天。地址还是请留一个给我，不过我相信多半用不着专程去请您回来；我也知道，不会传您去出庭作证、指证斯凯勒·卡尔顿的。”

===Original===
Cicely gave the required address, and though they continued the conversation for a short time, Rob concluded that the girl knew nothing that actually bore on the case. Her own false evidence and nervous apprehension had all been because of her anxiety about Mr. Carleton, and her fear that he had really been the murderer. Her written paper, and all the evidences of her jealousy of Miss Van Norman, were the result of her secret and unrequited love for the man, and her attempted flight was only because she feared that her uncontrollable emotion and impulsive utterances might help to incriminate him.

Fessenden was truly sorry for her, and glad that she could go away from the trying scenes for a time. He felt sure that she would come, if summoned, for now, relieved of her doubt of Carleton, she had no reason for refusing any testimony she could give.

It was in a kindly spirit that he bade her goodbye, and promised to use every effort not only to establish Carleton’s innocence, but to discover the guilty one.

===Chinese===
西塞莉把所要的地址给了他。随后两人又谈了一小会儿，罗布便断定，这姑娘并不掌握任何与案子实际相干的事。她作伪证，她神经质的惶恐，全都出于她对卡尔顿先生的记挂，怕他果真是凶手；她那张写下的字条，连同种种流露出她对范·诺曼小姐妒意的痕迹，都是她暗暗恋着此人而不得回报的结果；而她这次出逃未遂，也只因她怕自己按捺不住的感情和脱口而出的话，会给定他的罪添上助力。

费森登着实替她难过，也高兴她可以暂时离开这令人难堪的是非之地。他确信，只要一传她，她就会来；因为如今她对卡尔顿的疑团既已释然，便再没有理由拒绝提供她所能提供的任何证词。

他怀着一片好意同她道了别，并且允诺必定竭尽全力，不但要证实卡尔顿的清白，还要把有罪之人查出来。

===Original===
When Fessenden returned to the Van Norman house, several people were awaiting him in the library. Miss Morton and Kitty French were there, also Coroner Benson and Detective Fairbanks.

“Were you too late?” asked Kitty, as Rob entered the room.

“No, not too late. I found Miss Dupuy in the Grand Central station, and I had a talk with her.”

“Well?” said Kitty impatiently.

===Chinese===
费森登回到范·诺曼宅时，几个人正在书房里等他。莫顿小姐和基蒂·弗伦奇在那儿，验尸官本森和侦探费尔班克斯也在。

“没赶得上吗？”罗布进屋时，基蒂问道。

“没有，还不算晚。我在大中央车站找到了杜皮伊小姐，同她谈了一回。”

“怎么样？”基蒂迫不及待地问。

===Original===
“She is as innocent as you or I.”

“How did you find it out so quickly?” inquired Mr. Fairbanks, who had a real liking for the enthusiastic young fellow.

“Why, I found out that she *was* hanging over the baluster, as Hunt said; and she did see Carleton come in at quarter after eleven. She then went back to her room, and heard Carleton cry out at half-past eleven, and when she discovered what had happened she suspected Carleton of the deed; and, endeavoring to shield him, she refused to give evidence that might incriminate him.”

“But,” cried Kitty, “of course Mr. Carleton didn’t do it if Cicely did.”

===Chinese===
“她跟你我一样，是清白的。”

“你怎么这样快就弄明白了？”费尔班克斯先生问道；他打心底里喜欢这个满腔热忱的年轻人。

“嗐，我弄明白了：她确实扒在楼梯栏杆上朝下望，同亨特说的一样；她也确实看见卡尔顿十一点一刻进了门。随后她回到自己房里，十一点半听见卡尔顿喊出声来；等到发觉出了什么事，她便疑心是卡尔顿干的；于是竭力庇护他，不肯提供可能给他定罪的证词。”

“可是，”基蒂嚷道，“要是西塞莉干的，卡尔顿先生自然就没有干啦。”

===Original===
“But don’t you see, Miss French,” said the older detective, as Fessenden sat staring in blank surprise at what he deemed Kitty’s stupidity﻿—“don’t you see that if Miss Dupuy suspected Mr. Carleton she couldn’t by any possibility be guilty herself.”

“Why, of course she couldn’t!” exclaimed Kitty. “And I’m truly glad, for I can’t help liking that girl, if she is queer. But, then, who did do it?”

Suspicion was again at a standstill. There was no evidence to point anywhere; there were no clues to follow, and no one had any suggestion to offer.

===Chinese===
“可您没看出来吗，弗伦奇小姐，”年长的那位侦探说——这时费森登正瞪着眼怔怔坐在那里，只觉得基蒂愚钝得可以——“您没看出来，杜皮伊小姐既然疑心是卡尔顿先生干的，她自己就无论如何不可能是凶手。”

“嗳，她当然干不了！”基蒂嚷道，“我真高兴——那姑娘我着实喜欢，尽管她人古里古怪。可是，究竟是谁干的呢？”

猜疑又一次停了摆。没有任何证据指向哪一方；没有线索可循，也没有一个人提得出任何主张。

===Original===
It was at this juncture that Tom Willard and Schuyler Carleton came in together.

They were told of Fessenden’s interview with Miss Dupuy at the station, and Carleton expressed himself as thoroughly glad that the girl was exonerated. He said little, however, for it was a delicate subject, since it all hinged on Miss Dupuy’s affection for himself.

Tom Willard listened to Fessenden’s recital, but he only said that nothing would ever have induced him to suspect Miss Dupuy, anyway, for it could not have been the deed of a fragile young girl.

“The blow that killed Maddy was powerfully dealt,” said Tom; “and I can’t help thinking it was some tramp or professional burglar who was clever enough to elude Harris’s fastenings. Or some window may have been overlooked that night. At any rate, we have no more plausible theory.”

===Chinese===
正在这个节骨眼上，汤姆·威拉德和斯凯勒·卡尔顿一同走了进来。

大家把费森登在车站会见杜皮伊小姐的经过讲给他们听了。卡尔顿表示，这姑娘洗清了嫌疑，他由衷地欢喜；不过他没有多说，因为这个话题委实微妙——桩桩件件，全扣在杜皮伊小姐对他本人的一片痴情上。

汤姆·威拉德听费森登讲完，只说了一句：横竖无论怎样，他也决计想不到要去疑心杜皮伊小姐，因为那样一个柔弱的年轻姑娘，决干不出这样的事来。

“送掉玛迪性命的那一击，下手极重，”汤姆说，“我总忍不住想，这多半是哪个流浪汉，或是哪个惯窃老手，机灵得避开了哈里斯布置的种种锁钥。要不然，那天夜里许有哪扇窗子没人查到。横竖，我们再拿不出更近情理的说法了。”

===Original===
“We have not,” said Mr. Fairbanks; “but I for one am not content to let the matter rest here. I should like to suggest that we call in some celebrated detective, whose experience and skill would discover what is beyond the powers of Mr. Fessenden and myself.”

Rob felt flattered that Mr. Fairbanks classed him with himself, and felt anxious too that the suggestion of employing a more skilful detective should be carried out.

“But,” objected Coroner Benson, “to engage a detective of high standing would entail considerable expense, and I’m not sure that I’m authorized to sanction this.”

There was a silence, but nearly everyone in the room was thinking that surely this was the time for Tom Willard to make use of his lately inherited Van Norman money.

===Chinese===
“是拿不出，”费尔班克斯先生说，“不过我这一边，可不甘心就让这案子到此为止。我想提议：我们去请一位鼎鼎大名的侦探来；凭他的经验和本事，去查明费森登先生同我力所不及的真相。”

费尔班克斯先生把自己同他并称，罗布听来不免有几分受用；同时他也切盼雇请更能手的侦探这一提议能够付诸实行。

“可是，”验尸官本森提出异议，“聘一位声望高的侦探，得花相当大的一笔钱，我拿不准我有权限批准这件事。”

屋里静了一阵，可在座的人心里几乎都在想：如今正该是汤姆·威拉德动用他新近继承的范·诺曼钱财的时候了。

===Original===
Nor was Willard delinquent. Though showing no overwillingness in the matter, he said plainly that he would be glad if Coroner Benson or Mr. Fairbanks would engage the services of the best detective they could find, and allow him to defray all expenses attendant thereon.

At this a murmur of approval went round the room. All his hearers were at their wits’ end what to do next, and the opportunity of putting a really great detective on the case was welcome indeed.

“But I don’t believe,” said Willard, “that he will find out anything more than our own men have discovered.” The appreciative glance Tom gave Mr. Fairbanks and Rob quite soothed whatever touch of jealousy they may have felt of the new detective.

It was Carleton who suggested Fleming Stone. He did not know the man personally, but he had read and heard of the wonderful work he had done in celebrated cases all over the country.

===Chinese===
威拉德也没有怠慢。他虽未显得过分热心，话却说得分明：验尸官本森或是费尔班克斯先生，若肯延聘他们所能物色到的最好的侦探，并容他承担随之而来的一切费用，他将是十分乐意的。

这话一出，满屋响起一片赞许的低语。听他说话的人个个正为下一步束手无策，如今有机会把一位真正的大侦探请来办这案子，自然再欢迎不过。

“不过我不相信，”威拉德说，“他能查出比我们自己这些人已经查到的更多的东西来。”汤姆朝费尔班克斯先生和罗布投去的一瞥满含赏识，把他们俩对新来的侦探或许怀有的那一星醋意，抚慰得烟消云散。

提议请弗莱明·斯通（Fleming Stone）的，是卡尔顿。此人他素未谋面，可全国各地一桩桩名案中此人立下的奇功，他读到过，也听说过。

===Original===
Of course they had all heard of Fleming Stone, and each felt a thrill of gratitude to Willard, whose wealth made it possible to employ the great detective.

Mr. Fairbanks wasted no time, but wrote at once to Fleming Stone, and received a reply stating that he would arrive in Mapleton in a few days.

But in the meantime Rob Fessenden could not be idle.

In truth, he had a secret ambition to solve the mystery himself, before the great detective came, and to this end he stayed on in Mapleton, and racked his brain for ideas on the subject.

===Chinese===
弗莱明·斯通的名字，他们自然人人听过；每一个人都对威拉德涌起一阵感激之情——正是他的财富，使延请这位大侦探成为可能。

费尔班克斯先生一刻也不耽搁，当即写信给弗莱明·斯通，收到的回信说，几天之内他便可以抵达梅普尔顿。

然而在这期间，罗布·费森登却闲不下来。

说实在的，他心里藏着一份不肯出口的抱负：要赶在这位大侦探到来之前，亲手解开这个谜。为此他留在梅普尔顿不走，为这桩案子绞尽脑汁地想主意。

===Original===
Mr. Fairbanks was more easily discouraged, and frankly confessed the case was beyond his powers.

Privately, he still suspected Mr. Carleton, but in the face of Rob’s faith in his friend, and also because of the demeanor of Carleton himself, he couldn’t avow his suspicions.

For since Fessenden’s assertions of confidence, Carleton had changed in his attitude toward the world at large.

Still broken and saddened by the tragedy, he did not show that abject and self-condemnatory air which had hung round him during the inquest week.

===Chinese===
费尔班克斯先生却更容易气馁，索性坦白承认，这案子超出了他的能力。

私下里，他仍旧疑心卡尔顿先生；可是面对罗布对其朋友的笃信，再加上卡尔顿本人如今的神情举止，他没法把自己那份疑心宣之于口。

因为自打费森登那番信任的表示之后，卡尔顿对世上众人的态度已经变了。

惨祸依旧使他身心交瘁、哀伤不已，可验尸审讯那一周里萦绕在他身上的那种自惭自责、抬不起头的神气，已经不再见了。

===Original===
Kitty French had *almost* recovered faith in him, and had there been anyone else at all to suspect, she would have asserted her belief in his innocence.

Carleton himself seemed baffled. His suspicions had been directed toward Cicely, because he could see no other possibility; but the proof of her suspicions of himself, of course, showed he was wrong in the matter.

He could suggest nothing; he could think of nobody who might have done the deed, and he was thoroughly content to place the whole affair unreservedly in the hands of Fleming Stone.

Indeed, everyone seemed to be glad of the expected help, if we except Fessenden. He was restlessly eager to do something himself, and saw no reason why he shouldn’t keep on trying until Stone came.

===Chinese===
基蒂·弗伦奇对他的信心差一点儿就完全恢复了；倘若那时还有旁的什么人多少可供怀疑，她本会断然申明，她是相信他清白的。

卡尔顿自己倒像是一筹莫展。他的疑心原是朝着西塞莉去的，因为他看不出旁的可能；可她疑心他本人的事实证明，这一层他猜错了。

他提不出任何主张；他想不出有谁可能干出这桩事来，于是心悦诚服，甘愿把整个案子毫无保留地交到弗莱明·斯通手里。

说真的，这份指日可待的援助似乎人人都引以为幸——只有费森登除外。他焦灼地急于亲手做点什么，而且认为，没有任何道理不继续试下去，一直试到斯通到来为止。

## XXII A Talk with Miss Morton / 二十二　与莫顿小姐的一次谈话

===Original===

Of course Fessenden confided his wishes to Kitty French. Equally of course, that obliging young woman was desirous of helping him attain them. But neither of them could think of new lines of investigation to pursue.

“We’ve no clue but that little cachou,” said Miss French, by way of summing up; “and as that’s no good at all, we have really nothing that can be called a clue.”

“No,” agreed Rob, “and we have no suspect. Now that Carleton and Miss Dupuy are both out of it, I don’t see who could have done it.”

“I never felt fully satisfied about Miss Morton and her burned paper,” said Kitty thoughtfully.

They were walking along a village road while carrying on this conversation, so there was no danger of Miss Morton’s overhearing them.

===Chinese===

费森登自然把自己的心愿对基蒂·弗伦奇和盘托出；那位乐于助人的姑娘自然也一心想帮他如愿。可两个人谁都再想不出还有什么新的查究路子可走。

“咱们手里除了那颗小小的香口珠，再没有线索可凭，”弗伦奇小姐拢总归结道，“而那玩意儿又根本派不上用场，所以实际上，凡是称得上线索的东西，咱们一样也没有。”

“是啊，”罗布附和道，“咱们连个嫌疑人也没有。卡尔顿和杜皮伊小姐既然都洗清了，我真看不出这案子还能是谁干的。”

“莫顿小姐和她烧掉的那份纸，我心里一直没能踏实，”基蒂若有所思地说。

两人说这番话的时候，正沿着村里的一条大路散步，不必担心叫莫顿小姐听了去。

===Original===

“I’ve never felt satisfied about that woman, anyway,” said Rob. “The oftener I see her the less I like her. She’s too smug and complacent. And yet when she was questioned, she went all to pieces.”

“Well, as she flatly contradicted what Marie had said, of course they couldn’t keep on questioning her. You can’t take a servant’s word against a lady’s.”

“You ought to, in a serious case like this. I say, Kitty, let’s go there now and have a heart-to-heart talk with her.”

Kitty laughed at the idea of a heart-to-heart talk between those two people, but said she was willing to go.

===Chinese===

“那个女人，横竖我是从来没有放心过的，”罗布说，“见面越勤，我越看不上她。她太自鸣得意了。可偏偏受盘问的时候，她又整个儿垮了下来。”

“唉，她把玛丽的话驳得干干净净，人家自然不好再盘问下去。总不能拿一个仆人的话，去顶一位小姐的话。”

“像这样重大的案子，就该那么办。我说，基蒂，咱们这会儿就上她那儿去，同她推心置腹地谈一谈。”

一想到那两个人之间来一场推心置腹的谈话，基蒂不由得好笑，嘴上却说自己愿意去。

===Original===

“It mayn’t amount to anything,” went on Rob, “and yet, it may. I’ve asked Mr. Fairbanks to chase up that burned paper matter, but he said there was nothing in it. He didn’t hear Marie’s story, you see﻿—he only heard it retold, and he doesn’t know how sincere that girl seemed to be when she told about it.”

“Yes, and I saw Miss Morton in Maddy’s room, too. I think she ought to tell what she was up to.”

So to the Van Norman house went the two inquisitors, and had Miss Morton known of their fell designs she might not have greeted them as cordially as she did.

Miss Morton had grown fond of Kitty French during the girl’s stay with her, and she looked with approval on the fast-growing friendship between her and young Fessenden.

===Chinese===

“这一趟也许谈不出什么名堂，”罗布接着说，“可也许谈得出来。我请费尔班克斯先生去追查那份烧掉的纸的事，他说这里头没有什么可挖的。要知道，玛丽亲口讲的那番话他没有听过——他听到的只是转述，他不知道那姑娘讲起这件事的时候，看上去多么真心实意。”

“是啊，我还亲眼看见莫顿小姐在玛迪房里待过呢。她该说清楚，她当时在搞什么名堂。”

于是这两位问案的人便往范·诺曼宅邸去了；倘若莫顿小姐早知道他们这番不怀好意的来意，只怕未必会像实际这样热络地招呼他们。

基蒂·弗伦奇在她这里小住的日子，莫顿小姐已经同她处得十分亲热，对她同年轻的费森登之间一日千里增长的交情，也看着顺眼称许。

===Original===

As the hostess at the Van Norman house, too, Miss Morton showed a kindly hospitality, and though she was without doubt eccentric, and sometimes curt of speech, she conducted the household and directed the servants with very little friction or awkwardness.

She was most friendly toward Tom Willard and Schuyler Carleton, and the latter often dropped in at the tea hour. Fessenden dropped in at any hour of the day, and of course Mr. Fairbanks came and went as he chose.

Fessenden and Kitty found Miss Morton in the library, and, as they had decided beforehand, went straight to the root of the matter.

“Miss Morton,” Fessenden began, “I want to do a little more questioning on my own account, before Mr. Fleming Stone arrives. I’m sure you won’t object to helping me out a bit by answering a few queries.”

“Go ahead,” said Miss Morton grimly, but not unkindly.

===Chinese===

在范·诺曼宅邸，莫顿小姐俨然也做起女主人来，待客殷勤和气；她的古怪固然无可置疑，说话有时又嫌唐突，可她当家理事、调度仆人，倒极少有什么龃龉或失措。

她待汤姆·威拉德和斯凯勒·卡尔顿十分亲厚，后者常在用茶的钟点过来坐坐；费森登则一天到晚什么时辰都来，费尔班克斯先生更是来去自便。

费森登同基蒂在书房里找到了莫顿小姐，两人照着事先的商定，开门见山，直奔正题。

“莫顿小姐，”费森登开了口，“我想以私人身份再多盘问几句，赶在弗莱明·斯通（Fleming Stone）先生到来之前。请您费心回答几个问题，帮我一点忙，想来您不会不肯吧。”

“问吧，”莫顿小姐说，板着脸，倒并无恶意。

===Original===

“They are a bit personal,” went on Rob, who was at a loss how to begin, now that he was really told to do so.

“Well?”

This time, Miss Morton’s tone was more crisp, and Kitty began to see that Rob was on the wrong tack. So she took the helm herself, and said, with a winning smile:

“We want you to tell us frankly what was the paper you burned.”

Something in Miss Morton’s expression went to the girl’s heart, and she added impulsively:

===Chinese===

“这些问题有点涉及私事，”罗布接着说；如今人家真个叫他问了，他反倒不知从何问起。

“说吧。”

这一次，莫顿小姐的口气更脆了，基蒂开始看出罗布的话头走岔了路。于是她亲自掌起舵来，带着一副讨人喜欢的笑容说：

“我们想请您坦白告诉我们，您烧掉的那份纸究竟是什么。”

莫顿小姐神情里有一样东西触动了姑娘的心，她情不自禁地又添了一句：

===Original===

“I know it wasn’t anything that affects the case at all, and if you want to refuse us, you may.”

“I’d rather not tell you,” said Miss Morton, and a faraway look came into her strange eyes; “but since you have shown confidence in me, I prefer to return it.”

She took Kitty’s hand in hers, and from the gentle touch the girl was sure that whatever was the nature of the coming confidence, it was not that of a guilty conscience.

“As you know, Kitty,” she began, addressing the girl, though she glanced at Rob occasionally, “many years ago I was betrothed to Richard Van Norman. We foolishly allowed a trifling quarrel to separate us for life. I will not tell you the story of that now﻿—though I will, some time, if you care to hear it. But we were both quick-tempered, and the letters that passed between us at that time were full of hot, angry, unconsidered words. They were letters such as no human beings ought to have written to each other. Perhaps it was because of their exceeding bitterness, which we read and reread, that we never made up that quarrel, though neither of us ever loved anyone else, or ceased to love the other. At the death of Richard Van Norman, two years or more ago, I burned his letters which I had kept so long, and I wrote to Madeleine, asking her to return mine to me if they should be found among her uncle’s papers.”

===Chinese===

“我知道那纸同案子根本不相干，您要回绝我们，尽可以回绝。”

“我本不想告诉你们，”莫顿小姐说，她那双异样的眼睛里浮起一抹悠远的神色；“不过，你们既然对我推诚相见，我也愿意以诚相还。”

她把基蒂的手拉过去握在自己手里，从那一握的轻柔里，姑娘确信，无论她要吐露的这桩心事是什么性质，总不会出自一颗有愧的良心。

“你是知道的，基蒂，”她对姑娘开了口，说话间偶尔也瞟一瞟罗布，“许多年以前，我和理查德·范·诺曼订过婚。我们俩傻，竟听凭一场小小的口角把两人拆散了一辈子。那段经过，如今我不讲——往后你要是想听，我总有一日讲给你听。那时我们两人都性子急，彼此往来的书信，满纸都是火辣辣、气冲冲、不假思索写下的字句。那样的信，是谁也不该写给谁的。也许正因为信里那份入骨的刻毒——我们读了一遍又一遍——这场口角才终究没有弥合，虽然我们两人谁也不曾另爱旁人，谁也没有停止爱着对方。理查德·范·诺曼故去——那是两年多以前——我便把他那些我珍藏了这么久的信烧了，又写信给马德琳，请她若是能在她叔父的文件里找到我写的信，就把它们还给我。”

===Original===

“Dear Miss Morton,” said Kitty, “don’t tell any more if it pains you. We withdraw our request, don’t we, Rob?”

“Yes, indeed,” said Fessenden heartily; “forgive us, Miss Morton, for what is really an intrusion, and an unwarrantable one.”

“I want to tell you a little more,” Miss Morton resumed, “and afterward I’ll tell you why I’ve told it. Madeleine replied with a most kind letter, saying she had not found the letters, but should she ever do so, she would send them to me. About a year ago, she wrote and asked me to come here to see her. I came, thinking she had found those letters. She had not, but she had found her uncle’s diary, which disclosed his feelings toward me, both before and after our quarrel, and she told me then she intended to leave this place to me in her will, because she thought it ought to be mine. Truth to tell, I didn’t take much interest in this bequest, for I supposed the girl would long outlive me. But I had really no desire for the house without its master, and though I didn’t tell her so, I would rather have had the letters which I hoped she had found, than the news of her bequest.”

“Why did you want the letters so much, Miss Morton?” asked Kitty.

===Chinese===

“亲爱的莫顿小姐，”基蒂说，“要是说出来叫您难受，就别再讲了。我们把请求收回来，好不好，罗布？”

“正是，正是，”费森登恳切地说；“饶恕我们吧，莫顿小姐，这实在是打搅，而且是不该有的打搅。”

“我还想再多讲一点，”莫顿小姐接着说，“讲完之后，我再告诉你们我为什么要讲。马德琳回了一封信，写得极其厚道，说那些信没有找到，可一旦找到，一定给我送来。大约一年以前，她来信请我到这里来看她。我来了，只当她找到了那些信。信倒没有找到，她却找到了她叔父的日记，日记里披露了他对我的情意——口角之前也好，口角之后也好——她当时就告诉我，她打算在遗嘱里把这地方留给我，因为她觉得这地方本该归我。说实在话，我对这份遗赠没有多大兴致，我原以为这姑娘会比我多活好多年。况且没了主人的宅子，我实在无意于它；这话我没对她讲，可我宁愿得到那些信——我盼着她也找到了那些信——也不愿听到她遗赠宅子的消息。”

“您为什么那么想得到那些信呢，莫顿小姐？”基蒂问。

===Original===

“Because, my dear, they were a disgrace to me. They would be a disgrace to any woman alive. You, my child, with your gentle disposition, can’t understand what dreadful cruelty an angry woman can be guilty of on paper. Well, again Madeleine told me she would give me the letters if they ever appeared, and I went home. I didn’t hear from her again till shortly before her wedding, when she wrote me that the letters had been found in a secret drawer of Richard’s old desk. She invited me to come to her wedding, and said that she would then give me the letters. Of course I came, and that afternoon that I arrived she told me they were in her desk, and she would give them to me next morning. I was more than impatient for them﻿—I had waited forty years for them﻿—but I couldn’t trouble her on her wedding eve. And then﻿—when﻿—when she went away from us, without having given them into my possession, I was so afraid they would fall into other hands, that I went in search of them. I found them in her desk, I took them to my room and burned them without reading them. And that is the true story of the burned papers. I did look over a memorandum book, thinking it might tell where they were. But right after that I found the letters themselves in the next compartment, and I took them. They were mine.”

The dignified complacency with which Miss Morton uttered that last short sentence commanded the respect of her hearers.

“Indeed, they were yours, Miss Morton,” said Fessenden, “and I’m glad you secured them, before other eyes saw them.”

===Chinese===

“因为，亲爱的，那些信是我的耻辱。它们会是天下任何一个活着的女人的耻辱。你这孩子性情温婉，领会不到一个动怒的女人落在纸面上，能犯下多么可怕的残忍。唉，后来马德琳又对我说，那些信若有一日露面，一定交给我，我便回家去了。此后我再没有得到她的音信，直到她婚礼前不久，她来信说，那些信在理查德那张旧书桌的暗屉里找到了。她请我来参加婚礼，说届时便把信交给我。我自然来了；就在我到的那天下午，她告诉我信就在她的书桌里，第二天早晨给我。我盼信盼得心焦不止——我等了四十年了——可我不忍在婚礼前夜去搅扰她。接着——她——她竟撇下我们去了，信还不曾交到我手里，我怕极了它们会落到旁人手中，便自己动手去找。我在她的书桌里找到了，拿回自己房里，看也没看就烧了。这便是烧掉的那份纸的实情。我的确翻检过一本记事册，想着它也许写明信在何处。可紧接着，我就在相邻的一格里找到了信本身，我便拿了走。它们本来就是我的。”

莫顿小姐说出这短短一句收梢的话时，那副庄重自得的神气，叫听的人不由得肃然起敬。

“信的确是你的，莫顿小姐，”费森登说，“我很高兴你抢在旁人眼睛看到之前把它们取到了手。”

===Original===

Kitty said nothing, but held Miss Morton’s hand in a firm, gentle pressure that seemed to seal their friendship.

“But,” said Fessenden, a little diffidently, “why didn’t you tell all this at the inquest as frankly as you have told us?”

Miss Morton paled, and then grew red.

“I am an idiot about such things,” she said. “When questioned publicly, like that, I am so embarrassed and also so fearful that I scarcely know what I say. I try to hide this by a curt manner and a bravado of speech, with the result that I get desperate and say anything that comes into my head, whether it’s the truth or not. I not only told untruths, but I contradicted myself, when witnessing, but I couldn’t seem to help it. I lost control of my reasoning powers, and finally I felt my only safety was in denying it all. For﻿—and this was my greatest fear﻿—I thought they might suspect that I killed Madeleine, if they knew I *did* burn the papers. Afterward, I would have confessed that I had testified wrongly, but I couldn’t see how it would do any good.”

“No,” said Rob slowly, “except to exonerate Marie of falsehood.”

===Chinese===

基蒂什么也没说，只是把莫顿小姐的手紧紧一握，握得又稳又柔，像是要给两人的友情盖上一方印。

“可是，”费森登不无迟疑地说，“在验尸审讯上，您为什么不像对我们这样，把这一切坦白讲出来呢？”

莫顿小姐脸上先是一白，随即涨红了。

“这种事上我是个十足的傻子，”她说。“像那样当众受盘问，我又窘又怕，几乎不知道自己在说什么。我想拿生硬的态度和逞强的口吻把这一点遮掩过去，结果反倒弄得自己孤注一掷，脑子里冒出什么就说什么，也不管是真话假话。作证的时候，我不仅说了假话，还前后自相矛盾，可我似乎不由自主。我的脑子乱了方寸，到后来，我只觉得唯一的自保之道就是把一切全盘否认。因为——这才是我最怕的一层——我怕他们一旦知道我确实烧了那些纸，便会疑心是我杀了马德琳。事后我本打算招认自己的证词有误，可我看不出那样会有什么好处。”

“是啊，”罗布缓缓地说，“除非是为了证明玛丽没有说谎，还她一个清白。”

===Original===

Miss Morton set her lips together tightly, and seemed unwilling to pursue that subject.

“And now,” she said, “the reason I’ve told you two young people this, is because I want to warn you not to let a quarrel or a foolish misunderstanding of any sort come between you to spoil the happiness that I see is in store for you.”

“Good for you! Miss Morton!” cried Rob. “You’re a brick! You’ve precipitated matters a little; Kitty and I haven’t put it into words as yet, but﻿—we accept these preliminary congratulations﻿—don’t we, dear?”

And foolish little Kitty only smiled, and buried her face on Miss Morton’s shoulder instead of the young man’s!

===Chinese===

莫顿小姐紧紧抿住了嘴唇，似乎不愿再谈这个话头。

“还有，”她说，“我把这些讲给你们两个年轻人听，是因为我想告诫你们：千万别让一场口角、或任何一种愚蠢的误会横在你们中间，毁掉我看得出的、等着你们的那份幸福。”

“说得好！莫顿小姐！”罗布嚷道。“您真是个大好人！您这一来，把事情催得略微快了些；基蒂和我还没有把它说出口呢，不过——这份预贺我们领受了——是吧，亲爱的？”

而傻乎乎的小基蒂只是笑，把脸埋进了莫顿小姐的肩窝，而不是那位年轻人的肩头！

===Original===

And so, Miss Morton’s name was erased from Rob’s list of people to be inquired of, and, as he acknowledged to himself, he was quite ready now to turn over his share in the case to Fleming Stone.

And, too, since Miss Morton had given a gentle push to the rolling stone of his affair with Kitty, it rolled faster, and the two young people had their heart-to-heart talks with each other, instead of adding a third to the interview.

But there was just one more unfinished duty that Fessenden determined to attend to. Carleton had assured him that he was at liberty to talk to Dorothy Burt, if he chose, and Rob couldn’t help thinking that he ought to get all possible light on the case before Mr. Stone came; for he proposed to assist that gentleman greatly by his carefully tabulated statements, and his cross-referenced columns of evidence.

So, unaccompanied by Kitty, who was apt to prove a disturbing influence on his concentration of mind, he interviewed Miss Burt.

===Chinese===

这样，莫顿小姐的名字便从罗布那份待查问的名单上勾掉了；而且他自己也承认，他如今很乐意把自己经手的那份案子移交弗莱明·斯通。

再说，莫顿小姐既然轻轻推了一把那块滚石——他与基蒂的那桩情事——它便越滚越快；两位年轻人如今把推心置腹的谈话留给了彼此，不再在谈话里添上第三个人。

不过，还有一桩未了的差事，费森登决意要亲自办一办。卡尔顿向他担保过，他若愿意，随时可以去同多萝西·伯特谈谈；罗布忍不住想，在斯通先生到来之前，他应当把这桩案子可能得到的一切线索都搜集齐全；因为他打算拿自己那份条分缕析的制表陈述，还有互见参照的证据栏目，大大地助那位先生一臂之力。

于是，他不带基蒂同往——她那人在场，容易搅扰他凝神思索——独自去会了伯特小姐。

===Original===

It was not difficult to get an opportunity, as she rarely left the house, and Mrs. Carleton was not exigent in her demands on her companion’s time.

So the two strolled in the rose-garden late one afternoon, and Rob asked Miss Burt to tell him why she hesitated so when on the witness stand, and why she looked at Carleton with such unmistakable glances of inquiry, which he as certainly answered.

Dorothy Burt replied to the questions as frankly as they were put.

“To explain it to you, Mr. Fessenden,” she said, “I must first tell you that I loved Mr. Carleton even while Miss Van Norman was his affianced bride. I tell you this simply, both because it is the simple truth and because Mr. Carleton advised me to tell you, if you should ask me. And, knowing this, you may be surprised to learn that when I heard of Miss Van Norman’s death, I﻿—” she raised her wonderful eyes and looked straight at Rob﻿—“I thought she died by Schuyler’s hand. Yes, you may well look at me in surprise﻿—I know it was dreadful of me to think he *could* have done it, but﻿—I did think so. You see, I loved him﻿—and I *knew* he loved me. He had never told me so, had never breathed a word that was disloyal to Miss Van Norman﻿—and yet *I knew*. And that last evening in this very rose-garden, on the night before his wedding, we walked here together, and I knew from what he didn’t say, not from what he did say, that it was I whom he loved, and not she. He left me with a few cold, curt words that I knew only too well masked his real feelings, and I saw him no more that night. He *had* told me he was going over to Miss Van Norman’s, and so, when I heard of the﻿—the tragedy﻿—I couldn’t help thinking he had yielded to a sudden terrible impulse. Oh, I’m not defending myself for my wrong thought of him; I’m only confessing that I did think that.”

===Chinese===

机会并不难找，她很少出门，卡尔顿太太对女伴时间的索取也不算苛。

于是一天傍晚，两人在玫瑰园里信步，罗布请伯特小姐告诉他，她坐在证人席上时为什么那样迟疑，为什么拿那样分明是探询的目光望着卡尔顿，而他又同样分明地以目光作答。

问的人问得怎样直率，多萝西·伯特也就答得怎样直率。

“费森登先生，要向您解释明白，”她说，“我得先告诉您：早在范·诺曼小姐还是他名分已定的未婚妻时，我就爱着卡尔顿先生了。这话我直截了当地对您说，一来因为它本来就是简简单单的实话，二来卡尔顿先生也嘱咐过我，您若问起，就这样告诉您。知道了这一层，您也许要吃惊：我听说范·诺曼小姐死讯的时候，我——”她抬起那双美得惊人的眼睛，直直地望着罗布——“我还当她是死在斯凯勒手里的。是啊，您大可以惊讶地望着我——我知道，我竟想他干得出那种事来，实在罪过，可是——我确实这么想过。要知道，我爱他——我也知道他爱我。他从没对我说过，连一句有负范·诺曼小姐的话也不曾露过——可我就是知道。成婚前夜的那个傍晚，就在这座玫瑰园里，我们一同在这里散步；我从他没有说出的话里——而不是他说出的话里——知道了他爱的是我，不是她。他丢下三言两语冷冰冰的话便走了，那话把他的真情形遮得严严实实，这我心里清楚得很；那一夜我再没有见到他。他先前倒是对我说过，他要到范·诺曼小姐家去；所以，等我听说那桩——那桩惨案——我由不得想，他是一时受不住什么可怕之极的冲动。哦，我不是在为自己对他那份错误的臆想辩解；我只是招认，我确实那么想过。”

===Original===

“And how did you learn that you were mistaken,” said Rob gently, “and that Schuyler didn’t do it?”

“Why, the very next night he told me he loved me,” said the girl, her face alight with a tender glory, “and then I *knew*!”

“And your embarrassment at the questions on the witness stand?”

“Was only because I knew suspicion was directed toward him, and I feared I might say something to strengthen it, even while trying to do the opposite.”

“And you didn’t care whether you told the truth or not?”

===Chinese===

“那您后来是怎么知道您想错了，”罗布温声说道，“知道斯凯勒并不是凶手呢？”

“嗐，就在第二天晚上，他对我说他爱我，”姑娘说，满脸漾着一片温柔的荣光，“那时候我就知道啦！”

“那么您在证人席上答话时的局促不安呢？”

“只因为我明白怀疑的矛头正是指向他，我怕自己一不留神说出什么话来加重它，哪怕我正拼命想往相反的方向使劲。”

“那您就不管自己说的是不是真话了？”

===Original===

“If the truth would help to incriminate Schuyler, I would prefer not to tell it.”

The gentle sadness in Dorothy’s tone robbed this speech of the jarring note it would otherwise have held.

“You are right, Miss Burt,” said Rob, “and I thank you for the frank confidence you have shown in talking to me as freely as you have done.”

“Schuyler told me to,” said the girl simply.

===Chinese===

“倘若真话会有助于坐实斯凯勒的罪名，我宁可不说。”

多萝西话音里那一缕淡淡的哀伤，把这句话本来难免的刺耳意味化掉了。

“您没有错，伯特小姐，”罗布说，“您同我说得这样开诚布公，我谢谢您这份坦诚的信任。”

“是斯凯勒叫我说的，”姑娘简简单单地说。

## XXIII Fleming Stone / 二十三　弗莱明·斯通

===Original===

When Fessenden told Kitty of his interview with Dorothy Burt, she agreed that he had now followed every trail that had presented itself, or had been suggested by anybody.

Mr. Fairbanks, too, admitted that he was at his wits’ end, and saw no hope of a solution of the mystery except through the services of Fleming Stone. And so when the great detective arrived, both Fairbanks and Fessenden were ready to do anything they could to help him, but had no suggestions to make.

With her ever-ready hospitality, Miss Morton invited Mr. Stone to make his home at the Van Norman house, and, as this quite coincided with his own wishes, Stone took up his quarters there.

The first evening of his arrival he listened to the details of the case.

===Chinese===

费森登把自己同多萝西·伯特那次谈话的经过讲给基蒂听时，她也承认，凡是自己撞见过的、或是旁人提示过的踪迹，他如今都已一一追索过了。

费尔班克斯先生也自认已经一筹莫展，除了仰仗弗莱明·斯通的本事，看不出这桩疑案还有破解的希望。所以等这位大侦探一到，费尔班克斯和费森登两人都准备竭尽所能帮他一把，只是谁也提不出什么建议来。

莫顿小姐素来好客，当即请斯通先生住到范·诺曼宅邸里来；这正合他本人的心意，斯通便在宅中住了下来。

他到达的头一个晚上，便听人讲述了案情的种种细节。

===Original===

Fleming Stone was of a most attractive personality. He was nearly fifty years old, with graying hair and a kindly, responsive face.

At dinner he had won the admiration of all by his tact and interesting conversation. At the table the business upon which he had come had not been mentioned, but now the group assembled in the library felt that the time had come to talk of the matter.

It was a strangely-assorted household. Tom Willard, though the only relative of the Van Normans present, was in no way the head of the house. That position was held by Miss Morton, who, though kindhearted and hospitable, never let it be forgotten that she was owner and mistress of the mansion.

===Chinese===

弗莱明·斯通这个人，气质极有魅力。他年近五十，头发已然灰白，面容和善，神色最能随人心意而动。

席间，他以得体的举止和风趣的言谈博得了满座的钦佩。在餐桌上，他此来所为何事只字未提；这会儿众人聚在书房里，都觉得谈这桩案子的时刻到了。

这是个成分奇异的一家人。汤姆·威拉德虽是范·诺曼家在场唯一的亲眷，却全然算不得一家之主。这个位分属于莫顿小姐，她心地仁厚、待客殷勤，可也从不让人忘记，这座宅子的主人是她，当家作主的也是她。

===Original===

Kitty French was an honored guest, and as Miss Morton had invited her to stay as long as she would, she had determined now to stay through Mr. Stone’s sojourn there, after which, whatever the results of his work, she would go back to her home in New York.

Fessenden and Schuyler Carleton had been with them at dinner, and Mr. Benson and Mr. Fairbanks had come later, and now the group waited only on Mr. Stone’s pleasure to begin the recital of the case.

When Fleming Stone, then, asked Coroner Benson to give him the main facts, it seemed as if the great detective’s work was really about to begin.

===Chinese===

基蒂·弗伦奇是位备受礼敬的客人；莫顿小姐请她爱住多久便住多久，她便决意等斯通先生这一回住完了再走，到那时，且不管他查案的结果如何，她就回纽约自己家里去。

费森登和斯凯勒·卡尔顿同众人一道用了晚餐，本森先生和费尔班克斯先生来得晚些；此刻这一群人只等斯通先生示下，便要开始陈述案情。

于是，当弗莱明·斯通请验尸官本森先生把主要事实讲给他听时，这位大侦探的工作，看上去是真要开始了。

===Original===

“Would you rather see Mr. Benson alone?” asked Schuyler Carleton, actuated, doubtless, by his own shrinking from any publicity.

“Not at all,” said Stone briefly. “I prefer that you all should feel free to speak whenever you wish.”

Then Mr. Benson set forth in a concise way and in chronological order the facts as far as they were known, the suspicions that had been entertained and given up; and deplored the entire lack of clue or evidence that might lead to investigation in any definite direction.

===Chinese===

“你要不要同本森先生单独谈？”斯凯勒·卡尔顿问——他这一问，无疑是因为自己生性怕见张扬。

“完全不必，”斯通简截地说，“我倒情愿诸位想说什么就说什么，毫无拘束。”

于是本森先生把至今所知的事实，按时间先后，简明扼要地陈述了一遍，又讲了那些起过又撇下的种种怀疑；末了叹惜竟没有一条线索、一件证据，能把侦查引向任何一个明确的方向。

===Original===

The others, as Mr. Stone had suggested, made remarks when they chose, and the whole conversation was of an informal and colloquial nature. It seemed dominated by Fleming Stone’s mind. He drew opinions from one or another, until before they realized it everyone present had taken part in the recital. And to each Fleming Stone listened with deference and courtesy. The coroner’s legal phrases, Fessenden’s impetuous suggestions, Tom’s blunt remarks, Carleton’s half-timid utterances, Kitty’s volatile sallies, and even Miss Morton’s futile observations, all were listened to and responded to by Fleming Stone with an air of deep interest and consideration.

As the hour grew late Mr. Stone said that he felt thoroughly acquainted with the facts of the case so far as they could be told to him. He said he could express no opinion nor offer any suggestion that night, but that he hoped to come to some conclusions on the following day; and if they would all meet him in the same place the next evening, he would willingly disclose whatever he might have learned or discovered in the meantime. This put an end to the conversation, and Mr. Benson and Mr. Fairbanks went home. The ladies went to their rooms, and Carleton, Fessenden and Willard sat up for an hour’s smoke with Fleming Stone, who entertained them with talk on subjects far removed from murder or sudden death.

===Chinese===

其余的人依着斯通先生的意思，想到什么便插上一句，整场谈话全然非正式，尽是家常口吻。可主宰这一切的，似乎是弗莱明·斯通的头脑。他从这个人、那个人口里引出各自的见解，等众人觉察过来，在场的每一位都已经加入这场陈述了。而对每一个人，弗莱明·斯通都恭谨有礼地听着。验尸官的法律词令、费森登性急的提议、汤姆直率的议论、卡尔顿半带怯意的措辞、基蒂机变百出的打趣，乃至莫顿小姐那些全然无补于事的见解，弗莱明·斯通一概凝神听着，一一作答，神情专注而郑重。

夜渐深了，斯通先生说，凡能讲给他听的案情事实，他自信已经尽在于胸；今晚他不便表示意见，也提不出什么建议，但希望第二天能得出一些结论；倘若诸位明晚仍在原处同他相聚，他乐于把这中间查到或发现的一切和盘托出。谈话至此打住，本森先生和费尔班克斯先生回家去了。女眷们各自回房，卡尔顿、费森登和威拉德留下来，陪弗莱明·斯通抽了一个钟头的烟；他谈笑风生，讲的却尽是些同凶杀暴亡毫不相干的题目。

===Original===

The next morning Fleming Stone expressed a desire to be shown all the rooms in the house.

“In a case like this,” he said, “with no definite clues to follow, the only thing to do is to examine the premises in hope of happening upon something suggestive.”

Kitty was eager to be Mr. Stone’s guide, and easily obtained Miss Morton’s permission to go into all the rooms of the old mansion.

===Chinese===

第二天早晨，弗莱明·斯通表示，想把宅子里的房间统统看一遍。

“遇上这样的案子，”他说，“没有确凿的线索可循，唯一的办法就是把宅子细细察看一遍，盼着能碰上什么有启发的东西。”

基蒂急切地要给斯通先生当向导，轻易便得了莫顿小姐的允许，可以走遍老宅的所有房间。

===Original===

Fessenden went with them, and though the tour of the sleeping-rooms was quickly made, it was evident that the quick eye of the detective took in every detail that was visible. He stayed longer in Madeleine’s sitting-room, but, though he picked up a few papers from her desk and glanced at them, he showed no special interest in the room.

Downstairs they went then, and found Mr. Fairbanks in the library, awaiting them. He brought no news or fresh evidence, and had merely called in hope of seeing Mr. Stone.

The great detective was most frank and kindly toward his lesser colleague, and made him welcome with a genial courtesy.

===Chinese===

费森登也跟着同去。卧室那一圈看得很快，可侦探那双锐利的眼睛，显然把目之所及的每一处细节都收了进去。他在马德琳的起居室里待得久一些，虽也从她书桌上拿起几张纸张看了看，对这间屋子却没显出特别的兴致。

随后众人下了楼，见费尔班克斯先生已在书房里等着他们。他没带来什么消息或新的证据，不过是指望能见上斯通先生一面，才跑这一趟。

大侦探对这位小他一辈的同行极其坦率和气，亲切周到地款待了他。

===Original===

“I’m going to make a thorough examination of these lower rooms,” said Fleming Stone, “and I should be glad of the assistance of you two younger men. My eyes are not what they once were.”

Mr. Fairbanks and Rob well knew that this statement was merely an idle compliment to themselves; for the eyes of Fleming Stone had never yet missed a clue, however obscurely hidden.

But Kitty, ignorant of the principles of professional etiquette, really thought that Fleming Stone was depending on his two companions for assistance.

===Chinese===

“我打算把楼下这几个房间彻底查一遍，”弗莱明·斯通说，“有劳你们两位年轻人搭把手，我就高兴了。我这双眼睛，已不比当年了。”

费尔班克斯先生和罗布心里都明白，这话不过是对他们两位随口的恭维；因为弗莱明·斯通这双眼睛，还从来没有放过一条线索，哪怕藏得再隐晦。

可基蒂不懂行内的这些规矩，还真当弗莱明·斯通是靠着两位同伴帮忙呢。

===Original===

Tom Willard had gone out, and Miss Morton was looking after her all-important housekeeping, so the three men and Kitty French were alone in the library.

In his quick, quiet way Fleming Stone went rapidly round the room. He examined the window fixtures and curtains, the mantel and fireplace, the furniture and carpet, and came to a standstill by the library table. The dagger, which was kept in a drawer of the table, was shown to him, but though he examined it a moment, it seemed to have little interest for him.

“There’s not a clue in this room,” he said almost indignantly. “There probably were several the morning after the murder, but the thorough sweepings and dustings since have obliterated every trace.”

===Chinese===

汤姆·威拉德出门去了，莫顿小姐正忙着照管她那要紧的家务，书房里便只剩下三个男人，外加基蒂·弗伦奇。

弗莱明·斯通用他那一套敏捷而安静的路数，很快把房间绕了一圈。窗上的配件和窗帘、壁炉架和壁炉、家具和地毯，他都一一察看，末了在书房的桌子旁停住了脚。人家把存在桌子抽屉里的那把匕首拿给他看，他虽也端详了片刻，那东西似乎引不起他多大的兴趣。

“这屋里一条线索也没有，”他说得几乎带了火气，“凶案次日早晨，这里兴许还有好几条，可这之后那么彻底的清扫掸尘，早把每一丝痕迹都抹掉了。”

===Original===

Somewhat abruptly he went into the large hall. Here his proceedings in the library were duplicated. “Nothing at all,” he said; “but what could be expected in a room which is a general thoroughfare?”

Then he went into the drawing-room. The other three followed, feeling rather depressed at the hopeless outlook, and a little disappointed in the great detective.

Stone glanced around the large apartment.

===Chinese===

他没打招呼便蓦地走进宽大的大厅，把书房里那套察看在这儿又照样来了一遍。“什么也没有，”他说，“不过，一间人人打这里过往穿行的屋子，还能指望有什么呢？”

随后他走进客厅。另外三人跟在后面，眼见前景无望，心里颇为沮丧，对这位大侦探也有几分失望了。

斯通把这宽敞的房间环视了一周。

===Original===

“Swept, scrubbed, and polished,” he declared, as he glanced with disfavor at the immaculate room.

“And indeed it was quite necessary,” said Miss Morton, who entered just then. “After all those vines and flowers were taken away, and as a good deal of the furniture was out, I took occasion for a good bit of housecleaning.”

“Well,” said Fleming Stone quietly, “there’s one clue they didn’t sweep away. Here is where the assassin entered.”

===Chinese===

“扫了，刷了，还擦得锃亮，”他望着这一尘不染的房间，大不为然地下了断语。

“那也实在是不能不啊，”莫顿小姐恰在此时走了进来，“那些藤蔓花木统统搬走之后，又有好多家具挪了出去，我便趁势来了一场大扫除。”

“不过，”弗莱明·斯通平静地说，“有一条线索，他们可没扫掉。凶手就是从这里进来的。”

===Original===

As he spoke Mr. Stone was leaning against the mantel and looking down at the immaculately brushed hearth.

“Where?” cried Kitty, darting forward, and though the others gave no voice to their curiosity, they waited breathlessly for Stone’s next utterance.

The hearth and the whole fireplace were tiled, and in the floor tiling, under the andirons, was a rectangular iron plate with an oval opening closed by an iron cover. This cover was hinged, and could be raised and thrown back to permit ashes to be swept into the chute. The iron plate was sunk flush with the hearth and cemented into the brickwork, and the cover fitted into the rim so closely that scarce a seam showed.

===Chinese===

说话间，斯通先生正倚着壁炉架，低头望着那刷洗得纤尘不染的炉床。

“哪儿？”基蒂喊了一声，抢步上前；其余的人虽没把好奇心说出口，也都屏着气，等斯通的下一句话。

炉床连同整个壁炉都铺着瓷砖；铺地的瓷砖之间，柴架底下，嵌着一块长方形的铁板，铁板开着一个椭圆形的口子，上一面铁盖盖着。铁盖装了铰链，可以掀起来向后翻倒，好把炉灰扫进滑槽里去。铁板与炉床平齐，用水泥砌进砖石里，铁盖嵌进边框，严丝合缝，几乎看不出一丝接缝来。

===Original===

“He came up through this hole in the fireplace,” said Stone, almost as if talking to himself, “very soon after Miss Dupuy went upstairs at half-past ten. Before Mr. Carleton arrived at quarter after eleven, the murderer had finished his work, and had departed by this same means.”

While the others stood seemingly struck dumb by this revelation, Kitty excitedly flew to the fireplace and tried to raise the iron lid, but the andirons were in the way. Rob set them aside for her, while Stone said quietly, “Those andirons were probably not there that night?”

“No,” exclaimed Kitty; “they had been taken away, because we expected to fill the fireplace with flowers the next day.”

===Chinese===

“他是从壁炉这个洞里上来的，”斯通说，口气近乎自言自语，“十点半杜皮伊小姐上楼之后，工夫不大他就上来了。等到十一点一刻卡尔顿先生到的时候，凶手已经做完了他那桩事，又从原路走了。”

众人听着这个揭晓，一个个仿佛惊得说不出话；基蒂却兴冲冲扑到壁炉跟前，去掀那铁盖，偏偏柴架挡着路。罗布替她把柴架挪开，斯通那边静静地问：“那对柴架，那晚多半不在原处的吧？”

“不在，”基蒂嚷道，“早就挪开了，因为第二天我们要用鲜花把壁炉装满。”

===Original===

“But how could anybody get in the cellar?” asked Miss Morton, looking bewildered.

“The cellar is never carefully locked,” said Fleming Stone. “I came downstairs early this morning, and before breakfast Harris had shown me all through the cellar. He admits that several windows are always left open for the sake of ventilation, and claims that the carefully locked door in the hall at the head of the cellar stairs precludes all danger from that direction.”

“But I don’t understand,” said Mr. Fairbanks perplexedly. “If that opening is an ash-chute, such as I have in my own house, it is all bricked up down below, with the exception of a small opening for the removal of the ashes, and it would be quite impossible for anyone to climb up through it.”

===Chinese===

“可谁能下得去地窖呢？”莫顿小姐问，一脸茫然。

“地窖向来就没认真锁过，”弗莱明·斯通说，“今天一早我就下了楼，早饭前哈里斯已经领我把地窖走了一遍。他承认，为了通风，有几扇窗总那么敞着；却又说，地窖楼梯口大厅里那扇仔细锁着的门，把那个方向来的危险挡得一干二净。”

“可我不明白，”费尔班克斯先生大惑不解，“那口子若是个灰渣滑槽（ash-chute），同我家里那个一样，底下全用砖封死，只留个出灰的小口，任谁也没法从里头爬上来。”

===Original===

“But this one isn’t bricked up,” said Fleming Stone. “It was originally intended to be enclosed; but it seems this fireplace is rarely used. Harris tells me that the late Mr. Van Norman used to talk about having the chute completed, and having a fire here more often. But the library wood fire was more attractive as a family gathering place, and this formal room was used only on state occasions. However, as you see,” and Mr. Stone raised the iron lid again, “this opens directly into the cellar, and, I repeat, formed the means of entrance for the murderer of Madeleine Van Norman.”

Fleming Stone’s voice and manner were far from triumphant or jubilant at his discovery. He seemed rather to state the fact with regret, but as if it must be told.

Mr. Fairbanks looked amazed and thoughtful, but Rob Fessenden was frankly incredulous.

===Chinese===

“可这一个没有封砖，”弗莱明·斯通说，“原是打算封起来的；可看来这座壁炉难得生火。哈里斯告诉我，已故的范·诺曼先生从前常念叨，要把滑槽完工，也好在这儿多生几回火。可书房里的木柴火更招人喜欢，一家人爱聚在那儿，这间正经待客的屋子，只在有大典的场合才用一用。不过，诸位请看，”斯通先生说着又把铁盖掀了起来，“这口子直通地窖，我再说一遍，杀害马德琳·范·诺曼的凶手，就是从这里进来的。”

弗莱明·斯通发现了这一层，声气举止间却没有半点得意欢欣。他倒像是带着遗憾陈述这个事实，只是这事实不得不说罢了。

费尔班克斯先生一脸惊愕，若有所思；罗布·费森登却是明明白白的不信。

===Original===

“Mr. Stone,” he said respectfully, “I am sure you know what you’re talking about, but will you tell me how a man could get up through that hole? It doesn’t seem to me that a small-sized boy could squeeze through.”

Fleming Stone took a silver-cased tape-measure from his pocket, and handed it to Rob without a word.

Eagerly stooping on the hearth, Rob measured the oval opening in the iron plate. Although the rectangular plate was several inches larger each way, the oval opening measured exactly nine and one-half inches by thirteen and one-half inches.

===Chinese===

“斯通先生，”他恭恭敬敬地说，“我相信您说话自有道理，可您能不能告诉我，一个人怎么从那个洞里钻上来？依我看，就是个不大的孩子也未必挤得过去。”

弗莱明·斯通从口袋里掏出一把银壳卷尺，一言不发地递给罗布。

罗布在炉床边急切地俯下身去，量了量铁板上那个椭圆的口子。长方形的铁板纵横虽都大出几英寸，那椭圆的口子却不多不少，恰是九英寸半乘十三英寸半。

===Original===

“Who could get through that?” he inquired, as he announced the figures. “I’m sure I couldn’t.”

“And Schuyler Carleton is a larger man than you are,” observed Mr. Fairbanks.

“That lets Tom Willard out, too,” said Rob, with a slight smile; “for he’s nearly six feet tall, and weighs more than two hundred pounds.”

===Chinese===

“谁能钻得过这个？”他报出数字，问道，“反正我是万万不能。”

“斯凯勒·卡尔顿的块头可比你还大，”费尔班克斯先生说。

“这一来汤姆·威拉德也出局了，”罗布微微一笑，“他个头将近六英尺，体重超过两百磅。”

===Original===

“The only man I know of,” said Mr. Fairbanks thoughtfully, “who could come up through that hole is Slim Jim.”

“Who is Slim Jim?” cried Rob quickly. “Go for him; he is the man!”

“Not so fast,” said Mr. Fairbanks. “Slim Jim is a noted burglar and a suspected murderer, but he is safely in prison at present and has been for some months.”

===Chinese===

“据我所知，”费尔班克斯先生沉吟道，“唯一能从那个洞里上来的，只有瘦子吉姆（Slim Jim）。”

“瘦子吉姆是谁？”罗布急忙追问，“快去抓他，就是他了！”

“慢来慢来，”费尔班克斯先生说，“瘦子吉姆是个有名的飞贼，还背着一起杀人嫌疑，可他眼下安稳地待在牢里，已经好几个月了。”

===Original===

“But he may have escaped,” exclaimed Rob. “Are you sure he hasn’t?”

“I haven’t heard anything about him of late; but if he is or has been away from the prison, it can be easily found out.”

“Isn’t it unlikely,” said Fleming Stone quietly, “that a noted burglar should enter a house and commit murder, without making any attempt to steal?”

===Chinese===

“可他也许越狱了，”罗布嚷道，“你敢肯定他没有吗？”

“近来我没听着他的什么消息；不过他如今在不在牢里、或是曾经离开过没有，一查便知。”

“一位有名的飞贼，进了人家的宅子，杀了人，却丝毫不打算顺手偷点什么，”弗莱明·斯通静静地说，“这话可信吗？”

===Original===

“He may have been frightened away by the sound of Schuyler’s latchkey,” suggested Rob, and Kitty looked at him with pride in his ingenuity, and thought how much cleverer he was, after all, than the celebrated Fleming Stone.

Fessenden urged Mr. Fairbanks to go at once and look up the whereabouts of Slim Jim, and the detective was strongly inclined to go.

“Go, by all means, if you choose,” said Fleming Stone pleasantly. “There’s really nothing further to do here in the way of examination of the premises. I do not mind saying that my own suspicions are not directed toward Slim Jim, but my own suspicions are by no means an infallible guide. I will ask you, though, gentlemen, not to say anything about this ash-chute matter today. I consider it is my right to request this. Of course you can find out all about Slim Jim without stating how he entered the house.”

===Chinese===

“许是斯凯勒转动弹簧锁钥匙的声响把他吓跑了，”罗布揣测道。基蒂望着他，满心为他的机灵得意，心想他毕竟比那位大名鼎鼎的弗莱明·斯通高明多了。

费森登催费尔班克斯先生立刻去查瘦子吉姆的下落，那位侦探也极想去。

“二位请便，愿去就去，”弗莱明·斯通和颜悦色地说，“察看宅子的事，在这里其实再没有什么可做的了。我不妨直说，我自己心里的怀疑并不在瘦子吉姆身上；不过我个人的怀疑，也绝算不得万无一失的指引。只是二位先生，今天关于这灰渣滑槽的事，请一个字也不要往外说。这个请求，我认为是我的权利。当然，不必说明他是怎么进宅的，你们照样能把瘦子吉姆的底细查个一清二楚。”

===Original===

The two men promised not to say anything about the ash-chute to anybody, and hot upon the trail of the suspected burglar they went away.

Miss Morton excused herself, and upon Kitty French fell the burden of entertaining Mr. Stone. Nor was this young woman dismayed at the task.

Though not loquacious, the detective was an easy and pleasant talker, and he seemed quite ready to converse with the girl as if he had no other occupation on hand.

===Chinese===

两个人答应决不把灰渣滑槽的事对任何人吐露半个字，随即兴冲冲追着那飞贼嫌犯的踪迹去了。

莫顿小姐告退了，招待斯通先生的担子便落在基蒂·弗伦奇肩上。这姑娘对这份差事，倒也毫不发怵。

这位侦探虽不多话，谈起来却从容而悦人，而且他似乎极乐意同这姑娘闲谈，仿佛手头再没有旁的正事似的。

===Original===

“How wonderful you are!” said Kitty, clasping her hands beneath her chin as she looked at the great man. “To think of your spotting that fireplace thing right away! Though of course I never should have thought of anybody squeezing up through there. And Rob and I spent a whole morning searching these rooms for clues, and that was only the day after it happened.”

“What an opportunity!” Stone seemed interested. “And didn’t you find anything﻿—not *anything*?”

“No, not a thing. We were so disappointed. Oh, yes, Rob did find one little thing, but it was so little and so silly that I guess he forgot all about it.”

===Chinese===

“您可真了不得！”基蒂十指相扣托在下巴底下，仰望着这位大人物，“您一下子就瞧出了壁炉里那个名堂！当然啦，我是怎么也想不到，会有人从那儿挤上来的。出事第二天，我和罗布整整花了一个上午在这些屋里找线索呢。”

“多好的机会！”斯通似乎来了兴致，“那你们什么也没找到吗——竟什么都没找到？”

“是啊，什么也没有。我们失望极了。哦，对了，罗布倒真找着过一个小玩意儿，可那东西小得可笑，我想他早忘得一干二净了。”

===Original===

“What was it?”

“Why, I’ve almost forgotten the name. Oh, yes, Rob said it was a cachou﻿—a little silver thing, you know, like a tiny pill. Rob says some men eat them after they’ve been smoking. But he asked all the men that ever came here, and they all said they didn’t use them. Maybe the burglar dropped it.”

“Maybe he did. Where did you find it?”

===Chinese===

“是什么？”

“哎呀，名字我都快忘了。哦，对了，罗布说那叫香口珠——一个小小的银亮玩意儿，你知道的，就像一粒小药丸。罗布说，有些男人抽过烟就含一粒。可他把来过这儿的男人们问了个遍，人人都说自己不用那玩意儿。兴许是那飞贼掉的。”

“兴许正是他掉的。你们在哪儿找着的？”

===Original===

“Rob found it. It was right in that corner by the mantel, just near the fireplace.”

Fleming Stone stood up. “Miss French,” said he, “if it is any satisfaction to you, you may know that you have helped me a great deal in my work. Will you excuse me now, as I find I have important business elsewhere?”

Kitty smiled and bowed politely, but after Mr. Stone had left her she wondered what she could have said or done that helped him; and she wondered, too, what had caused that unspeakably sad look in his eyes as he went away.

===Chinese===

“是罗布找着的。就在壁炉架旁边那个角落里，紧挨着壁炉。”

弗莱明·斯通站起身来。“弗伦奇小姐，”他说，“倘若这能让您觉得欣慰，那么您不妨知道，您方才帮了我工作上一个天大的忙。现在我别处还有要紧的事，失陪了，可以吗？”

基蒂笑一笑，客客气气地欠了欠身。可斯通先生走后，她左思右想，不明白自己说了什么、做了什么，竟帮了他那么大的忙；她还想不明白，他离去的时候，眼睛里那一份说不出的哀伤，又是从何而来。

## XXIV A Confession / 二十四　自白

===Original===

Mr. Taylor, the landlord of the Mapleton Inn, showed a pleased surprise when Fleming Stone walked into his hotel and approached the desk. The men had never met, but everybody in Mapleton knew that Fleming Stone was in town, and had heard repeated and accurate descriptions of his appearance.

“Perhaps you can spare half an hour for a smoke and a chat,” said Stone affably, and though Mr. Taylor heartily agreed, he did not confess that he could easily have spared half a day or more had the great detective asked him.

In the landlord’s private office they sat down for a smoke, and soon the conversation, without effort, drifted around to the Van Norman affair.

Unlike detectives of fiction, Fleming Stone was by no means secretive or closemouthed. Indeed he was discursive, and Mr. Taylor marvelled that such a great man should indulge in such trivial gossip. They talked of old Richard Van Norman and the earlier days of the Van Norman family.

===Chinese===

弗莱明·斯通走进梅普尔顿旅馆，向柜台走来，掌柜泰勒先生脸上现出又惊又喜的神色。两人素未谋面，可梅普尔顿镇上无人不知道弗莱明·斯通来了，他那一副相貌，众人早已听人描摹过一遍又一遍，说得毫厘不爽。

“能不能劳驾赏半个钟头，抽支烟，聊聊闲天？”斯通和颜悦色地说。泰勒先生满口应承，满心欢喜，却没有招认：这位大侦探若肯开口，莫说半个钟头，就是半天他也轻易腾得出来。

两人在掌柜的私室里坐下抽烟，不多时，话头不费一点气力，便自然而然漂到了范·诺曼家的案子上。

小说里写的那种侦探，往往守口如瓶、讳莫如深，弗莱明·斯通却全然不是这样。他反倒海阔天空，无所不谈，泰勒先生暗自纳罕：这样一位大人物，竟有兴致说这些鸡毛蒜皮的闲话。两人谈起了老理查德·范·诺曼，谈起了范·诺曼家早年的旧事。

===Original===

“You’ve lived here a long time, then?” inquired Mr. Stone.

“Yes, sir. Boy and man, I’ve lived here nigh onto sixty years.”

“But this fine modern hotel of yours is not as old as that?”

The landlord’s face glowed with pride. “Right you are, sir. Some few years ago wife had some money left her, and we built the old place over﻿—pretty near made a whole new house of it.”

“You have many guests?”

===Chinese===

“这么说，您在这儿住了很久了？”斯通先生问道。

“是啊，先生。打小到大，我在这儿住了快六十年了。”

“可您这座气派的新式旅馆，总没有那般年纪吧？”

掌柜的脸上放光，满是自豪。“您说得一点不错，先生。前几年内人得了一份遗产，我们就把老房子翻盖了一遍——差不多盖成了一座全新的宅子。”

“客人多吗？”

===Original===

“Well, not as many as I’d like; but as many as I can expect in a little town like this. Mostly transients, of course; drummers and men of that sort. Young Willard stayed here, when the Van Norman house was full of company, but after the﻿—the trouble, he went back there to stay.”

“Affable sort of man, Willard, isn’t he?” observed Stone.

“Yes, he’s all of that, but he’s a scapegrace. He used to lead this town a dance when he lived here.”

“How long since he lived here?”

“Oh, he’s only been away a matter of three years, or that. ’Bout a year before his uncle died they quarrelled. They both had the devil’s own temper, and they had quarrelled before, but this time it was for keeps; and so off goes Mr. Tom, and never turns up again until he comes to Miss Madeleine’s wedding.”

===Chinese===

“唉，比不上我心盼的那么多；可在这样一个小镇上，也只好指望这些了。多半是过往的客，自然是些旅行推销员之流。威拉德家的少爷在这儿住过，那是范·诺曼宅邸住满客人的时候；后来出了那桩——那桩祸事，他就回那边住去了。”

“威拉德这人倒和气，是不是？”斯通说。

“和气是和气，可他是个浪荡子。他住在这儿那些年，把全镇搅得鸡犬不宁。”

“他离开这儿多久了？”

“哦，他走了也不过三年光景。他叔父去世前一年上下，爷儿俩吵翻了。两人都是顶了名的火爆性子，先前也吵过，可这一回是彻底闹掰了；汤姆先生这就走了，从此再没露过面，直到马德琳小姐办喜事才回来。”

===Original===

“Was he in any business when he lived here?”

“Yes, he had a good position as engineer in a big factory. He was a good worker, Tom was, and not afraid of anything. Always jolly and good-natured, except when he’d have one of them fearful fits of temper. Then he was like a raging lion﻿—no, more like a tiger; quiet-like, but deep and desperate.”

Soon after Fleming Stone rose to go. “Thank you very much,” he said politely, “for your half-hour. And, by the way, have you any cachous? I find I haven’t any with me, and after smoking, you know, before going back to the ladies﻿—”

“Yes, yes, I know; but I don’t happen to have any. But wait a minute, I believe Tripp has some.”

===Chinese===

“他住在这儿的时候，做过什么营生没有？”

“做过的，他在一家大工厂里当工程师，位置很不坏。汤姆干活是把好手，天不怕地不怕。平日总是乐呵呵、好性儿，只有犯起他那可怕的倔脾气来才另当别论。那时节他就像一头暴怒的狮子——不，更像一头老虎；不声不响，却深沉阴鸷，凶起来连命都不要。”

不多时，弗莱明·斯通便起身告辞。“多谢您了，”他客客气气地说，“谢谢您这半个钟头。对了，顺便问一句，您这儿有香口珠吗？我这儿一颗也没带；抽过烟，您知道的，回女眷那边之前——”

“懂，懂，我知道；不巧我手边没有。您稍等，我记得特里普那儿有。”

===Original===

He threw open the door and gave a quick whistle.

A boy appeared so suddenly that he could not have been far away, and, moreover, his sharp black eyes and alert manner betokened the type of boy who would be apt to be listening about.

His hand was already in his pocket when Mr. Taylor said to him, “Tripp, didn’t I see you have a small bottle of cachous?﻿—those little silver pellets, you know.”

“Yessir;” and Tripp drew forth a half-filled bottle.

“That’s right. Give them to the gentleman.”

===Chinese===

他一把推开房门，利落地打了个呼哨。

一个男孩应声出现，来得那么快，可见人本来就在左近；再说，他那双尖利的黑眼睛和那副机警相，一看便知是惯会在一旁偷听话头的那路孩子。

泰勒先生向他开口时，他的手已经伸进了口袋：“特里普，我记得你有一小瓶香口珠吧？——就是那种银亮亮的小珠儿。”

“是，先生。”特里普掏出一个半满的小瓶来。

“对，就是它。拿给这位先生。”

===Original===

“Oh, I only want a couple,” said Fleming Stone, taking the vial which Tripp thrust toward him. “Where did you get these, my boy?”

The boy blushed and looked down, twisting his fingers in embarrassment.

“Speak up, Tripp,” said the landlord sternly. “Answer the gentleman, and see that you tell the truth.”

“I ain’t going to tell no lie,” said Tripp doggedly. “I found this here bottle in the bureau-drawer of number fourteen a few days ago.”

“Fourteen? That’s the room Mr. Willard had,” said Mr. Taylor, reflectively.

===Chinese===

“哦，我只要两粒。”弗莱明·斯通接过特里普递过来的小瓶，说道，“这些东西你是从哪儿弄来的，孩子？”

男孩涨红了脸，垂下眼睛，手指扭来扭去，窘迫不堪。

“说呀，特里普，”掌柜的厉声说，“回答这位先生，可给我说实话。”

“我又不撒谎，”特里普犟头犟脑地说，“这瓶子是我前几天在十四号房间的五斗柜抽屉里捡着的。”

“十四号？那不是威拉德先生住的屋子吗。”泰勒先生若有所思地说。

===Original===

“Yessir, but *he* didn’t leave them there. They were there before. I seen ’em, and I knew that hatchet-faced hardware man left ’em; then Mr. Willard, he come, but he didn’t swipe ’em, so I did. That ain’t no harm, is it?”

“Not a bit,” said Fleming Stone, “since you’ve told the truth about it, and here’s a dollar for your honesty. And I’m going to ask you not to say anything more about the matter, for a few days at least. Also I’m going to ask to be allowed to take a look at room number fourteen.”

“Certainly, sir. Tripp, show the gentleman up,” and Mr. Taylor fairly rubbed his hands with satisfaction to think that he and his premises were being made use of by the great detective.

“Yessir. It’s at the back of the house, sir. This way, sir.”

===Chinese===

“是，先生，可那不是他留下的，早就搁在那儿了。我亲眼见过，我知道是那个长着斧劈脸的五金商人落下的；后来威拉德先生来了，他没顺手拿走，就归了我。这不算什么过错，对吧？”

“半点过错也没有，”弗莱明·斯通说，“你说了实话，这儿有一块美元，就赏你这份诚实。另外我要请你，这件事一个字也别再往外说，至少这几天之内。还有，我要请你们容我上去看一看十四号房间。”

“行，先生。特里普，领这位先生上去。”泰勒先生喜得直搓手：这位大侦探竟用上了他，用上了他的房子。

“是，先生。屋子在房子后头，先生。这边走，先生。”

===Original===

Mr. Stone’s survey of the room was exceedingly brief. He gave one glance around, looked out of the only window it contained, tried the key in the lock, and then expressed himself satisfied.

Tripp, disappointed at the quickly-finished performance, elaborately pointed out the exact spot where he had found the cachou bottle, but Mr. Stone did not seem greatly interested.

However, the interview was financially successful to Tripp, and after Mr. Stone’s departure he turned several handsprings by way of expressing his satisfaction with the detective gentleman.

===Chinese===

斯通先生查看那间屋子，快得出奇。他朝四下扫了一眼，望了望屋里仅有那扇窗的窗外，拿钥匙在锁里试了试，随即表示满意。

特里普见这场差事草草收场，好不失望，便煞有介事地指明他捡着香口珠瓶子的确切位置，可斯通先生似乎兴致不高。

不过这一遭见面对特里普的钱袋大有斩获；斯通先生走后，他一连翻了几个跟斗，算是对这位侦探先生表示满意。

---

===Original===

After dinner that evening the group of the night before reassembled in the library.

A strange feeling of oppression seemed to hang over all. The very fact that Fleming Stone had as yet said nothing of any discoveries he might have made, and the continued courtesy of his pleasant, affable demeanor, seemed to imply that he had succeeded rather than failed in his mission.

Although genial and quickly responsive, he was, after all, an inscrutable man; and Mr. Fairbanks, for one, had learned that his gentle cordiality often hid deep thoughts in a quickly-working mind.

Without preamble, as soon as they were seated Mr. Stone began:

===Chinese===

当天晚饭后，前一晚的那群人重新在书房里聚齐。

一种异样的沉郁仿佛压在每个人心头。弗莱明·斯通至今对他可能有了什么发现只字不提，而他那愉悦和气的风度又始终如一——单凭这一层，似乎就在暗示：他此行所负的使命，是成功，而不是失败。

他虽然和蔼，反应也快，骨子里终究是个高深莫测的人；费尔班克斯先生便是深知底细的人之一：他那份温文热络的背后，往往藏着一副头脑的深沉心思，转得飞快。

众人刚刚落座，斯通先生也不作任何开场白，开口便道：

===Original===

“Employed by Coroner Benson, I was asked to come here to discover, if might be, the murderer of Miss Madeleine Van Norman. By some unmistakable evidence which I have found, by some reliable witnesses with whom I have talked, and by some proofs which I have discovered, I have learned beyond all doubt who is the criminal, and how the deed was done. Is it the wish of all present that I should now make known what I have discovered, or is it preferred that I should tell Coroner Benson alone?”

For several minutes nobody spoke, and then the coroner said, “Unless anyone present states an objection, you may proceed to tell us what you know, here and now, Mr. Stone.”

After waiting a moment longer and hearing no objection raised, Fleming Stone proceeded.

===Chinese===

“我受验尸官本森先生之聘来到此地，为的是查明——倘若是可能的话——马德琳·范·诺曼小姐的凶手究竟是谁。凭我找到的若干确凿证据，凭我交谈过的若干可靠证人，凭我发现的若干实证，凶手是谁、罪行是怎样犯下的，我已了然于胸，绝无半点疑义。是在场的诸位都愿意让我此刻就公布我的发现呢，还是宁可我只讲给验尸官本森先生一个人听？”

好几分钟没有人作声。末了，验尸官说道：“倘若在座无人表示反对，斯通先生，您就请在此地、在此刻，把您所知道的讲给我们听吧。”

弗莱明·斯通又等了片刻，见无人提出异议，便继续说了下去。

===Original===

“The man who murdered Miss Van Norman entered the house through a cellar window. He climbed up through the ash-chute in the drawing-room fireplace.”

Although some of Mr. Stone’s hearers had listened to this revelation in the morning, the others had not heard of it, and every face expressed utter astonishment, if not unbelief﻿—with the exception of one. Tom Willard turned white and stared at Fleming Stone as if he had not understood.

“What?” he said hoarsely.

As if he had not heard the interruption, Fleming Stone went on:

“Who that man was, I think I need not tell you. Is he not already telling you himself?”

===Chinese===

“杀害范·诺曼小姐的凶手，是从地窖的一扇窗子进的宅，又从客厅壁炉的灰渣滑槽里爬上来的。”

斯通先生这番披露，在场的几个人早晨虽已听过，其余的人却是头一回听闻；一张张脸上都写满大出所料的惊愕，纵然还谈不上不信——只有一个人例外。汤姆·威拉德脸色刷地白了，直勾勾地瞪着弗莱明·斯通，仿佛压根儿没有听懂。

“什么？”他哑着嗓子说。

弗莱明·斯通仿佛没有听见这句插话，接着说了下去：

“那个人是谁，我想不必由我来说了。他不是已经在亲口告诉诸位了么？”

===Original===

Willard’s face grew drawn and stiff, like that of a paralyzed man, but his burning eyes seemed unable to tear themselves away from the quiet gaze of Fleming Stone. Then with a groan Willard’s head sank into his hands and he fell forward on the table﻿—the very table at which Madeleine had sat on that fatal night.

There was a stir, and Schuyler Carleton rushed forward to Willard’s assistance if need be. But the man had not fainted, and, raising his white face, he squared his shoulders, clenched his hands, and, again fixing his eyes on those of Fleming Stone, said in a desperate voice, “Go on.”

“I must go on,” said Stone, gently. “I know each one of you is thinking that it is absurd to imagine a man of Mr. Willard’s weight and girth climbing up through the seemingly small opening in the fireplace. But this can be explained. To one who does not know how, such a feat would seem impossible, and, moreover, it would be impossible. It is only one who knows how who can do it. There are men in certain occupations, such as engineers and boiler men, who are continually obliged to squeeze through holes quite as small. The regular boiler manhole is oval, and measures ten by fifteen inches, but there are many of them in large tanks which measure even less each way. I had occasion some time ago to interview an engineer on this subject. He weighed two hundred and fifteen pounds, and had a chest measure of forty-two inches. He told me that he could go through a much smaller manhole than another workman who weighed only one hundred and sixty pounds, simply because he knew how. It is done by certain manipulations of the great muscles and by following a certain routine of procedure. But the method is unimportant, for the moment. The fact remains, and can be verified by any engineer. I discovered today that Mr. Willard is or has been an expert engineer, and for many years held such a position in a large factory right here in Mapleton. As to Mr. Willard’s presence in this house upon that fatal night, a tiny clue discovered by Mr. Fessenden gives us indubitable proof. Mr. Fessenden found next morning on the drawing-room floor a cachou. I have learned that these are by no means in common use in Mapleton, and, moreover, that it is not the custom of any one of the men now present to use them. I further learned that after Mr. Willard left here that night to go to the hotel he found by chance a small bottle of these in the room which was assigned to him. I am assuming that he carelessly put a few in his pocket, and that in his struggle through the ash-chute one fell upon the carpet. The room which Mr. Willard occupied at Mapleton Inn is in the second story, and its window opens upon a veranda roof which has a gentle slope almost to the ground. This provides an easy means of exit and entrance, and as Mr. Willard has no alibi later than half-past ten on that evening, the time would permit him to come here and go away again before the hour when Mr. Carleton is known to have arrived.”

Then turning and meeting Tom’s intent gaze, Fleming Stone addressed himself directly to him, and said, “Why you chose to kill your cousin, I don’t know; but you *did*.”

===Chinese===

威拉德的脸抽紧了，僵住了，像瘫痪病人的脸一样；可他那双灼人的眼睛，似乎怎么也挣不脱弗莱明·斯通那份平静的注视。随后他低哼一声，头埋进两只手里，整个人朝桌上伏了下去——正是那个致命之夜马德琳坐过的桌子。

满屋一阵骚动，斯凯勒·卡尔顿抢步上前，预备必要时搭一把手。可这个人并没有昏过去；他抬起惨白的脸，挺起肩膀，攥紧两拳，又把目光钉在弗莱明·斯通的眼睛上，用一种破釜沉舟的声音说：“讲下去。”

“我不能不讲下去，”斯通温和地说，“我知道，诸位每一位心里都在想：威拉德先生那样的体重和腰身，要钻过壁炉里那个看上去很小的口子，简直是荒唐。可这是能够解释的。不懂门道的人，自然觉得这等事断无可能——事实上，对他也确实不可能。唯有懂得门道的人才做得来。有些行当里的人，比如工程师和司炉工，就常常不得不从同样小的洞口里挤过去。标准的锅炉人孔是椭圆的，尺寸十英寸乘十五英寸；大储罐上有许多人孔，纵横尺寸还要更小。前些时候，我曾就这个问题访问过一位工程师。他体重二百一十五磅，胸围四十二英寸。他告诉我，比起另一个体重只有一百六十磅的工人，他反倒能钻过小得多的人孔，缘故无他，只在于他懂得方法。这靠的是对大肌群的一套运使，外加一套固定的操作次序。不过眼下，方法并不打紧。事实摆在那里，任何一位工程师都能为之作证。我今天查明了，威拉德先生如今是、或者曾经是一名熟练的工程师，多年以来就在梅普尔顿本地一家大工厂里担任这个职位。至于那个致命之夜威拉德先生为何在这座宅子里，费森登先生发现的一条小小的线索，给了我们确凿无疑的证明。费森登先生第二天早晨在客厅的地板上捡到一粒香口珠。我查明，这种东西在梅普尔顿绝非人人常用；而且，眼下在场的诸位先生，没有一位有含用它的习惯。我还查明，那天夜里威拉德先生离开这里回旅馆之后，偶然在他住的那间屋里发现一小瓶这种东西。我推想，他随手抓了几粒放进口袋，从灰渣滑槽里奋力挤上来的时候，有一粒落在了地毯上。威拉德先生在梅普尔顿旅馆住的屋子在二楼，窗子开在游廊顶上，那廊顶坡度平缓，几乎斜到地面。这便给他提供了一条进出两便的现成门路；况且那天晚上十点半以后，威拉德先生拿不出不在场的证明，时间上尽容得他到这里来一趟再折回去，赶在人所共知的卡尔顿先生到达的时刻之前。”

说着，他转过身来，正迎上汤姆凝望的目光。弗莱明·斯通径直对他开口了：“你为什么下决心杀死你的表妹，我不知道；可人确实是你杀的。”

===Original===

“I did,” said Tom, in a hollow voice, “and I will tell you why.” He rose as he spoke, and standing by the table, he steadied himself by placing one hand upon it.

“It was entirely unpremeditated,” he said, “and I’m going to tell you about it, because I owe a confession to Madeleine’s memory, though I am responsible for my deed to no one here present.”

Though Willard spoke with no attempt at pride or defiance, his tone and look were those of a man hopeless and utterly crushed. He addressed himself principally to Fleming Stone, looking now and then at Carleton, but not so much as glancing at anyone else.

“It is no secret, I think, that I loved my cousin Madeleine. Many, many times I have pleaded with her to marry me. But never mind about that. When I came here to attend her wedding, I couldn’t help seeing that the man she was about to marry did not love and worship her as I did. I besought her to give him up and to marry me, but she would not listen to that for a moment. That day before the wedding they had a little tiff, and Carleton did not return for dinner, though Madeleine expected him. She was all broken up about this, and was not herself during the evening. When I left her, at about ten o’clock, to go to the hotel, her sad face haunted me, and I could not dispel the idea that I must have one more talk with her, and beg her not to marry a man who did not love her.”

===Chinese===

“是我杀的，”汤姆说，声音空洞，“我也要告诉你为什么。”他一边说一边站起身来，站在桌旁，一只手撑在桌上，稳住了自己。

“这完全不是预谋，”他说，“我要把事情原原本本讲出来，因为我在马德琳的遗念面前欠下一份自白，尽管我这桩行为，在座诸位谁也无权来追究。”

威拉德说这番话，既不逞强，也不挑衅，可他的语气和神情，属于一个万念俱灰、被彻底压垮的人。他的话主要是对弗莱明·斯通说的，偶尔望一望卡尔顿；旁的人，他连看也不曾看一眼。

“我爱我的表妹马德琳，这想必算不得什么秘密。我求过她多少回，求她嫁给我。不过这些且不去说它。我回来参加她的婚礼，不由自主地看在眼里：她要嫁的那个男人，并不像我这样爱她、把她奉若神明。我恳求她甩了他，嫁给我，可她连听也不肯听一下。婚礼前一天，他们俩小小地拌了几句嘴，卡尔顿没有回来吃晚饭，虽然马德琳一直等着他。她为这个伤心透了，一晚上都失魂落魄。十点钟光景，我辞了她回旅馆去，她那张愁苦的脸却一直缠着我不放；我怎么也甩不开一个念头：我必须再同她谈一次，求她别嫁一个不爱她的男人。”

===Original===

Without seeming to do so, Fleming Stone stole a glance at Carleton. The man sat quietly, with bowed head, as one who hears himself denounced, but recognizes the truth.

“I was in my room at the hotel,” went on Tom, “and was preparing for bed when the irresistible impulse came to me to go and see Maddy once more before her wedding day. I had no thought of wrongdoing. I came out through the window, instead of in the ordinary way, only because I knew the inn was about to be closed for the night, and I knew I could get back the same way. A trellis, that was simply a ladder, reached up to the low roof, and it was quite as easy an exit as through the front door. As to the cachous, I *had* found the stray vial there, and had slipped a couple in my pocket, without really thinking anything about it. I don’t usually carry them, but they are by no means unfamiliar to me. I came directly over here, and found the house partially darkened, as if for the night. There was a low light in the library and hall but the blinds were drawn, and I could see only a glimpse of Maddy’s yellow dress on the floor. I was about to ring the bell, when I suddenly thought that I didn’t care to rouse the household, or even the servants, and, remembering the way I often used to get in when I came home at night later than my uncle approved, I went around and entered by a cellar window. I came up through the fireplace, exactly as Mr. Stone has described to you. It is astonishingly easy to anyone who knows how, and quite impossible for one who does not. I crossed the drawing-room at once, and entered the library. Naturally, I made very little noise, but still I am surprised that Hunt did not hear me. I did not try to be entirely silent, for I had no thought of evil in my heart. Madeleine looked up as I came into this room, and smiled. She asked me how I got in, and I told her, and we both laughed at some old reminiscences. I did not see that paper that Miss Dupuy wrote. Then I told her frankly that I wanted her to give up Carleton, for he did not love her and I did. When I said that about Carleton, Maddy burst into weeping, and said it wasn’t true. I said it was, and offered to prove it, and then we quarrelled. To you who do not know our family temper this may sound trivial, but it was not. We had a most intense and fiery quarrel, and though probably our voices were not raised﻿—that was not our way﻿—we were so furious with each other that we were practically beside ourselves. Maddened, too, by jealousy, and by being baffled in my errand, I suddenly resolved to kill both my cousin and myself. I picked up the dagger and told her what I was about to do, being fully determined to stab her and then myself. She did not scream, she simply sat there﻿—in her superb beauty﻿—her arm resting on the table, and said quietly, ‘You dare not do it!’

“This threw me into a frenzy, and with one thrust I drove the dagger home to her heart. She died without a sound, and I pulled out the dagger to turn it upon myself.
